「这不是有点不讲道理吗？」他问，「你几乎还没怎么看呢。」

玛吉对他的这种情绪了如指掌。他总是把它留到自己下决心要交锋的时候。任何说理对它都无济于事。

「好孩子，」她费力地说，「对不起。也许是不讲道理。可那一类东西在我看来确实太可憎了。就是这样。……我不是来谈这个的。……告诉我——」

「你不是吗？」劳里说。

玛吉默然。

「你不是吗？」

「嗯——是来的。可我不想再谈了。」

===Original===
Laurie smiled so that it might be seen.

"Well, what else did you want to say?" (He glanced purposely at the book. Maggie ignored his glance.)

"I just came to see how you were getting on."

"How do you mean? With the book?"

"No; in every way."

He looked up at her swiftly and suddenly, and she saw that his agony of sorrow was acute beneath all his attempts at superiority, his courteous fractiousness, and his set face. She was filled suddenly with an enormous pity.

"Oh! Laurie, I'm so sorry," she cried out. "Can't I do anything?"

"Nothing, thanks; nothing at all," he said quietly.

===Chinese===
劳里笑了一下，有意叫人看见。

「好吧，你还想说什么？」（他故意瞟了一眼那本书。玛吉装作没看见。）

「我只是来看看你近来怎么样。」

「怎么样？是指看那本书？」

「不；是各方面。」

他猛地一抬眼，飞快地望了她一下，她这才看出来，在他那一副副超然的姿态、那种彬彬有礼的乖戾、那一张绷紧的脸之下，他的悲痛原是何等剧烈。一阵强烈的怜悯顿时充满她心间。

「哦！劳里，我真难过，」她叫道，「我不能做点什么吗？」

「什么也不用，谢谢；什么也不用，」他平静地说。

===Original===
Again pity and misery surged up within her, and she cast all prudence to the winds. She had not realized how fond she was of this boy till she saw once more that look in his eyes.

"Oh! Laurie, you know I didn't like it; but—but I don't know what to do, I'm so sorry. But don't spoil it all," she said wildly, hardly knowing what she feared.

"I beg your pardon?"

"You know what I mean. Don't spoil it, by—by fancying things."

"Maggie," said the boy quietly, "you must let me alone. You can't help."

"Can't I?"

"You can't help," he repeated. "I must go my own way. Please don't say any more. I can't stand it."

There followed a dead silence. Then Maggie recovered and stood up. He rose with her.

"Forgive me, Laurie, won't you? I must say this. You'll remember I'll always do anything I can, won't you?"

Then she was gone.

===Chinese===
怜悯与痛苦又一次在她心里翻涌上来，她把一切谨慎都抛到了九霄云外。直到此刻再次看见他眼里的那种神情，她才明白自己是多么疼爱这个少年。

「哦！劳里，你知道我本来不喜欢这套；可是——可是我不知道该怎么办，我心里真难过。只是别把一切都毁了，」她慌乱地说，自己几乎也不知道在怕什么。

「你说什么？」

「你明白我的意思。别——别胡思乱想，把一切都毁了。」

「玛吉，」少年平静地说，「你别管我。你帮不上。」

「帮不上吗？」

「你帮不上，」他又说了一遍。「我得走我自己的路。请别再说了。我受不了。」

随后是一片死寂。然后玛吉定下神来，站起身。他也随着她站起来。

「原谅我，劳里，好吗？我非说这一句不可：你肯记住——我永远愿意为你尽我所能——你肯记住吗？」

然后她便走了。

### IV / IV

===Original===
The ladies went to bed early at Stantons. At ten o'clock precisely a clinking of bedroom candlesticks was heard in the hall, followed by the sound of locking doors. This was the signal. Mrs. Baxter laid aside her embroidery with the punctuality of a religious at the sound of a bell, and said two words—

"My dears."

There were occasionally exclamatory expostulations from the two at the piquet-table, but in nine cases out of ten the game had been designed with an eye upon the clock, and hardly any delay followed. Mrs. Baxter kissed her son, and passed her arm through Maggie's. Laurie followed; gave them candles, and generally took one himself.

But this evening there was no piquet. Laurie had stayed later than usual in the dining room, and had wandered rather restlessly about when he had joined the others. He looked at a London evening paper for a little, paced about, vanished again, and only returned as the ladies were making ready to depart. Then he gave them their candlesticks, and himself came back to the drawing room.

===Chinese===
斯坦顿家的太太小姐们睡得早。一到十点整，门厅里便听得见卧室烛台碰响的声音，接着便是关门的声响。这就是号令。巴克斯特太太像修士听见钟声一般准时地搁下绣活，说了两个字——

「该睡了。」

两个在皮克牌（piquet）桌前的人偶尔会发出一两声惊叹似的抗议，但十回有九回，那盘牌本来就是看着钟点安排的，所以几乎没有什么拖延。巴克斯特太太吻过儿子，把胳膊伸进玛吉的臂弯。劳里跟在后头，把烛台递给她们，自己一般也拿一支。

可这天傍晚没有打皮克牌。劳里在餐厅里耽搁得比往常久，等到出来与众人会合之后，又颇为焦躁地走来走去。他看了一会儿伦敦的晚报，踱了几步，又不见了，直到太太小姐们准备散去时才回来。于是他把烛台递给她们，自己又回到客厅里。

===Original===
He was, in fact, in a far more perturbed and excited mood than even Maggie had had any idea of. She had interrupted him halfway through the book, but he had read again steadily until five minutes before dinner, and had, indeed, gone back again to finish it afterwards. He had now finished it; and he wanted to think.

It had had a surprising effect on him, coming as it did upon a state of mind intensely stirred to its depths by his sorrow. Crossness, as I have said, had been the natural psychological result of his emotions; but his emotions were none the less real. The froth of whipped cream is real cream, after all.

Now Laurie had seen perfectly well the extreme unconvincingness of Mrs. Stapleton, and had been genuine enough in his little shrug of disapproval in answer to Maggie's, after lunch; yet that lady's remarks had been sufficient just to ignite the train of thought. This train had smoldered in the afternoon, had been fanned ever so slightly by two breezes—the sense of Maggie's superiority and the faint rebellious reaction which had come upon him with regard to his personal religion. Certainly he had had Mass said for Amy this morning; but it had been by almost a superstitious rather than a religious instinct. He was, in fact, in that state of religious unreality which occasionally comes upon converts within a year or two of the change of their faith. The impetus of old association is absent, and the force of novelty has died.

===Chinese===
事实上，他此刻的心境，比玛吉所能想象的还要纷乱和亢奋得多。她曾打断他看书，看到一半；可他后来又稳稳地读到饭前五分钟，吃完饭又回去把它看完了。如今他已把它看完；而他需要想一想。

这本书对他的影响惊人，因为它是落在他那已被悲伤搅动到最深处的精神状态之上。如前所述，暴躁乃是他种种情绪自然的心理结果；然而那些情绪却是千真万确的。搅打过的奶油上面的泡沫，到底也是真正的奶油。

劳里是看得清清楚楚斯台普顿太太那副毫无说服力的样子的，午饭之后他附和着玛吉而耸一耸肩、表示不以为然，也是出于真心；然而那位太太的话，毕竟足以点燃那条思路。这条思路在下午一直闷烧着，又被两阵微风轻轻地扇了扇——一阵是玛吉那种高高在上的姿态，另一阵是他对自己的宗教产生的一种淡淡的叛逆反应。不错，他今天早上确实是为艾米求了一台弥撒；可那几乎是出于一种迷信而非宗教的本能。事实上，他正处在一种宗教上不真实的状态之中——这种状态有时会降临到那些改宗之后一两年内的信徒身上。旧日联系的冲力已经消失，而新鲜感的势头也已死去了。

===Original===
Underneath all this then, it must be remembered that the one thing that was intensely real to him was his sense of loss of the one soul in whom his own had been wrapped up. Even this afternoon as yesterday, even this morning as he lay awake, he had been conscious of an irresistible impulse to demand some sign, to catch some glimpse of that which was now denied to him.

It was in this mood that he had read the book; and it is not to be wondered at that he had been excited by it.

For it opened up to him, beneath all its sham mysticism, its intolerable affectations, its grotesque parody of spirituality—of all of which he was largely aware—a glimmering avenue of a faintly possible hope of which he had never dreamed—a hope, at least, of that half self-deception which is so tempting to certain characters.

Here, in this book, written by a living man, whose name and address were given, were stories so startling, and theories so apparently consonant with themselves and with other partly known facts—stories and theories, too, which met so precisely his own overmastering desire, that it is little wonder that he was affected by them.

===Chinese===
那么，在这一切之下，必须记住，对他而言唯一千真万确之事，就是他失去了那个他自己的灵魂曾全然寄托于她的灵魂——这种失落之感。无论今天下午还是昨天，也无论今天早上他躺在床上醒着的时候，他都意识到一种不可遏制的冲动：要讨一个征兆，要窥见一眼如今已被剥夺给他的那一切。

他正是在这种心境里读了那本书；那么它使他兴奋起来，也就不足为奇了。

因为这本书，在它那套虚假的神秘主义、它那叫人无法忍受的矫揉造作、它对灵性的滑稽模仿——这一切他多半都看得明白——之下，向他敞开了一条隐约的小径，通向一种他做梦也没想到过的、似有可能的微弱希望——或者至少，是那种半自欺的希望，这种希望对某些性格的人是莫大的诱惑。

在这里，这本由一个在世之人所写、署着姓名住址的书中，有着那么些骇人的故事，又有着那么些表面上自圆其说、又与其他半知半解的事实相吻合的理论——而这些故事与理论又恰恰那么精准地迎合了他自己压倒一切的渴望——他被它们所动，也就不足为怪了。

===Original===
Naturally, even during his reading, a thousand answers and adverse comments had sprung to his mind—suggestions of fraud, of lying, of hallucination—but yet, here the possibility remained. Here were living men and women who, with the usual complement of senses and reason, declared categorically and in detail, that on this and that date, in this place and the other, after having taken all possible precautions against fraud, they had received messages from the dead—messages of which the purport was understood by none but themselves—that they had seen with their eyes, in sufficient light, the actual features of the dead whom they loved, that they had even clasped their hands, and held for an instant the bodies of those whom they had seen die with their own eyes, and buried.

===Chinese===
当然，即便在他阅读的时候，成千条反驳和不利的评论也已涌上他的心头——什么骗局啦、谎话啦、幻觉啦——然而，那一种可能性仍然存在。这里有一些活着的男男女女，他们同样具备平常的感官和理性，却一桩桩、一件件、明明白白地宣告：在某年某月某日，在某处某地，在采取了种种防范欺诈的手段之后，他们收到了来自死者的讯息——这些讯息的含义只有他们自己才懂得——他们亲眼见过、在充足的光线下见过他们所爱的死者的真实面貌，他们甚至握过他们的手，与那些他们亲眼看着死去、亲眼看着下葬的人的身体，相拥过一刹那。

---

===Original===
When the ladies' footsteps had ceased to sound overhead, Laurie went to the French window, opened it, and passed on to the lawn.

He was astonished at the warmth of the September night. The little wind that had been chilly this afternoon had dropped with the coming of the dark, and high overhead he could see the great masses of the leaves motionless against the sky. He passed round the house, and beneath the yews, and sat down on the garden bench.

It was darker here than outside on the lawn. Beneath his feet were the soft needles from the trees, and above him, as he looked out, still sunk in his thought, he could see the glimmer of a star or two between the branches.

It was a fragrant, kindly night. From the hamlet of half a dozen houses beyond the garden came no sound; and the house, too, was still behind him. An illuminated window somewhere on the first floor went out as he looked at it, like a soul leaving a body; once a sleepy bird somewhere in the shrubbery chirped to its mate and was silent again.

===Chinese===
等到头顶上太太小姐们的脚步声不再响起，劳里便走到落地窗前，推开它，走到草坪上去。

九月的夜竟这样暖，他吃了一惊。下午那阵还嫌寒凉的小风，随着天黑已经歇了；抬头望去，他能看见头顶上那大团大团的树叶，纹丝不动地映着夜空。他从屋子的另一面绕过去，走到紫杉树下，在花园的长椅上坐下来。

这里比外头草坪上要暗些。脚底下是树上落下的柔软针叶；他朝外望去，仍沉浸在思索中时，能看见枝桠之间一两点星光的微芒。

这是一个芬芳、温存的夜。花园外那由六七座房子组成的小村落没有传来一点声响；身后的宅子也静悄悄的。他望着二楼某处一扇亮着的窗，那窗子便在他眼前灭了，像是一个灵魂离开了肉体；灌木丛里某处，一只睡眼惺忪的小鸟朝它的伴侣叫了一声，又复归于沉寂。

===Original===
Then as he still labored in argument, putting this against that, and weighing that against the other, his emotion rose up in an irresistible torrent, and all consideration ceased. One thing remained: he must have Amy, or he must die.

---

===Chinese===
然后，他仍在那里苦苦地辩驳，把这一条与那一条相对，把那一条与另一条相称，他的情感便如不可阻挡的洪流般涌起，一切考虑都停止了。只剩下一件事：他必须得到艾米，否则他就得死。

---

===Original===
It was five or six minutes before he moved again from that attitude of clenched hands and tensely strung muscles into which his sudden passion had cast him.

During those minutes he had willed with his whole power that she should come to him now and here, down in this warm and fragrant darkness, hidden from all eyes—in this sweet silence, round which sleep kept its guard. Such things had happened before; such things must have happened, for the will and the love of man are the mightiest forces in creation. Surely again and again it had happened; there must be somewhere in the world man after man who had so called back the dead—a husband sobbing silently in the dark, a child wailing for his mother; surely that force had before, in the world's history, willed back again from the mysterious dark of space the dear personality that was all that even heaven could give, had even compelled into a semblance of life some sort of body to clothe it in. These things must have happened—only secrets had been well kept.

===Chinese===
过了五六分钟，他才从他那一阵骤然的激情所把他抛入的那种双拳紧握、筋肉绷紧的姿态里动弹一下。

在那几分钟里，他正以全部的力量发着愿，要她此时此地来到他面前，降临到这一团温暖芳香的黑暗里，避开一切眼睛——降临到这一片甜蜜的寂静里，四周有睡眠把着岗。这样的事从前发生过；这样的事一定发生过，因为人的意志和爱是造化之中最强大的力量。它一定发生过一次又一次；世上一定有一个又一个的男人曾经这样把死者唤回过——一个丈夫在黑暗中无声地啜泣，一个孩子哭喊着要他的母亲；那股力量，在世界的历史上，一定曾经把那个亲爱的、连天堂也无可增添的人儿，从太虚的神秘幽暗里重新愿了回来，甚至强行把某种身体勉强凑成一具似生非生的样子，给她披上。这些事一定发生过——只不过秘密被严严地保守住了。

===Original===
So this boy had willed it; yet the dark had remained empty; and no shadow, no faintly outlined face, had even for an instant blotted out the star on which he stared; no touch on his shoulder, no whisper in his ear. It had seemed as he strove there, in the silence, that it must be done; that there was no limit to power concentrated and intense. Yet it had not happened.…

Once he had shuddered a little; and the very shudder of fear had had in it a touch of delicious, trembling expectation. Yet it had not happened.

Laurie relaxed his muscles therefore, let his breath exhale in a long sigh, and once more remembered the book he had read and Mrs. Stapleton's feverish, self-conscious thought.

Half an hour later his mother, listening in her bed, heard his footsteps pass her room.

===Chinese===
于是这少年就这样发了愿；然而黑暗依旧空空如也；没有一道影子，没有一张隐约显出轮廓的面孔，曾经哪怕一刹那遮断过他凝视的那颗星；没有一只手按上他的肩头，没有一声低语落进他的耳朵。他在那寂静中苦苦使劲的时候，似乎觉得这件事一定会成；觉得凝聚到极处、紧张到极处的力量是没有限度的。然而它竟没有发生。……

有一回他微微打了个寒颤；那一下因恐惧而起的寒颤里，还夹着一点甜蜜的、颤动的期盼。然而它竟没有发生。

于是劳里松弛了筋肉，让一口气化作一声长叹呼了出来，又重新想起了他读过的那本书，以及斯台普顿太太那套发着热病、忸怩作态的思想。

半小时之后，他母亲在床上听着，听见他的脚步声从她房门前走过。

## III / III

===Original===
Lady Laura Bethell, spinster, had just returned to her house in Queen’s Gate, with her dearest friend, Mrs. Stapleton, for a few days of psychical orgy. It was in her house, as much as in any in London, that the modern prophets were to be met with—severe-looking women in shapeless dresses, little men and big, with long hair and cloaks; and it was in her drawing room that tea and Queen cakes were dispensed to inquirers, and papers read and discussed when the revels were over.

Lady Laura herself was not yet completely emancipated from what her friends sometimes called the graveclothes of so-called Revelation. To her it seemed a profound truth that things could be true and untrue simultaneously—that what might be facts on This Side, as she would have expressed it, might be falsehoods on the Other. She was accustomed, therefore, to attend All Saints’, Carlton Gardens, in the morning, and psychical drawing rooms or halls in the evening, and to declare to her friends how beautifully the one aspect illuminated and interpreted the other.

For the rest, she was a small, fair-haired woman, with penciled dark eyebrows, a small aquiline nose, gold pince-nez, and an exquisite taste in dress.

===Chinese===
劳拉·贝塞尔夫人（Lady Laura Bethell），一位老姑娘，刚回到她王后门（Queen’s Gate）的宅子里，与她最要好的朋友斯台普顿太太（Mrs. Stapleton）一同过几天通灵的狂欢。正是在她这所宅子里——伦敦再没有哪一家比它更甚——可以见到那些现代的先知：神情严峻、身穿不成形的宽袍的女人，以及大大小小的男人，都蓄着长发、披着斗篷；也正是在她的客厅里，茶点和皇后蛋糕被端到诸位求道者面前，待狂欢散场，便有人宣读论文、加以讨论。

至于劳拉夫人本人，她还没有完全挣脱朋友们有时所谓的「所谓启示的裹尸布」。在她看来，事物可以同时既真又不真——这一层仿佛是一条深刻的真理：用她自己的话来说，在这边（This Side）成立的，到了那边（the Other）也许便是谎话。因此她惯于上午去卡尔顿花园（Carlton Gardens）的万圣堂（All Saints’），晚上又出入各种通灵客厅或通灵讲堂，并向朋友们宣称，这两方面彼此之间照应发明，是何等美妙。

至于其余，她是个身材娇小、金发白皙的女人，描着黑眉，生一只小小的鹰钩鼻，戴一副金丝夹鼻眼镜，衣着品味极佳。

===Original===
The two were seated this Tuesday evening, a week after Mrs. Stapleton’s visit to the Stantons, in the drawing room of the Queen’s Gate house, over the remnants of what corresponded to five-o’clock tea. I say “corresponded,” since both of them were sufficiently advanced to have renounced actual tea altogether. Mrs. Stapleton partook of a little hot water out of a copper-jacketed jug; her hostess of boiled milk. They shared their Plasmon biscuits together. These things were considered important for those who would successfully find the Higher Light.

At this instant they were discussing Mr. Vincent.

“Dearest, he seems to me so different from the others,” mewed Lady Laura. “He is such a man, you know. So often those others are not quite like men at all; they wear such funny clothes, and their hair always is so queer, somehow.”

“Darling, I know what you mean. Yes, there’s a great deal of that about James Vincent. Even dear Tom was almost polite to him: he couldn’t bear the others: he said that he always thought they were going to paw him.”

“And then his powers,” continued Lady Laura—“his powers always seem to me so much greater. The magnetism is so much more evident.”

===Chinese===
这个星期二的傍晚，距斯台普顿太太去斯坦顿家已有一周，两人坐在王后门宅子的客厅里，面前摆着某种相当于五点钟茶点的东西的残局。我说「相当于」，因为她们二位都已修炼到相当境界，索性把真正的茶 altogether 戒了。斯台普顿太太从一个铜壳壶里喝一点热水；她的女主人则喝煮过的牛奶。两人一同分享她们的普拉斯蒙（Plasmon）饼干。凡欲成功地寻得那更高之光（the Higher Light）的人，这些都是不可忽视的要务。

此时此刻，她们正在谈论文森特先生（Mr. Vincent）。

「亲爱的，我觉得他跟其他那些人太不一样了。」劳拉夫人细声细气地说。「他是那么一个有男人气概的人，你知道。别的那些常常压根儿就不像个男人；他们穿得那样古怪，那头发又总是那么怪里怪气的，说不上来。」

「达令，我懂你的意思。是的，詹姆斯·文森特（James Vincent）身上确实有不少叫人另眼相看的地方。连亲爱的汤姆对他都差不多算是客气了：别的人他可受不了：他说他老觉得那些人好像要伸手来抓他似的。」

「还有他的本领，」劳拉夫人接着说——「他的本领在我看总要大得多。那股磁力也明显得多。」

===Original===
Mrs. Stapleton finished her hot water.

“We are going on Sunday?” she said questioningly.

“Yes; just a small party. And he comes here tomorrow, you remember, just for a talk. I have asked a clergyman I know in to meet him. It seems to me such a pity that our religious teachers should know so little of what is going on.”

“Who is he?”

“Oh, Mr. Jamieson… just a young clergyman I met in the summer. I promised to let him know the next time Mr. Vincent came to me.”

Mrs. Stapleton murmured her gratification.

===Chinese===
斯台普顿太太喝完了她的热水。

「我们星期天去？」她带着询问的口气说。

「是的；只是一小拨人。还有他明天要来这里，你记得的，只是随便谈谈。我请了一位我认识的牧师来跟他见面。我觉得，我们的宗教导师们对眼下正发生的事竟这般一无所知，实在可惜。」

「他是谁？」

「哦，杰米森先生（Mr. Jamieson）……不过是位年轻的牧师，我夏天认识的。我答应过他，下次文森特先生来我这里就告诉他一声。」

斯台普顿太太低声表示了她的欣慰。

===Original===
These two had really a great deal in common besides their faith. It is true that Mrs. Stapleton was forty, and her friend but thirty-one; but the former did all that was possible to compensate for this by adroit toilette tactics. Both, too, were accustomed to dress in soft materials, with long chains bearing various emblems; they did their hair in the same way; they cultivated the same kinds of tones in their voices—a purring, mewing manner—suggestive of intuitive kittens. Both alike had a passion for proselytism. But after that the differences began. There was a deal more in Mrs. Stapleton besides the kittenish qualities. She was perfectly capable of delivering a speech in public; she had written some really well-expressed articles in various Higher periodicals; and she had a willpower beyond the ordinary. At the point where Lady Laura began to deprecate and soothe, Mrs. Stapleton began to clear decks for action, so to speak, to be incisive, to be fervent, even to be rather eloquent. She kept “dear Tom,” the Colonel, not crushed or beaten, for that was beyond the power of man to do, but at least silently acquiescent in her program: he allowed her even to entertain her prophetical friends at his expense, now and then; and, even when among men, refrained from too bitter speech. It was said by the Colonel’s friends that Mrs. Colonel had a tongue of her own. Certainly, she ruled her house well and did her duty; and it was only because of her husband’s absence in Scotland that during this time she was permitting herself the refreshment of a week or two among the illuminated.

===Chinese===
这两位除了信仰之外，实在还有许多共通之处。斯台普顿太太诚然四十岁了，而她的朋友不过三十一；可前者靠着巧妙的梳妆术，竭力将这一点弥补过去。两人也都惯于穿柔软料子的衣裳，挂着垂长的、缀着各样徽记的链子；她们梳一样的发式；她们用同一种腔调训练自己的嗓音——一种呼噜呼噜、细声细气的作派——叫人联想到凭直觉行事的小猫。两人都同样热衷于劝人入道。但再往下，不同之处便开始了。斯台普顿太太身上除了这点猫咪气，还大有别的。她完全能够当众发表演说；她为各种「更高」刊物写过几篇确实文从字顺的文章；她还有一股超乎寻常的意志力。劳拉夫人开始委婉周旋、息事宁人的那个分寸上，斯台普顿太太恰好开始收拾甲板、准备行动，可以这样说——开始犀利，开始热烈，甚至相当雄辩。她把「亲爱的汤姆」，那位上校，管得虽说不至于俯首帖耳、一败涂地——那是超乎人力所能为的——至少也得叫他在她的方案前默然顺从：他甚至允许她偶尔拿他的开销来款待她那些先知朋友；而且，即便在男人堆里，他也忍着不说太过刻薄的话。上校的朋友们都说，上校夫人有她自己的口条。她确乎把这个家治得井井有条，尽到了她的本分；也只因丈夫去了苏格兰不在家，这段时间她才容许自己到这些「受光照者」当中，舒舒服服地消受一两个礼拜。

===Original===
At about six o’clock Lady Laura announced her intention of retiring for her evening meditation. Opening out of her bedroom was a small dressing-room that she had fitted up for this purpose with all the broad suggestiveness that marks the Higher Thought: decked with ornaments emblematical of at least three religions, and provided with a faldstool and an exceedingly easy chair. It was here that she was accustomed to spend an hour before dinner, with closed eyes, emancipating herself from the fetters of sense; and rising to a due appreciation of that nothingness that was all, from which all came and to which it retired.

“I must go, dearest; it is time.”

A ring at the bell below made her pause.

“Do you think that can be Mr. Vincent?” she said, pleasantly apprehensive. “It’s not the right day, but one never knows.”

A footman’s figure entered.

“Mr. Baxter, my lady.… Is your ladyship at home?”

“Mr. Baxter—”

Mrs. Stapleton rose.

“Let me see him instead, dearest.… You remember… from Stantons.”

“I wonder what he wants?” murmured the hostess. “Yes, do see him, Maud; you can always fetch me if it’s anything.”

===Chinese===
大约六点钟，劳拉夫人宣布她要告退去做晚间的冥想。从她卧室里套出一小间梳妆室，她为这个目的把它布置得十分齐全，处处带着「更高思想」（the Higher Thought）那种大而化之的暗示意味：摆满了至少象征三种宗教的饰物，还配有一张折叠跪凳和一张舒服到极点的安乐椅。她惯于在饭前在这里消磨一个钟头，闭着眼，把自己从感官的枷锁中解放出来；渐渐上升到对那「一切皆是」的虚无的应有体认——万物由之而来，亦将归之而去。

「我得走了，亲爱的；到时候了。」

楼下一阵门铃声叫她顿住。

「你想那是文森特先生吗？」她带着几分又喜又怕的口气说。「今天不是正日子，不过也说不定。」

一个男仆的影子走了进来。

「巴克斯特先生到，夫人。……夫人您见客吗？」

「巴克斯特先生——」

斯台普顿太太站起身来。

「让我替你去见他吧，亲爱的。……你记得的……在斯坦顿家见过的那位。」

「不知道他有什么事？」女主人低声道。「好吧，你去见他，莫德（Maud）；要是有什么事，你随时来叫我。」

===Original===
Then she was gone. Mrs. Stapleton sank into a chair again; and in a minute Laurie was shaking hands with her.

Mrs. Stapleton was accustomed to deal with young men, and through long habit had learned how to flatter them without appearing to do so. Laurie’s type, however, was less familiar to her. She preferred the kind that grow their hair rather long and wear turndown collars, and have just found out the hopeless banality of all orthodoxy whatever. She even bore with them when they called themselves unmoral. But she remembered Laurie, the silent boy at lunch last week, she had even mentioned him to Lady Laura, and received information about the village girl, more or less correct. She was also aware that he was a Catholic.

She gave him her hand without rising.

“Lady Laura asked me to excuse her absence to you, Mr. Baxter. To be quite truthful, she is at home, but had just gone upstairs for her meditation.”

“Indeed!”

“Yes, you know; we think that so important, just as you do. Do sit down, Mr. Baxter. You have had tea?”

“Yes, thanks.”

“I hope she will be down before you go. I don’t think she’ll be very long this evening. Can I give her any message, Mr. Baxter, in case you don’t see her?”

===Chinese===
于是她便走了。斯台普顿太太重新坐进椅子里；不出一分钟，劳里便进来与她握手了。

斯台普顿太太素来惯于应付年轻男子，凭多年的习惯，早已学会了怎样不着痕迹地奉承他们。然而劳里这一型，她倒不那么熟。她更中意那一类——头发留得颇长，戴翻领衬衫，刚刚发现一切正统信仰都无可救药地平庸乏味。他们哪怕自称「非道德」，她也忍得住。可她记得劳里，上礼拜午饭时那个一声不响的少年，她甚至向劳拉夫人提过他，也得到了关于那个乡下姑娘的一些消息，大体不差。她也知道他是个天主教徒。

她没有起身，把手伸给了他。

「巴克斯特先生，劳拉夫人让我代她向你告罪。说实话，她人在家，只是刚刚上楼去做冥想了。」

「是吗！」

「是的，你知道；我们觉得这事要紧得很，跟你们一样。请坐，巴克斯特先生。你用过茶了吗？」

「用过了，谢谢。」

「希望她能在你走之前下来。我看她今晚不会太久。万一你见不到她，要不要我替你带句话，巴克斯特先生？」

===Original===
Laurie put his hat and stick down carefully, and crossed his legs.

“No; I don’t think so, thanks,” he said. “The fact is, I came partly to find out your address, if I might.”

Mrs. Stapleton rustled and rearranged herself.

“Oh! but that’s charming of you,” she said. “Is there anything particular?”

“Yes,” said Laurie slowly; “at least it seems rather particular to me. It’s what you were talking about the other day.”

“Now how nice of you to say that! Do you know, I was wondering as we talked. Now do tell me exactly what is in your mind, Mr. Baxter.”

Mrs. Stapleton was conscious of a considerable sense of pleasure. Usually she found this kind of man very imperceptive and gross. Laurie seemed perfectly at his ease, dressed quite in the proper way, and had an air of presentableness that usually only went with Philistinism. She determined to do her best.

“May I speak quite freely, please?” he asked, looking straight at her.

“Please, please,” she said, with that touch of childish intensity that her friends thought so innocent and beautiful.

===Chinese===
劳里小心翼翼地放下帽子和手杖，翘起了腿。

「不；我看不必了，谢谢，」他说。「其实，我来有一部分是想知道您的住址，不知可不可以。」

斯台普顿太太窸窸窣窣地把身子整理了一番。

「哦！那可太好了，」她说。「有什么特别的事吗？」

「有的，」劳里慢吞吞地说；「至少在我看来挺要紧。就是您那天谈到的那件事。」

「哎，你肯这样说，太好了！你可知道，我们谈着的时候我就在琢磨呢。那么，你心里到底是怎么想的，请原原本本告诉我吧，巴克斯特先生。」

斯台普顿太太心里着实有一阵欢喜。她平日里觉得这种男人又迟钝、又粗俗。劳里却仿佛十分自在，衣着的式样也颇为得体，那副拿得出手的气派，通常只与庸俗的市侩气为伍。她打定主意要尽她所能。

「我可以直截了当地说吗，请问？」他问道，眼睛直直地看着她。

「请说，请说，」她说，带着一点孩子气的热切——朋友们都说这份热切多么天真，多么动人。

===Original===
“Well, it’s like this,” said Laurie. “I’ve always rather disliked all that kind of thing, more than I can say. It did seem to me so—well—so feeble, don’t you know; and then I’m a Catholic, you see, and so—”

“Yes; yes?”

“Well, I’ve been reading Mr. Stainton Moses, and one or two other books; and I must say that an awful lot of it seems to me still great rubbish; and then there are any amount of frauds, aren’t there, Mrs. Stapleton, in that line?”

“Alas! Ah, yes!”

“But then I don’t know what to make of some of the evidence that remains. It seems to me that if evidence is worth anything at all, there must be something real at the back of it all. And then, if that is so, if it really is true that it is possible to get into actual touch with people who are dead—I mean really and truly, so that there’s no kind of doubt about it—well, that does seem to me about the most important thing in the world. Do you see?”

===Chinese===
「是这样，」劳里说。「我向来挺讨厌这一类的事，讨厌得没法说。它在我看实在——呃——实在叫人觉得软弱无力，你知道；况且我是个天主教徒，所以——」

「是的；然后呢？」

「我读了斯坦顿·摩西先生（Mr. Stainton Moses）的书，还有一两本别的；我得说，那里头有一大堆，在我看仍旧是十足的废话；再说了，那一行里头骗子也多得数不胜数，不是吗，斯台普顿太太？」

「唉！是啊！」

「可剩下的一些证据，我又不知道该怎么看了。在我看来，倘若证据还算有点价值的话，那么这一切的背后，总该有点真实的东西。再说，倘若果真如此——倘若真能与亡故的人取得实质的联络——我说的是千真万确，毋庸置疑的那种——那么，在我看来，这便是世上头等重要的事了。你明白吗？」

===Original===
She kept her eyes on his face for an instant or two. Plainly he was really moved; his face had gone a little white in the lamplight and his hands were clasped tightly enough over his knee to whiten the knuckles. She remembered Lady Laura’s remarks about the village girl, and understood. But she perceived that she must not attempt intimacy just yet with this young man: he would resent it. Besides, she was shrewd enough to see by his manner that he did not altogether like her.

She nodded pensively once or twice. Then she turned to him with a bright smile. “I understand entirely,” she said. “(May I too speak quite freely? Yes?) Well, I am so glad you have spoken out. Of course, we are quite accustomed to being distrusted and feared. After all, it is the privilege of all truth-seekers to suffer, is it not? Well, I will say what is in my heart.

“First, you are quite right about some of our workers being dishonest sometimes. They are, Mr. Baxter. I have seen more than one, myself, exposed. But that is natural, is it not? Why, there have been bad Catholics, too, have there not? And, after all, we are only human; and there is a great temptation sometimes not to send people away disappointed. You have heard those stories, I expect, Mr. Baxter?”

“I have heard of Mr. Eglinton.”

“Ah! Poor Willie.… Yes. But he had great powers, for all that.… Well, but the point you want to get at is this, is it not? Is it really true, underneath it all? Is that it?”

===Chinese===
她盯着他的脸看了一两瞬。他显然是真动了心；灯光下他的脸已有些发白，双手紧攥在膝上，攥得指节都泛白了。她记起劳拉夫人关于那个乡下姑娘的一番话，心里明白了。可她也察觉，对这个年轻人眼下还不能贸然套近乎：他会反感的。况且她相当精明，已从他举止间看出，他并不怎么喜欢她。

她若有所思地点了一两下头。然后她转过脸来，带着明朗的微笑。「我完全明白，」她说。「（我也可以直截了当地说吗？可以？）好，你能把话说出来，我真高兴。自然，我们早已惯于被人不信任、被人害怕了。归根到底，受苦本是所有求真者的特权，不是吗？好，我就把心里的话说出来。

「第一，你说我们这一行里有些干事的人有时不老实，这话一点不错。他们确实如此，巴克斯特先生。被我亲眼看见当众戳穿的，就不止一个。可这也理所当然，不是吗？为什么，连天主教徒里都有坏人，不是吗？况且，说到底我们也不过是凡人；有时候，不忍叫人满怀希望而来、扫兴而归，这其中的诱惑可实在不小。那些事你想必听说过吧，巴克斯特先生？」

「我听说过埃格林顿先生（Mr. Eglinton）的事。」

「啊！可怜的威利（Willie）。……是的。可话虽如此，他的本事是很大的。……好，可你想弄明白的，是这一点，对不对？这一切的底下，到底是不是真的？是这样吗？」

===Original===
Laurie nodded, looking at her steadily. She leaned forward.

“Mr. Baxter, by all that I hold most sacred, I assure you that it is, that I myself have seen and touched… *touched*… my own father, who crossed over twenty years ago. I have received messages from his own lips… and communications in other ways too, concerning matters only known to him and to myself. Is that sufficient? No;” (she held up a delicate silencing hand) “… no, I will not ask you to take my word. I will ask you to test it for yourself.”

Laurie too leaned forward now in his low chair, his hands clasped between his knees.

“You will—you will let me test it?” he said in a low voice.

She sat back easily, pushing her draperies straight. She was in some fine silk that fell straight from her high slender waist to her copper-colored shoes.

“Listen, Mr. Baxter. Tomorrow there is coming to this house certainly the greatest medium in London, if not in Europe. (Of course we cannot compete with the East. We are only children beside them.) Well, this man, Mr. Vincent—I think I spoke of him to you last week—he is coming here just for a talk to one or two friends. There shall be no difficulty if you wish it. I will speak to Lady Laura before you go.”

===Chinese===
劳里点了点头，定定地看着她。她向前倾着身子。

「巴克斯特先生，凭我所视为最神圣的一切向你担保，我向你保证：这是真的——我亲眼见过、亲手摸过……*摸过*……我自己的父亲，他二十年前便到了那边。我亲耳从他口中听过消息……也以别的方式领过信息，所说的都是唯有他和我两人才知道的事。这够了吗？不；」（她举起一只纤细的手，叫他不要作声）「……不，我不叫你单凭我的话就信。我要叫你自己去试一试。」

劳里这时也在那张矮椅子上向前倾着身子，双手攥在两膝之间。

「你肯——你肯叫我去试一试？」他低声说。

她从容地往后靠了靠，把身上那一袭衣裙捋顺。她穿着一身上好的丝绸料子，从她那高而纤细的腰笔直垂下，一直落到她那双铜色的鞋上。

「听我说，巴克斯特先生。明天要到这宅子里来的，是伦敦——哪怕全欧洲——数一数二的灵媒（medium），这绝非夸口。（自然，我们比不上东方。在他们面前我们不过是个孩子。）好，这个人，文森特先生——我想上礼拜我跟你提过他——他要来这里，只是跟一两位朋友谈谈。你要是愿意，不会有任何难处的。你走之前，我会跟劳拉夫人说去。」

===Original===
Laurie looked at her without moving.

“I shall be very much obliged,” he said. “You will remember that I am not yet in the least convinced? I only want to know.”

“That is exactly the right attitude. That is all we have any right to ask. We do not ask for blind faith, Mr. Baxter—only for believing after having seen.”

Laurie nodded slowly.

“That seems to me reasonable,” he said.

There was silence for a moment. Then she determined on a bold stroke.

“There is someone in particular—Mr. Baxter—forgive me for asking—someone who has passed over—?”

She sank her voice to what she had been informed was a sympathetic tone, and was scarcely prepared for the sudden tightening of that face.

“That is my affair, Mrs. Stapleton.”

Ah well, she had been premature. She would fetch Lady Laura, she said; she thought she might venture for such a purpose. No, she would not be away three minutes. Then she rustled out.

===Chinese===
劳里一动不动地看着她。

「那就太承情了，」他说。「请您记得，我还一点都没有被说服呢？我不过想知道而已。」

「这恰恰是应有的态度。我们至多也只有权利要求这一点。我们并不要求盲信，巴克斯特先生——只求见了之后再去相信。」

劳里缓缓点头。

「这我听着还算公道，」他说。

一时默然。接着她打定主意，要冒一冒险。

「是不是有某一位——巴克斯特先生——恕我冒昧——某一位已经过去了的人——？」

她把声音压低到人家告诉她最为「同情」的那种调子，却几乎没料到那张脸会那样陡然绷紧。

「这是我的私事，斯台普顿太太。」

罢了，她操之过急了。她说，她去把劳拉夫人叫下来；为着这样一桩事，她想自己还可以壮着胆子去打扰一下。不，去了就回，前后不过三分钟。于是她窸窸窣窣地走了出去。

===Original===
Laurie went to the fire to wait, and stood there, mechanically warming his hands and staring down at that sleeping core of red coal.

He had taken his courage in both hands in coming at all. In spite of his brave words to Maggie, he had been conscious of a curious repulsion with regard to the whole matter—a repulsion not only of contempt towards the elaborate affectations of the woman he had determined to consult. Yet he had come.

What he had said just now had been perfectly true. He was not yet in the least convinced, but he was anxious, intensely and passionately anxious, goaded too by desire.

Ah! surely it was absurd and fantastic—here in London, in this century. He turned and faced the lamplit room, letting his eyes wander round the picture-hung walls, the blue stamped paper, the Empire furniture, the general appearance of beautiful comfort and sane modern life. It was absurd and fantastic; he would be disappointed again, as he had been disappointed in everything else. These things did not happen—the dead did not return. Step by step those things that for centuries had been deemed evidence of the supernatural, one by one had been explained and discounted. Hypnotism, water divining, witchcraft, and the rest. All these had once been believed to be indisputable proofs of a life beyond the grave, of strange supernormal personalities, and these, one by one, had been either accounted for or discredited. It was mad of him to be alarmed or excited. No, he would go through with it, expecting nothing, hoping nothing. But he would just go through with it to satisfy himself.…

The door opened, and the two ladies came in.

“I am delighted that you called, Mr. Baxter; and on such an errand!”

Lady Laura put out a hand, tremulous with pleasure at welcoming a possible disciple.

“Mrs. Stapleton has explained—” began Laurie.

“I understand everything. You come as a skeptic—no, not as a skeptic, but as an inquirer, that is all that we wish.… Then tomorrow, at about half-past four.”

===Chinese===
劳里走到炉边去等，站在那儿，机械地烘着手，垂眼望着那一炉酣睡的红煤核。

他这次肯登门，已是鼓足了全部勇气。尽管他对玛吉说了那番硬气话，他对这整桩事仍感到一种古怪的反感——这反感不仅是对那位他打定主意要请教的女人那身煞费苦心的做作气派的轻蔑。可他还是来了。

他方才所说的话，句句是真。他还一点没有被说服，可他焦灼着——那种焦灼深切而炽烈——他还被一股欲望驱赶着。

啊！这当然是荒唐、是妄想——在伦敦，在这世纪里。他转过身，面朝着这间灯烛辉煌的屋子，任凭目光在那挂满画的墙壁上游走，看那蓝色压花墙纸，那帝国式的家具，看那种种显示出舒适与理智的现代生活的气象。这是荒唐，是妄想；他还会再失望一场，正如他在别的一切事上都失望过一样。这种事不会发生——亡者不归。数百年来被视为超自然之佐证的事物，业已一步一步地被人解释清楚、打了折扣。催眠术、寻水术、巫术，等等。所有这些都曾被当作无可辩驳的凭据，证明死后另有生命、另有种种超常的人格，而它们一个接一个地，要么被说清楚了，要么声名扫地。他为此惊慌、为此兴奋，是疯了。不，他要硬着头皮把这桩事做到底，不存指望，不存希望。可他还是要把它做到底，好叫自己心里有个交代。……

门开了，两位女士走了进来。

「巴克斯特先生，你能登门，而且是为此事而来，我真太高兴了！」

劳拉夫人伸出一只手来，因欢迎一位可能的皈依者而欢喜得微微发颤。

「斯台普顿太太已经说——」劳里开口道。

「我全明白了。你以怀疑者的身份而来——不，不是怀疑者，而是求道者，这正是我们所希望的。……那么明天，四点半左右。」

## IV / IV

### I / I

===Original===
It was a mellow October afternoon, glowing towards sunset, as Laurie came across the south end of the park to his appointment next day; and the effect of it upon his mind was singularly unsuggestive of supernatural mystery. Instead, rather, the warm sky, the lights beginning to peep here and there, though an hour before sunset, turned him rather in the direction of the natural and the domestic.

He wondered what his mother and Maggie would say if they knew his errand, for he had sufficient self-control not to have told them of his intentions. As regards his mother he did not care very much. Of course she would deprecate it and feebly dissuade; but he recognized that there was no particular principle behind, beyond a sense of discomfort at the unknown. But it was necessary for him to argue with himself about Maggie. The angry kind of contempt that he knew she would feel needed an answer; and he gave it by reminding himself that she had been brought up in a convent-school, that she knew nothing of the world, and that, lastly, he himself did not take the matter seriously. He was aware, too, that the instinctive repulsion that she felt so keenly found a certain echo in his own feelings; but he explained this by the novelty of the thing.

In fact, the attitude of mind in which he more or less succeeded in arraying himself was that of one who goes to see a serious conjurer. It would be rather fun, he thought, to see a table dancing. But there was not wholly wanting that inexplicable tendency of some natures deliberately to deceive themselves on what lies nearest to their hearts.

===Chinese===
次日午后，劳里穿过公园南端前去赴约，正值十月里一个柔和的下午，天色正向着夕阳燃起来；而这光景在他心头所唤起的，却奇特地与超自然的奥秘毫不相干。倒不如说，那暖融融的天、那一盏接一盏这里那里亮起来的灯火——虽离日落还有整整一个钟头——反倒把他的心思引向了人间的、家常的那一面。

他揣想着，母亲和玛吉若知道他此行的目的，会作何话说；他总算还有足够的自持，不曾把自己的打算告诉她们。至于母亲那边，他并不十分在意。她当然会不以为然，会软软地劝阻几句；可他也看得分明，那背后并无什么特别的原则，不过是对未知之事有一种不自在罢了。至于玛吉，他却不能不跟自己辩论一番。他深知她会怀着的那种怒冲冲的轻蔑，是需要一个答复的；而他给自己的答复是——她在修道院学校里长大，对这世上的事一窍不通；况且，末了，他自己也并不把这桩事当真。他也清楚，她所敏锐感到的那种本能的反感，在他自己心里也不无某种回响；可他用「事属新奇」四个字把它解释了过去。

事实上，他多多少少成功给自己穿戴上的那副心境，正是一个去看一位正经魔术师的人的心境。看一张桌子跳舞，他想，倒也颇有趣味。然而，那种在自己心头最近处的事上、偏偏存心自欺的、说不清道不明的倾向——这在某些天性里本也是不能完全免去的。

===Original===
Mr. Vincent had not yet arrived when he was shown upstairs, even though Laurie himself was late. (This was partly deliberate. He thought it best to show a little nonchalance.) There was only a young clergyman in the room with the ladies; and the two were introduced.

“Mr. Baxter—Mr. Jamieson.”

He seemed a harmless young man, thought Laurie, and plainly a little nervous at the situation in which he found himself, as might a greyhound carry himself in a kennel of well-bred foxhounds. He was very correctly dressed, with Roman collar and stock, and obviously had not long left a theological college. He had an engaging kind of courtesy, ecclesiastically cut features, and curly black hair. He sat balancing a delicate cup adroitly on his knee.

“Mr. Jamieson is so anxious to know all that is going on,” explained Lady Laura, with a voluble frankness. “He thinks it so necessary to be abreast of the times, as he said to me the other day.”

Laurie assented, grimly pitying the young man for his indiscreet confidences. The clergyman looked priggish in his efforts not to do so.

“He has a class of young men on Sundays,” continued the hostess—“(Another biscuit, Maud darling?)—whom he tries to interest in all modern movements. He thinks it so important.”

Mr. Jamieson cleared his throat in a virile manner.

“Just so,” he said; “exactly so.”

“And so I told him he must really come and meet Mr. Vincent.… I can’t think why he is so late; but he has so many calls upon his time, that I am sure I wonder—”

“Mr. Vincent,” announced the footman.

===Chinese===
他被引上楼时，文森特先生（Mr. Vincent）竟还没有到，尽管劳里自己已经来迟了。（这也有一半是故意的。他觉得露一点漫不经心为妙。）屋里跟两位女士一同在场的，只有一位年轻的牧师；于是两人被引见了一番。

「巴克斯特先生——杰米森先生（Mr. Jamieson）。」

劳里心想，他看上去是个无伤大雅的年轻人，而且显然对眼下的场合有些发怵，那样子好比一条灵犬置身于一群血统纯正的猎狐犬的犬舍之中。他穿得一丝不苟，戴着罗马式硬领和领巾，分明离开神学院不久。他有一种讨人喜欢的客气，五官是教会中人那种棱角，一头黑色鬈发。他坐在那里，把一只精致的小茶杯灵巧地端在膝上，稳稳地保持平衡。

「杰米森先生急着想把发生的一切都弄个明白，」劳拉·贝塞尔夫人（Lady Laura Bethell）解释道，话滔滔不绝而又坦率，「他认为务必要跟上时代的脚步，前几天他正这么对我说呢。」

劳里随声附和，心里却阴郁地可怜这年轻人竟如此轻率地交了底。那牧师则为了不让自己也显得轻率，反倒端出了一副道学气。

「他星期日还带着一班年轻人，」女主人接着说——「（再来一块饼干，亲爱的莫德？）——他想方设法叫他们对一切现代的思潮都感起兴趣来。他认为这要紧极了。」

杰米森先生男子气地清了清嗓子。

「正是，」他说，「一点不错。」

「所以我便对他说，他实在得来见见文森特先生。……我真想不通他怎么这样晚；可他有那么多事要应酬，我真是纳闷——」

「文森特先生到了。」仆人通报道。

===Original===
A rather fine figure of a man came forward into the room, dressed in much better taste than Laurie somehow had expected, and not at all like the type of an insane dissenting minister in broadcloth which he had feared. Instead, it was a big man that he saw, stooping a little, inclined to stoutness, with a full curly beard tinged with grey, rather overhung brows, and a high forehead, from which the same kind of curly greyish hair was beginning to retreat. He was in a well-cut frock-coat and dark trousers, with the collar of the period and a dark tie.

Lady Laura was in a flutter of welcome, pouring out little sentences, leading him to a seat, introducing him, and finally pressing refreshments into his hands.

“It is too good of you,” she said; “too good of you, with all your engagements.… These gentlemen are most anxious.… Mrs. Stapleton of course you know.… And you will just sit and talk to us… like friends… won’t you.… No, no! no formal speech at all… just a few words… and you will allow us to ask you questions.…”

And so on.

===Chinese===
一个颇为体面的男子走上前来，进了屋子，衣着的品位远比劳里不知怎地所预料的为高，也全然不似他所惧怕的那种穿着粗花呢、疯疯癫癫的非国教牧师的做派。他所看到的，倒是个大块头的男子，背微微佝偻，有点发福，一部染着灰白的浓密鬈须，眉脊略突，高高的额上，同色的灰鬈发已开始后退。他身穿剪裁得体的双排扣礼服大衣，深色长裤，戴的是时下流行的领子，系一条深色领带。

劳拉夫人殷勤得手忙脚乱地欢迎着，一句一句的小话倾泻而出，把他引到座位上，替他引见，末了又把茶点直塞到他手里。

「你真是太好了，」她说，「太好了，你这么忙，还有那么多事……这两位先生都急得很。……斯台普顿太太你当然是认得的。……你这就坐下，跟我们说说话……像朋友那样……好不好。……不，不！千万别来什么正经的演讲……随便几句就好……你容我们问你几个问题。……」

如此这般，不一而足。

===Original===
Meanwhile Laurie observed the high priest carefully and narrowly, and was quite unable to see any of the unpleasant qualities he had expected. He sat easily, without self-consciousness or arrogance or unpleasant humility. He had a pair of pleasant, shrewd, and rather kind eyes; and his voice, when he said a word or two in answer to Lady Laura’s volubility, was of that resonant softness that is always a delight to hear. In fact, his whole bearing and personality was that of a rather exceptional average man—a publisher, it might be, or a retired lawyer—a family man with a sober round of life and ordinary duties, who brought to their fulfillment a wholesome, kindly, but distinctly strong character of his own. Laurie hardly knew whether he was pleased or disappointed. He would almost have preferred a wild creature with rolling eyes, in a cloak; yet he would have been secretly amused and contemptuous at such a man.

“The sitting is off for Sunday, by the way, Lady Laura,” said the newcomer.

“Indeed! How is that?”

“Oh! there was some mistake about the rooms; it’s the secretary’s fault; you mustn’t blame me.”

Lady Laura cried out her dismay and disappointment, and Mrs. Stapleton played chorus. It was *too* tiresome, they said, *too* provoking, particularly just now, when “Annie” was so complacent. (Mrs. Stapleton explained kindly to the two young gentlemen that “Annie” was a spirit who had lately made various very interesting revelations.) What was to be done? Were there no other rooms?

Mr. Vincent shook his head. It was too late, he said, to make arrangements now.

===Chinese===
与此同时，劳里仔仔细细、目不转睛地打量着这位大祭司般的人物，却怎么也找不到他所预想的任何一种讨人厌的品质。他坐得从容自在，既不自矜，也不傲慢，更无那种令人不快的谦卑。他有一双和悦、精明、且颇为善良的眼睛；当他说上一两句话以应答劳拉夫人的滔滔不绝时，那嗓音带着一种洪亮而柔和的质地，听来永远是一种悦事。事实上，他的整个举止为人，是一个颇不寻常的寻常之辈——也许是个出版商，或是个退休的律师——一个有家室的人，过着规规矩矩的圆全日子，尽着寻常的本分，而把自己那份康健、和善、却分明相当强硬的性格带进这些本分里去。劳里简直说不上自己是欢喜还是失望。他几乎倒宁愿看到一个披斗篷、翻白眼的狂人；然而果真是那样一个人，他暗地里又会觉得好笑、且心生鄙夷。

「对了，劳拉夫人，星期日那场降神会（séance）取消了，」这位新到者说。

「当真！怎么回事？」

「噢！地方上出了点岔子；是秘书的过错；你可不能怪我。」

劳拉夫人惊呼着她的懊恼与失望，斯台普顿太太（Mrs. Stapleton）则在旁帮腔。这事真叫人*太*恼火了，她们说，*太*气人了，偏偏赶在「安妮」如此配合的时候。（斯台普顿太太和蔼地向两位年轻先生解释，「安妮」是一位最近披露了好几桩极有趣之事的亡灵。）那可怎么办呢？再没有别的屋子了么？

文森特先生摇了摇头。他说，事到如今再安排，已来不及了。

===Original===
While the ladies continued to buzz, and Mr. Jamieson to listen from the extreme edge of his chair, Laurie continued to make mental comments. He felt distinctly puzzled by the marked difference between the prophet and his disciples. These were so shallow; this so impressive by the most ordinary of all methods, and the most difficult of imitation, that is, by sheer human personality. He could not grasp the least common multiple of the two sides. Yet this man tolerated these women, and, indeed, seemed very kind and friendly towards them. He seemed to possess that sort of competence which rises from the fact of having well-arranged ideas and complete certitude about them.

And at last a pause came. Mr. Vincent set down his cup for the second time, refused buttered bun, and waited.

“Yes, do smoke, Mr. Vincent.”

The man drew out his cigarette-case, smiling, offering it to the two men. Laurie took one; the clergyman refused.

“And now, Mr. Vincent.”

Again he smiled, in a half-embarrassed way.

“But no speeches, I think you said,” he remarked.

“Oh! well, you know what I mean; just like friends, you know. Treat us all like that.”

(Mrs. Stapleton rose, came nearer the circle, rustled down again, and sank into an elaborate silence.)

“Well, what is it these gentlemen wish to hear?”

“Everything—everything,” cried Lady Laura. “They claim to know nothing at all.”

===Chinese===
当两位女士继续嗡嗡地絮叨、而杰米森先生只在椅子最前沿坐着倾听时，劳里心里仍在不停地作着评点。这位先知与其门徒之间判若霄壤的差异，令他着实困惑。那几个如此浅薄；这一位却以所有办法中最寻常、也最难摹仿的一种——即纯凭其人格——而令人肃然起敬。他怎么也把握不住这两边之间的最小公倍数。然而这人竟能容忍这些妇人，而且，不唯容忍，待她们还似乎十分和善友好。他仿佛拥有那样一种干练，那干练源自一件事：他的种种想法安排得井井有条，且对它们怀有彻底的确信。

终于，停顿来了。文森特先生第二回放下茶杯，谢绝了抹了黄油的圆面包，等着。

「好啦，请抽烟吧，文森特先生。」

那人掏出烟盒，微笑着，递向两位男士。劳里取了一支；牧师谢绝了。

「那么现在，文森特先生。」

他又笑了一笑，带着几分不好意思。

「可我记得你说不要演讲来着，」他说。

「噢！这个嘛，你懂我的意思的；就像朋友那样，你懂。就把我们都当作那样的朋友。」

（斯台普顿太太站起身，走近这一圈人，又窸窸窣窣地坐了下去，沉入一种煞费苦心的沉默之中。）

「那么，这两位先生想听些什么呢？」

「什么都要听——什么都要听，」劳拉夫人嚷道，「他们自称什么都不懂。」

===Original===
Laurie thought it time to explain himself a little. He felt he would not like to take this man at an unfair advantage.

“I should just like to say this,” he said. “I have told Mrs. Stapleton already. It is this. I must confess that so far as I am concerned I am not a believer. But neither am I a skeptic. I am just a real agnostic in this matter. I have read several books; and I have been impressed. But there’s a great deal in them that seems to me nonsense; perhaps I had better say which I don’t understand. This materializing business, for instance.… I can understand that the minds of the dead can affect ours; but I don’t see how they can affect matter—in table-rapping, for instance, and still more in appearing, and our being able to touch and see them.… I think that’s my position,” he ended rather lamely.

The fact was that he was a little disconcerted by the other’s eyes. They were, as I have said, kind and shrewd eyes, but they had a good deal of power as well. Mr. Vincent sat motionless during this little speech, just looking at him, not at all offensively, yet with the effect of making the young man feel rather like a defiant and naughty little boy who is trying to explain.

Laurie sat back and drew on his cigarette rather hard.

“I understand perfectly,” said the steady voice. “You are in a very reasonable position. I wish all were as open-minded. May I say a word or two?”

“Please.”

“Well, it is materialization that puzzles you, is it?”

“Exactly,” said Laurie. “Our theologians tell us—by the way, I am a Catholic.” (The other bowed a little.) “Our theologians, I believe, tell us that such a thing cannot be, except under peculiar circumstances, as in the lives of the saints, and so on.”

===Chinese===
劳里觉得该把自己稍作交代了。他觉着不愿占这人的便宜，占个不公平的上风。

「我想先说这么一句，」他说，「这话我已对斯台普顿太太说过了。就是这一句：我得承认，就我个人而言，我并非一个信者。可我也并非一个怀疑论者。在这件事上，我不过是个真正的不可知论者罢了。我读过好几本书；也有几处令我印象深刻。可书里有好些在我看来简直是胡说；也许我该说，有好些是我所不懂的。比如这显形（materialization）的名堂。……亡者的心智能影响我们的心智，这个我能理解；可我看不出它们怎能影响物质——比如桌子敲击之类，更不要说现身，还能让我们触摸得着、看得见了。……我想，这就是我的立场了，」他末了说得颇有些无力。

实情是，对方那双眼睛叫他有点发窘。如我所说，那是一双和善而精明的眼睛，可其中也颇有些威慑之力。文森特先生在这一小段话里端坐不动，只是看着他，丝毫没有冒犯之意，效果却叫这年轻人觉得自己活像一个桀骜、调皮、正竭力辩解的小男孩。

劳里往后一靠，狠狠地抽了一口烟。

「我完全明白，」那沉稳的嗓音说道，「你的立场很有理性。我巴不得人人都这样心胸开阔呢。容我说一两句么？」

「请。」

「那么，是显形这件事叫你困惑，是不是？」

「正是，」劳里说，「我们的神学家告诉我们——对了，我是个天主教徒。」（对方微微颔首。）「我相信我们的神学家是说，这样的事不可能发生，除非在特殊的情形之下，比如诸圣的生平之类。」

===Original===
“Are you bound to believe all that your theologians say?” asked the other quietly.

“Well, it would be very rash indeed—” began Laurie.

“Exactly, I see. But what if you approach it from the other side, and try to find out instead whether these things actually do happen. I do not wish to be rude, Mr. Baxter; but you remember that your theologians—I am not so foolish as to say the Church, for I know that that was not so—but your theologians, you know, made a mistake about Galileo.”

Laurie winced a little. Mr. Jamieson cleared his throat in gentle approval.

“Now I don’t ask you to accept anything contrary to your faith,” went on the other gently; “but if you really wish to look into this matter, you must set aside for the present all other presuppositions. You must not begin by assuming that the theologians are always right, nor even in asking how or why these things should happen. The one point is, *do they happen*?”

His last words had a curious little effect as of a sudden flame. He had spoken smoothly and quietly; then he had suddenly put an unexpected emphasis into the little sentence at the end. Laurie jumped, internally. Yes, that was the point, he assented internally.

“Now,” went on the other, again in that slow, reassuring voice, flicking off the ash of his cigarette, “is it possible for you to doubt that these things happen? May I ask you what books you have read?”

Laurie named three or four.

“And they have not convinced you?”

“Not altogether.”

“Yet you accept human evidence for a great many much more remarkable things than these—as a Catholic.”

“That is divine revelation,” said Laurie, sure of his ground.

“Pardon me,” said the other. “I do not in the least say it is not divine revelation—that is another question—but you receive the statement that it is so, on the word of man. Is that not true?”

Laurie was silent. He did not quite know what to say; and he almost feared the next words. But he was astonished that the other did not press home the point.

“Think over that, Mr. Baxter. That is all I ask. And now for the real thing. You sincerely wish to be convinced?”

“I am ready to be convinced.”

===Chinese===
「你非得相信你们神学家所说的全部么？」那一位平静地问。

「这个，那确是十分轻率——」劳里开口道。

「一点不错，我明白。可你若从另一头下手，转而去查一查这些事究竟是否当真发生，又当如何呢。我不想失礼，巴克斯特先生；可你总记得，你们的神学家——我还不至于愚蠢到说是圣教会，因为我知道并非如此——可你们的神学家，你晓得，在伽利略那桩事上，是弄错了的。」

劳里微微瑟缩了一下。杰米森先生轻声清了清嗓子，以示赞同。

「如今我并不要求你接受任何有违你信仰的东西，」那一位温言接着说，「可你若当真想探究这件事，就得暂时撇开一切别的前提。你不可一上来便假定神学家总是对的，甚至也不可一上来便追问这些事如何发生、为何发生。唯一的关键在于：*它们究竟发不发生？*」

他末了这句话有一种奇异的、如骤然一闪火焰般的效果。他说得一直圆顺而平静；忽然之间，却对末了那短短的一句倾注了出人意料的重音。劳里心里猛地一跳。是的，关键就在这里，他心里应道。

「那么，」那一位又说，重又换上那缓慢、宽稳的嗓音，弹了弹烟灰，「你可还能怀疑这些事会发生么？容我问一句，你读过哪些书？」

劳里报了三四种。

「它们还不曾说服你？」

「不完全是。」

「可你作为一个天主教徒，对于许许多多比这些更为奇异得多的事，你都是凭人的见证而接受的。」

「那是属神的启示，」劳里说，自觉站得稳稳的。

「恕我直言，」那一位说，「我一点也没有说那不是属神的启示——那是另一个问题——可是你之接收「它是属神的启示」这一陈述，凭的是人的话。这不属实么？」

劳里沉默了。他不太知道该说什么；他几乎有些害怕接下来的一句话。可他惊讶地发现，对方并没有趁势追逼这一点。

「把这件事想一想，巴克斯特先生。我只求你这一点。现在说正经的。你真心想被说服么？」

「我愿被说服。」

===Original===
The medium paused an instant, looking intently at the fire. Then he tossed the stump of his cigarette away and lighted another. The two ladies sat motionless.

“You seem fond of *a priori* arguments, Mr. Baxter,” he began, with a kindly smile. “Let us have one or two, then.

“Consider first the relation of your soul to your body. That is infinitely mysterious, is it not? An emotion rises in your soul, and a flush of blood marks it. That is the subconscious mechanism of your body. But to say that, does not explain it. It is only a label. You follow me? Yes? Or still more mysterious is your conscious power. You will to raise your hand, and it obeys. Muscular action? Oh yes; but that is but another label.” (He turned his eyes, suddenly somber, upon the staring, listening young man, and his voice rose a little.) “Go right behind all that, Mr. Baxter, down to the mysteries. What is that link between soul and body? You do not know! Nor does the wisest scientist in the world. Nor ever will. Yet there the link is!”

Again he paused.

Laurie was aware of a rising half-excited interest far beyond the power of the words he heard. Yet the manner of these too was striking. It was not the sham mysticism he had expected. There was a certain reverence in them, an admitting of mysteries, that seemed hard to reconcile with the ideas he had formed of the dogmatism of these folk.

===Chinese===
灵媒（medium）停了一瞬，凝神望着炉火。接着他把烟蒂一抛，又点了一支。两位女士一动不动地坐着。

「你似乎颇好*先验的*（*a priori*）论证，巴克斯特先生，」他含着和悦的微笑开口道，「那么，我们便来上一两条吧。

「先想想你的灵魂与你的身体之间的关系。那是无限神秘的，不是么？一种情绪在你的灵魂里升起，一涌血色便随之标出它来。那便是你身体的潜意识机制。可那样说，并不曾解释它。那不过是一个标签罢了。你跟得上么？跟得上？又或者，更为神秘的，是你那有意识的意志之力。你立意要抬手，手便服从了。肌肉的动作么？噢，是的；可那也不过是另一个标签罢了。」（他把那双忽然沉郁下来的眼睛转向这目不转睛、凝神倾听的年轻人，嗓音也略略提高了些。）「一直追溯到这一切的背后去吧，巴克斯特先生，追到那些奥秘里去。灵魂与身体之间的那一条纽带，究竟是什么？你不知道！世上最博学的科学家也不知道。而且永远不会知道。然而纽带就在那里！」

他又顿了一顿。

劳里觉察到一种半带兴奋的兴趣正在自己心头升起，那力量远非他所听到的那些话语本身所能及。然而这些话语的风度也是惊人的。那并非他所预想的假 mysticism 假神秘。话语里有几分虔敬，有一种对奥秘的承认，这似乎很难与他原先心目中、这等人的那种独断作风调和起来。

===Original===
“Now begin again,” continued the quiet, virile voice. “You believe, as a Christian, in the immortality of the soul, in the survival of personality after death. Thank God for that! All do not, in these days. Then I need not labor at that.

“Now, Mr. Baxter, imagine to yourself some soul that you have loved passionately, who has crossed over to the other side.” (Laurie drew a long, noiseless breath, steadying himself with clenched hands.) “She has come to the unimaginable glories, according to her measure; she is at an end of doubts and fears and suspicions. She knows because she sees.… But do you think that she is absorbed in these things? You know nothing of human love, Mr. Baxter” (the voice trembled with genuine emotion)… “if you can think that!… If you can think that her thought turns only to herself and her joys. Why, her life has been lived in your love by our hypothesis—you were at her bedside when she died, perhaps; and she clung to you as to God Himself, when the shadow deepened. Do you think that her first thought, or at least her second, will not be of you?… In all that she sees, she will desire you to see it also. She will strive, crave, hunger for you—not that she may possess you, but that you may be one with her in her own possession; she will send out vibration after vibration of sympathy and longing; and you, on this side, will be tuned to her as none other can be—you, on this side, will be empty for her love, for the sight and sound of her.… Is death then so strong?—stronger than love? Can a Christian believe that?”

===Chinese===
「现在重新开始，」那沉静而雄健的嗓音接着说，「你作为一个基督徒，相信灵魂的不朽，相信人格在死后犹存。为这一点感谢上帝吧！这些年头，并非人人如此。那么我便不必在这一层上费力了。

「如今，巴克斯特先生，你且在心中设想一个你曾狂热地爱过的灵魂，一个已经过去、到了那边（crossed over）的灵魂。」（劳里长长地、无声地吸了一口气，攥紧了拳头稳住自己。）「她已经按着她的分，来到了那不可想象的荣耀之中；她已到了疑虑、恐惧、猜忌的尽头。她知道，因为她看见。……可你以为她是全然沉浸在这些之中的么？你对人间的爱一无所知，巴克斯特先生」（那嗓音因真挚的情感而颤抖）……「你若能作如是想！……你若以为她的心念只会转向她自己、转向她自己的欢乐。咄，照我们的假设，她的这一辈子原是活在对你的爱之中的——她咽气时，你也许就在床前；当那阴影加深之际，她紧攀着你，如同紧攀上帝本身。你以为她的头一个念头，至少第二个念头，会不是关于你的么？……凡她所见的一切，她都要你也得见。她要竭力、渴慕、饥渴地追寻你——不是为着占有你，而是为着叫你与她、在她自己的那份有里面合而为一；她要发出一波接一波的同情与渴望的震颤；而你，在这一边，将如旁人所不能地被调到与她同频——你，在这一边，将为她的爱而空着，为得见她、得闻她而空着。……那么，死竟这样强么？——比爱还强？一个基督徒能相信这个么？」

===Original===
The change in the man was extraordinary. His heavy beard and brows hid half his face, but his whole being glowed passionately in his voice, even in his little trembling gestures, and Laurie sat astonished. Every word uttered seemed to fit his own case, to express by an almost perfect vehicle the vague thoughts that had struggled in his own heart during this last week. It was Amy of whom the man spoke, Amy with her eyes and hair, peering from the glorious gloom to catch some glimpse of her lover in his meaningless light of earthly day.

Mr. Vincent cleared his throat a little, and at the sound the two motionless women stirred and rustled a little. The sound of a hansom, the spanking trot and wintry jingle of bells swelled out of the distance, passed, and went into silence before he spoke again. Then it was in his usual slow voice that he continued.

“Conceive such a soul as that, Mr. Baxter. She desires to communicate with one she loves on earth, with you or me, and it is a human and innocent desire. Yet she has lost that connection, that machinery of which we have spoken—that connection of which we know nothing, between matter and spirit, except that it exists. What is she to do? Well, at least she will do this, she will bend every power that she possesses upon that medium—I mean matter—through which alone the communication can be made; as a man on an island, beyond the power of a human voice, will use any instrument, however grotesque, to signal to a passing ship. Would any decent man, Mr. Baxter, mock at the pathos and effort of that, even if it were some grotesque thing, like a flannel shirt on the end of an oar? Yet men mock at the tapping of a table!…

“Well, then, this longing soul uses every means at her disposal, concentrates every power she possesses. Is it so very unreasonable, so very unchristian, so very dishonoring to the love of God, to think that she sometimes succeeds?… that she is able, under comparatively exceptional circumstances, to reestablish that connection with material things, that was perfectly normal and natural to her during her earthly life.… Tell me, Mr. Baxter.”

Laurie shifted a little in his chair.

“I cannot say that it is,” he said, in a voice that seemed strange in his own ears. The medium smiled a little.

“So much for *a priori* reasoning,” he said. “There remains only the fact whether such things do happen or not. There I must leave you to yourself, Mr. Baxter.”

===Chinese===
这人的变化是非同小可的。他那浓密的胡须与眉毛遮住了半张脸，可他整个的人都在他的嗓音里——甚至在他那微微发颤的小小手势里——热烈地燃着光，劳里坐在那里，不胜骇异。吐出的每一个字，都仿佛正对着他自己的境况，仿佛以一种几近完美的载体，说出了这一周以来在他自己心里挣扎着的、那些朦胧的念头。这人所说的，正是艾米，正是艾米，带着她的眼、她的发，从那荣耀的幽暗里探出身来，想在她情人那毫无意义的尘世日光里，瞥见他一眼。

文森特先生轻轻清了清嗓子，这一声之下，那两个纹丝不动的女人动了动、窸窣了一下。一辆双轮马车的声音——那轻快的蹄声与冬日里叮当的铃响——从远处涌起，经过，归于沉寂，这之后他才重新开口。于是他又以他素常那缓慢的嗓音接着说。

「设想这样一颗灵魂吧，巴克斯特先生。她渴望与她所爱之人——与你或与我——在世上相通，这是一桩合乎人情、清白无邪的渴望。然而她已失去了那条联结，失去了我们方才所说的那套机括——那条我们一无所知、只知道它确实存在的、物质与灵之间的联结。她该怎么办呢？那么，至少她会做这一件事：她要倾其所有之力，集中到那媒介上——我指的是物质——唯独通过它，交通才得以成就；正如一个困在岛上、喊不出人声的人，无论用多么怪异的器具，都要向一艘经过的船打出信号。巴克斯特先生，但凡一个体面的人，岂会嘲笑那其中的凄恻与努力，哪怕它竟是一桩怪异之物，比如挑在桨头的一件法兰绒衬衫？然而人们竟嘲笑一张桌子的叩击！……

「那么，这一颗渴慕的灵魂便穷尽她手边的一切办法，凝聚她拥有的一切力量。设想她有时竟能成功——这难道很悖理么？很不基督教么？很辱没上帝的爱么？……设想她能够在相对而言特殊的情形之下，重建那条与物质之物的联结——而在她尘世的一生中，这联结原是再正常、再自然不过的。……告诉我，巴克斯特先生。」

劳里在椅子里稍稍挪动了一下。

「我不能说它是，」他说，声音落进自己耳中竟觉着陌生。灵媒微微一笑。

「先验的推论便说到这里，」他说，「剩下的，只是这些事究竟当不当真发生这一桩事实。在那一点上，我只得把你交给你自己了，巴克斯特先生。」

===Original===
Laurie sat forward suddenly.

“But that is exactly where I need your help, sir,” he said.

A murmur broke from the ladies’ lips simultaneously, resembling applause. Mr. Jamieson sat back and swallowed perceptibly in his throat.

“You have said so much, sir,” went on Laurie deliberately, “that you have, so to speak, put yourself in my debt. I must ask you to take me further.”

Mr. Vincent smiled full at him.

“You must take your place with others,” he said. “These ladies—”

“Mr. Vincent, Mr. Vincent,” cried Lady Laura. “He is quite right, you must help him. You must help us all.”

“Well, Sunday week,” he began deprecatingly.

Mrs. Stapleton broke in.

“No, no; now, Mr. Vincent, now. Do something now. Surely the circumstances are favorable.”

“I must be gone again at six-thirty,” said the man hesitatingly.

Laurie broke in. He felt desperate.

“If you can show me anything of this, sir, you can surely show it now. If you do not show it now—”

“Well, Mr. Baxter?” put in the voice, sharp and incisive, as if expecting an insult and challenging it.

Laurie broke down.

“I can only say,” he cried, “that I beg and entreat of you to do what you can—now and here.”

There was a silence.

“And you, Mr. Jamieson?”

The young clergyman started, as if from a daze. Then he rose abruptly.

“I—I must be going, Lady Laura,” he said. “I had no idea it was so late. I—I have a confirmation class.”

An instant later he was gone.

“That is as well,” observed the medium. “And you are sure, Mr. Baxter, that you wish me to try? You must remember that I promise nothing.”

“I wish you to try.”

“And if nothing happens?”

“If nothing happens, I will promise to—to continue my search. I shall know then that—that it is at least sincere.”

Mr. Vincent rose to his feet.

“A little table just here, Lady Laura, if you please, and a pencil and paper.… Will you kindly take your seats?… Yes, Mr. Baxter, draw up your chair… here. Now, please, we must have complete silence, and, so far as possible, silence of thought.”

===Chinese===
劳里忽然身子向前一倾。

「可这一点，恰恰正是我需要您帮忙的地方啊，先生，」他说。

两位女士的唇间同时迸出一阵低语，仿佛喝彩。杰米森先生往后一靠，喉间明显地咽了一咽。

「您说了这许多，先生，」劳里从容不迫地接着说，「可以说，您已经把自己欠下了我的。我不得不请您再带我走远一些。」

文森特先生直望着他笑了。

「那你只好与别的人一同排队了，」他说，「这两位女士——」

「文森特先生，文森特先生，」劳拉夫人叫道，「他完全说得对，你一定得帮帮他。你一定得帮帮我们大家。」

「这个嘛，下下个星期日，」他摇头婉拒道。

斯台普顿太太插了进来。

「不，不；就现在，文森特先生，就现在。现在就来一点吧。眼下的情形总归是有利的。」

「我六点半还得走，」那人为难地说。

劳里插了进去。他觉着自己已是孤注一掷。

「您若能给我看一点什么，先生，您现在就一定能给我看。您要是不现在就——」

「那么怎么样，巴克斯特先生？」那嗓音插进来，又尖又利，仿佛预料着一种侮辱，正挑衅地等着。

劳里崩溃了。

「我只能说，」他喊道，「我恳求您、恳求您，尽您所能——此时，此地。」

一阵沉默。

「那么你呢，杰米森先生？」

那年轻的牧师一惊，仿佛从恍惚中醒过来。接着他猛地站起身。

「我——我该走了，劳拉夫人，」他说，「我竟不知已经这样晚了。我——我还有一个坚振礼的查经班。」

转眼之间他便走了。

「这倒也好，」灵媒说，「那么你拿定主意了，巴克斯特先生，你要我试一试？你得记住，我什么也不答应。」

「我要您试。」

「要是什么也不发生呢？」

「要是什么也不发生，我答应——答应继续我的寻求。那我便会知道——至少它是诚意的。」

文森特先生站起身来。

「一张小桌子，就搁在这里，劳拉夫人，劳驾，再要一支铅笔、一张纸。……各位请就座好么？……对，巴克斯特先生，把椅子拉过来……就在这儿。好了，请各位务必完全静下来，并尽可能做到——心也静下来。」

### II / II

===Original===
The table, a small, round rosewood one, stood, bare of any cloth, upon the hearthrug. The two ladies sat, motionless statues once more, upon the side furthest from the fire, with their hands resting lightly upon the surface. Laurie sat on one side and the medium on the other. Mr. Vincent had received his paper and pencil almost immediately, and now sat resting his right hand with the pencil upon the paper as if to write, his left hand upon his knee as he sat, turned away slightly from the center.

Laurie looked at him closely.…

And now he began to be aware of a certain quite indefinable change in the face at which he looked. The eyes were open—no, it was not in them that the change lay, nor in the lines about the mouth, so far as he could see them, nor in any detail, anywhere. Neither was it the face of a dreamer or a sleepwalker, or of the dead, when the lines disappear and life retires. It was a living, conscious face, yet it was changed. The lips were slightly parted, and the breath came evenly between them. It was more like the face of one lost in deep, absorbed, introspective thought. Laurie decided that this was the explanation.

He looked at the hand on the paper—well shaped, brownish, capable—perfectly motionless, the pencil held lightly between the finger and thumb.

Then he glanced up at the two ladies.

They too were perfectly motionless, but there was no change in them. The eyes of both were downcast, fixed steadily upon the paper. And as he looked he saw Lady Laura begin to lift her lids slowly as if to glance at him. He looked himself upon the paper and the motionless fingers.

He was astonished at the speed with which the situation had developed. Five minutes ago he had been listening to talk, and joining in it. The clergyman had been here; he himself had been sitting a yard further back. Now they sat here as if they had sat for an hour. It seemed that the progress of events had stopped.…

Then he began to listen for the sounds of the world outside, for within here it seemed as if a silence of a very strange quality had suddenly descended and enveloped them. It was as if a section—that place in which he sat—had been cut out of time and space. It was apart here, it was different altogether.…

===Chinese===
那是一张小小的、圆面的红木桌，不着一块桌布，光秃秃地立在炉前地毯上。两位女士坐在离火最远的那一面，重又化作了纹丝不动的雕像，双手轻轻搭在桌面上。劳里坐在这一边，灵媒坐在那一边。文森特先生几乎是立刻便领到了他的纸和笔，此刻正坐着，右手执笔搁在纸上、作势欲书，左手放在膝上，身子微微从中央偏开一些。

劳里细细地端详着他。……

这时，他开始察觉到，自己所注视的那张脸上，起了某一种说不清道不明的变化。眼睛是睁着的——不，变化并不在眼睛里，也不在他所能看见的嘴角那些纹路里，也不在任何一个细节里、任何一处。那也不是一张梦游者或夜游者的脸，也不是死者的脸——死者脸上线条消失、生机退去。这是一张活生生的、有意识的脸，然而它确是变了。双唇微微分开，呼吸均匀地从唇间通过。这更像是一张沉迷于深邃、专注、内省之思的脸。劳里断定这便是其中的缘故。

他看了看搁在纸上的那只手——形状匀称，肤色微褐，干练有力——一动不动，拇指与食指之间轻轻夹着那支铅笔。

然后他抬头瞥了一眼两位女士。

她俩也一动不动，可脸上并无变化。两人的眼睑都垂着，稳稳地盯着那张纸。就在他看着的当儿，他看见劳拉夫人开始缓缓掀起眼皮，像是想瞟他一眼。他便自己也去看那张纸、去看那几根不动的手指。

局势发展之速令他惊异。五分钟之前，他还在听着人说话，自己也还在搭着腔。那位牧师还在这里；他自己坐的位置还比现在靠后一码。此刻他们却坐在这里，仿佛已经坐了一个钟头。事情的推进仿佛已停滞了。……

接着，他开始去倾听外间世界的声响，因为这里头，仿佛有一种质地极奇特的寂静忽然降临了下来，把他们笼罩住了。仿佛有一截——他所坐着的这一处——从时间与空间里被切了出来。这里是别处的，完全是另一个天地。……

===Original===
He began to be intensely and minutely conscious of the world outside—so entirely conscious that he lost all perception of that at which he stared; whether it was the paper, or the strong, motionless hand, or the introspective face, he was afterwards unaware. But he heard all the quiet roar of the London evening, and was able to distinguish even the note of each instrument that helped to make up that untiring, inconclusive orchestra. Far away to the northwards sounded a great thoroughfare, the rolling of wheels, a myriad hoofs, the pulse of motor vehicles, and the cries of street boys; upon all these his attention dwelt as they came up through the outward windows into that dead silent, lamplit room of which he had lost consciousness. Again a hansom came up the street, with the rap of hoofs, the swish of a whip, the wintry jingle of bells.…

He began gently to consider these things, to perceive, rather than to form, little inward pictures of what they signified; he saw the lighted omnibus, the little swirl of faces round a news-board.

Then he began to consider what had brought him here; it seemed that he saw himself, coming in his dark suit across the park, turning into the thoroughfare and across it. He began to consider Amy; and it seemed to him that in this intense and living silence he was conscious of her for the first time without sorrow since ten days ago. He began to consider.

===Chinese===
他开始极为强烈、极为细致地意识到外间的那个世界——意识得那样彻底，以至于他对所凝视之物已全无所感；到底是那张纸，还是那只结实而不动的手，还是那张内省的脸，他事后竟全说不上来。可他却听见了伦敦黄昏那一片低沉的、平静的喧腾，甚至能分辨出汇成这支不倦的、无终无始的管弦乐的、每一件乐器的音色。远远地，北边传来一条通衢大道的声音——车轮的辚辚，无数蹄声，汽车的脉动，还有街头男孩的叫喊；他的注意力便落在这一切之上，看它们如何穿过外层的窗户、涌进这一间他已失去了意识的、死寂的、灯火通明的屋子。又一辆双轮马车从街上驶过，蹄声得得，鞭梢一响，冬日里叮当的铃声。……

他开始轻轻地去思量这些东西，与其说是构想、不如说是觉察到一幅幅小小的内里画面，描画出它们所意味着的什么；他看见了灯火通明的公共马车，看见了一群面孔绕着一块报牌打转的小小的旋涡。

然后他开始思量是什么把他带到了这里；他仿佛看见了自己，穿着深色衣裳穿过公园，拐进那条大街，又穿过去。他开始思量艾米；而在这浓烈的、活的寂静之中，他觉着自己竟是十日以来头一回、没有悲伤地意识到了她。他开始思量。

---

===Original===
Something brought him back in an instant to the room and his perception of it, but he had not an idea what this was, whether a movement or a sound. But on considering it afterwards he remembered that it was as that sound is that wakes a man at the very instant of his falling asleep, a sharp momentary tick, as of a clock. Yet he had not been in the least sleepy.

On the contrary, he perceived now with an extreme and alert attention the hand on the paper; he even turned his head slightly to see if the pencil had moved. It was as motionless as at the beginning. He glanced up, with a touch of surprise, at his hostess’s face, and caught her in the very act of turning her eyes from his. There was no impatience in her movement: rather her face was of one absorbed, listening intently, not like the bearded face opposite, introspective and intuitive, but eagerly, though motionlessly, observant of the objective world. He looked at Mrs. Stapleton. She too bore the same expression of intent regarding thought on her usually rather tiresome face.

Then once again the silence began to come down, like a long, noiseless hush.

This time, however, his progress was swifter and more sure. He passed with the speed of thought through those processes that had been measurable before, faintly conscious of the words spoken before the sitting began—

“… If possible, the silence of thought.”

He thought he understood now what this signified, and that he was experiencing it. No longer did he dwell upon, or consider, with any voluntary activity, the images that passed before him. Rather they moved past him while he simply regarded them without understanding. His perception ran swiftly outwards, as through concentric circles, yet he was not sure whether it were outwards or inwards that he went. The roar of London, with its flight of ocular visions, sank behind him, and without any further sense of mental travel, he found himself perceiving his own home, whether in memory, imagination, or fact he did not know. But he perceived his mother, in the familiar lamplit room, over her needlework, and Maggie—Maggie looking at him with a strange, almost terrified expression in her great eyes. Then these too were gone; and he was out in some warm silence, filled with a single presence—that which he desired; and there he stopped.

===Chinese===
不知什么东西，一瞬之间便把他拽回了这间屋子、拽回了对这屋子的知觉，可他却一点也说不上来那是什么，是一个动作，还是一声响。可事后回想起来，他记得那一声，正如同人在刚刚入睡的那一刹那、把他唤醒的那一声——一声短促的、一闪即逝的滴答，像钟表的声音。可他方才一点也不困。

恰恰相反，此刻他以一种极其警醒的专注，察觉到了搁在纸上的那只手；他甚至微微侧过头去，看看铅笔动过没有。它仍像起初那样一动不动。他带着一丝惊讶抬头去看女主人的脸，正撞见她把视线从他身上移开的那一刹那。她的动作里并无不耐：倒不如说，那是一张沉迷其中的脸，正凝神倾听，不像对面那张蓄着胡子的脸——那是内省的、凭直觉的——她这是在热切地、虽则一动不动地、观察着外在世界。他去看斯台普顿太太。她那张平日颇叫人厌烦的脸上，此刻也是这副凝神注思的神情。

于是那寂静再一次降临下来，如同一道悠长的、无声的屏息。

然而这一次，他行进得更快、更稳。他以思想的速度，穿过方才还可计量的那些进程，隐约还听见降神会开始之前说过的那句话——

「……可能的话，心也静下来。」

他想他如今懂得这话的意思了，而且他正在经历它。那些在他眼前掠过的景象，他已不再以任何自主的动作品味它、思忖它。倒不如说，是它们从他面前走过，而他只是看着它们，不解地看着。他的知觉飞快地向外奔去，仿佛穿过一圈又一圈同心圆，可他也说不准自己奔的到底是向外还是向内。伦敦的喧腾，连同它那一群飞掠的目中景象，沉到了他身后；他不再有任何心思上跋涉的感觉，便发觉自己正感知着自己的家——是在记忆里、想象里，还是当真如此，他全不知道。可他确实感知到了他的母亲，在那间熟悉的灯火通明的屋子里，低头做着针线；还有玛吉——玛吉正用她那双大眼睛望着他，眼里是一种古怪的、近乎惊恐的神情。接着，这些也消逝了；于是他置身于某一片温暖的寂静里，其中充盈着唯一的一种临在——他所渴望的那一种；他便在那里停了下来。

---

===Original===
He was not in the least aware of how long this lasted. But he found himself at a certain moment in time, looking steadily at the white paper on the table, from which the hand had gone, again conscious of the sudden passing of some clear sound that left no echo—as sharp as the crack of a whip. Oh! the paper—that was the important point! He bent a little closer, and was aware of a sharp disappointment as he saw it was stainless of writing. Then he was astonished that the hand and pencil had gone from it, and looked up quickly.

Mr. Vincent was looking at him with a strange expression.

At first he thought he might have interrupted, and wondered with dismay whether this were so. But there was no sign of anger in those eyes—nothing but a curious and kindly interest.

“Nothing happened?” he exclaimed hastily. “You have written nothing?”

He looked at the ladies.

Lady Laura too was looking at him with the same strange interest as the medium. Mrs. Stapleton, he noticed, was just folding up, in an unobtrusive manner, several sheets of paper that he had not noticed before.

He felt a little stiff, and moved as if to stand up; but, to his astonishment, the big man was up in an instant, laying his hands on his shoulders.

“Just sit still quietly for a few minutes,” said the kindly voice. “Just sit still.”

“Why—why—” began Laurie, bewildered.

“Yes, just sit still quietly,” went on the voice; “you feel a little tired.”

“Just a little,” said Laurie. “But—”

“Yes, yes; just sit still. No; don’t speak.”

Then a silence fell again.

===Chinese===
这事延续了多久，他全无所知。可是在某个时辰上，他发觉自己正目不转睛地盯着桌上那张白纸——那只手已经离开了它——又一次意识到某一记清晰、却不留回声的响声刚刚掠过——如鞭梢一甩那样锐利。啊！那张纸——这才是要紧的关键！他把身子略略凑近，一瞥之下，发觉上面竟未着只字，一阵锐利的失望便涌了上来。继而他又诧异于那只手和那支铅笔竟已离开了纸面，便赶紧抬起头来。

文森特先生正以一种古怪的神情望着他。

起初他以为自己打断了人家，惶恐地琢磨着是否当真如此。可那双眼睛里并无怒意——唯有某种好奇而和善的关注。

「什么也没发生么？」他急急地嚷道，「您什么也没写下？」

他望向两位女士。

劳拉夫人也正以与灵媒同样的、古怪的关注望着他。他注意到，斯台普顿太太正不露声色地折起几张他方才并未留意的纸。

他觉得身子有些发僵，动了一动，像是想站起来；可出乎他意料，那大块头一转眼便站了起来，两手按住了他的肩头。

「就这么安安静静地再坐几分钟，」那和善的嗓音说道，「就这么坐着。」

「怎么——怎么——」劳里发蒙地开口。

「是的，就这么安安静静地坐着，」那嗓音接着说，「你觉得有点累。」

「有一点，」劳里说，「可——」

「是的，是的；就这么坐着。不；别说话。」

接着，又一次沉寂落了下来。

===Original===
Laurie began to wonder what this was all about. Certainly he felt tired, yet strangely elated. But he felt no inclination to move; and sat back, passive, looking at his own hands on his knees. But he was disappointed that nothing had happened.

Then the thought of time came into his mind. He supposed that it would be about ten minutes past six. The sitting had begun a little before six. He glanced up at the clock on the mantelpiece; but it was one of those bulgy-faced Empire gilt affairs that display everything except the hour. He still waited a moment, feeling all this to be very unusual and unconventional. Why should he sit here like an invalid, and why should these three sit here and watch him so closely?

He shifted a little in his chair, feeling that an effort was due from him. The question of the time of day struck him as a suitably conventional remark with which to break the embarrassing silence.

“What is the time?” he said. “I am afraid I ought to be—”

“There is plenty of time,” said the grave voice across the table.

With a sudden movement Laurie was on his feet, peering at the clock, knowing that something was wrong somewhere. Then he turned to the company bewildered and suspicious.

“Why, it is nearly eight,” he cried.

Mr. Vincent smiled reassuringly.

“It is about that,” he said. “Please sit down again, Mr. Baxter.”

“But—but—” began Laurie.

“Please sit down again, Mr. Baxter,” repeated the voice, with a touch of imperiousness that there was no resisting.

Laurie sat down again; but he was alert, suspicious, and intensely puzzled.

===Chinese===
劳里开始纳闷这究竟是怎么一回事。他确是觉得累了，却又奇异地振奋。可他一点不想动弹；便向后靠着，被动的，看着自己搁在膝上的双手。可他失望于什么也没发生。

接着，时间这个念头钻进了他的脑子。他估摸着此刻该是六点十来分。降神会是六点不到一点开始的。他抬头看了看壁炉架上的那只钟；可那是帝国式的、鼓着腮帮子的、镀金的玩意儿，这种钟什么都显示，唯独不显示钟点。他又等了一会儿，只觉这一切极为不寻常、极不合常规。他凭什么像个病人似的坐在这里，而这三位又凭什么坐在这里这样紧紧地盯着他？

他在椅子里挪了一挪，觉得该自己出一点力了。问一问时辰，在他看来正是打破这尴尬沉默的一句妥当得体的套话。

「几点了？」他说，「我怕是我该——」

「时间有的是，」桌对面那个沉肃的嗓音说。

劳里一个突然的动作便站了起来，眯眼去看那只钟，心里知道总有哪一处不对劲。接着他回过身来，又困惑又疑心地望着众人。

「怎么，都快八点了。」他叫道。

文森特先生宽慰地笑了笑。

「差不多吧，」他说，「请再坐下吧，巴克斯特先生。」

「可——可——」劳里开口。

「请再坐下吧，巴克斯特先生，」那嗓音重复道，带上一丝不容违拗的专横。

劳里又坐下了；可他这会儿机警、多疑，又困惑极了。

===Original===
“Will you kindly tell me what has happened?” he asked sharply.

“You feel tired?”

“No; I am all right. Kindly tell me what has happened.”

He saw Lady Laura whisper something in an undertone he could not hear. Mr. Vincent stood up with a nod and leaned himself against the mantelpiece, looking down at the rather indignant young man.

“Certainly,” he said. “You are sure you are not exhausted, Mr. Baxter?”

“Not in the least,” said Laurie.

“Well, then, you passed into trance about five minutes—”

“*What?*”

“You passed into trance about five minutes past six; you came out of it five minutes ago.”

“Trance?” gasped Laurie.

“Certainly. A very deep and satisfactory trance. There is nothing to be frightened of, Mr. Baxter. It is an unusual gift, that is all. I have seldom seen a more satisfactory instance. May I ask you a question or two, sir?”

Laurie nodded vaguely. He was still trying hopelessly to take in what had been said.

“You nearly passed into trance a little earlier. May I ask whether you heard or saw anything that recalled you?”

Laurie shut his eyes tight in an effort to think. He felt dimly rather proud of himself.

“It was quite short. Then you came back and looked at Lady Laura. Try to remember.”

“I remember thinking I had heard a sound.”

The medium nodded.

“Just so,” he said.

“That would be the third,” said Lady Laura, nodding sagely.

“Third what?” said Laurie rather rudely.

No one paid any attention to him.

===Chinese===
「可否劳驾告诉我，发生了什么事？」他尖锐地问。

「你觉得累么？」

「不；我没事。劳驾告诉我，发生了什么事。」

他看见劳拉夫人低声不知耳语了一句什么，他听不见。文森特先生点点头，站起身来，倚在壁炉架上，低头打量着这个颇为愤然的年轻人。

「当然可以，」他说，「你确实不累么，巴克斯特先生？」

「一点也不，」劳里说。

「那么，你在六点五分前后便进入了通灵状态（trance）——」

「*什么？*」

「你在六点五分前后进入了通灵状态；你是五分钟前才出来的。」

「通灵状态？」劳里倒抽一口气。

「当然。一次极深、极圆满的通灵状态。没什么好怕的，巴克斯特先生。这是一种不寻常的天赋，如此而已。我难得见到比这更圆满的实例。可否容我问你一两个问题，先生？」

劳里茫然地点了点头。他仍在绝望地努力消化方才所听到的话。

「你在稍早一点的时候，几乎就要进入通灵状态了。可否问一句，你是否听见、或看见了什么把你唤回来的东西？」

劳里紧紧闭上眼，使劲回想。他隐隐觉得自己还挺有点得意的。

「那一下很短。后来你回过神来，看了劳拉夫人一眼。试着记一记。」

「我记得自己以为听见了一声响。」

灵媒点了点头。

「正是，」他说。

「那便是第三次了，」劳拉夫人煞有介事地点着头说。

「第三次什么？」劳里颇为粗鲁地问。

谁也没有理会他。

===Original===
“Now can you give any account of the last hour and a half?” continued the medium tranquilly.

Laurie considered again. He was still a little confused.

“I remember thinking about the streets,” he said, “and then of my own home, and then…” He stopped.

“Yes; and then?”

“Then of a certain private matter.”

“Ah! We must not pry then. But can you answer one question more? Was it connected with any person who has crossed over?”

“It was,” said Laurie shortly.

“Just so,” said the medium.

Laurie felt suspicious.

“Why do you ask that?” he said.

Mr. Vincent looked at him steadily.

“I think I had better tell you, Mr. Baxter; it is more straightforward, though you will not like it. You will be surprised to hear that you talked very considerably during this hour and a half; and from all that you said I should suppose you were controlled by a spirit recently crossed over—a young girl who on being questioned gave the name of Amy Nugent—”

Laurie sprang to his feet, furious.

“You have been spying, sir. How dare you—”

“Sit down, Mr. Baxter, or you shall not hear a word more,” rang out the imperious, unruffled voice. “Sit down this instant.”

Laurie shot a look at the two ladies. Then he remembered himself. He sat down.

===Chinese===
「那么，你能就这一个半钟头说上点什么么？」灵媒平静地接着说。

劳里又想了想。他仍有些糊涂。

「我记得自己想着那些街道，」他说，「然后又想我自己的家，然后……」他停住了。

「嗯；然后呢？」

「然后想了一桩私事。」

「啊！那我们便不得探问了。可你还能再答一个问题么？那可跟某个已经过去、到了那边的人有关？」

「是，」劳里简短地说。

「正是，」灵媒说。

劳里觉出疑心来了。

「你为何问这个？」他说。

文森特先生定定地看着他。

「我想我最好还是告诉你，巴克斯特先生；这样更坦白，虽说你不爱听。你听了怕是要吃惊的——在这一个半钟头里，你说了一大堆话；而据你所说的一切，我揣度你乃是被一位新近过去的亡灵所控制——一个年轻的姑娘，经盘问之下，自报姓名叫艾米·纽金特（Amy Nugent）——」

劳里勃然大怒，一跃而起。

「你这是在刺探，先生。你怎么敢——」

「坐下，巴克斯特先生，否则你一个字也别想再听，」那专横而不乱的嗓音响了出来，「马上坐下。」

劳里狠狠瞥了那两位女士一眼。接着他想起了自己。他坐了下来。

===Original===
“I am not at all angry, Mr. Baxter,” came the voice, suave and kindly again. “Your thought was very natural. But I think I can prove to you that you are mistaken.”

Mr. Vincent glanced at Mrs. Stapleton with an almost imperceptible frown, then back at Laurie.

“Let me see, Mr. Baxter.… Is there anyone on earth besides yourself who knew that you had sat out, about ten days ago or so, under some yew trees in your garden at home, and thought of this young girl—that you—”

Laurie looked at him in dumb dismay; some little sound broke from his mouth.

“Well, is that enough, Mr. Baxter?”

Lady Laura slid in a sentence here.

“Dear Mr. Baxter, you need not be in the least alarmed. All that has passed here is, of course, as sacred as in the confessional. We should not dream, without your leave—”

“One moment,” gasped the boy.

He drove his face into his hands and sat overwhelmed.

Presently he looked up.

“But *I* knew it,” he said. “*I* knew it. It was just my own self which spoke.”

===Chinese===
「我一点也不生气，巴克斯特先生，」那嗓音又变得温文而和善了，「你的想法很自然。可我想我能向你证明，是你弄错了。」

文森特先生几乎觉察不出地皱了皱眉，看了斯台普顿太太一眼，又回头来看劳里。

「让我想想看，巴克斯特先生。……普天之下，除了你自己之外，可还有第二个人知道，大约十天之前，你曾在自家花园的几棵紫杉树下坐着、想着这位姑娘——你——」

劳里哑然失措地望着他；嘴里漏出一点含混的声响。

「那么，这够不够，巴克斯特先生？」

劳拉夫人在这儿插进了一句。

「亲爱的巴克斯特先生，你一点儿也不必惊慌。这里所经过的一切，当然，如同在告解（confession）中一样神圣。没有你的准许，我们做梦也不会——」

「且慢，」少年喘着气说。

他把脸埋进双手里，颓然坐着，被压倒了。

过了一会儿他抬起头来。

「可*我*自己是知道的，」他说，「*我*知道。方才说话的，不过是我自己罢了。」

===Original===
The medium smiled.

“Yes,” he said, “of course that is the first answer.” He placed one hand on the table, leaning forward, and began to play his fingers as if on a piano. Laurie watched the movement, which seemed vaguely familiar.

“Can you account for that, Mr. Baxter? You did that several times. It seemed uncharacteristic of you, somehow.”

Laurie looked at him, mute. He remembered now. He half raised a hand in protest.

“And… and do you ever stammer?” went on the man.

Still Laurie was silent. It was beyond belief or imagination.

“Now if those things were characteristic—”

“Stop, sir,” cried the boy; and then, “But those too might be unconscious imitation.”

“They might,” said the other. “But then we had the advantage of watching you. And there were other things.”

“I beg your pardon?”

“There was the loud continuous rapping, at the beginning and the end. You were awakened twice by these.”

Laurie remained perfectly motionless without a word. He was still striving to marshal this flood of mad ideas. It was incredible, amazing.

Then he stood up.

“I must go away,” he said. “I—I don’t know what to think.”

“You had better stay a little longer and rest,” said the medium kindly.

The boy shook his head.

“I must go at once,” he said. “I cannot trust myself.”

===Chinese===
灵媒微微一笑。

「不错，」他说，「这当然是头一个答案。」他把一只手搁在桌上，倾着身子，开始用手指弹动着，仿佛在弹一架钢琴。劳里瞧着这动作，觉着依稀有些眼熟。

「你可解释得了这个么，巴克斯特先生？你做了好几次。这看上去，总不大像你的作风。」

劳里哑口无言地看着他。他这会儿想起来了。他半抬起一只手，像是要抗议。

「还有……还有，你平时可结巴么？」那人接着说。

劳里仍不作声。这已超乎了相信、超乎了想象。

「倘若这些恰是你的作风——」

「住口，先生，」少年叫道；继而又说，「可这些，也说不定是无意识的摹仿。」

「也说不定，」那一位说，「可这一回，我们占了就近看着你的便宜。况且还有别的。」

「你说什么？」

「还有那一阵阵响亮的、连续不断的叩击声，一在开头，一在末了。你这两回都是被它惊醒的。」

劳里一动不动，一言不发。他仍在努力整顿这一股疯狂念头的洪流。这真叫人难以置信、匪夷所思。

接着他站起身来。

「我得走了，」他说，「我——我不知道该作何想法。」

「你最好再多待一会儿，歇一歇，」灵媒和善地说。

少年摇了摇头。

「我非得马上走不可，」他说，「我信不过我自己。」

===Original===
He went out without a word, followed by the medium. The two ladies sat eyeing one another.

“It has been astonishing… astonishing,” sighed Mrs. Stapleton. “What a find!”

There was no more said. Lady Laura sat as one in trance herself.

Then Mr. Vincent returned.

“You must not lose sight of that young man,” he said abruptly. “It is an extraordinary case.”

“I have all the notes here,” remarked Mrs. Stapleton.

“Yes; you had better keep them. He must not see them at present.”

===Chinese===
他一言不发地走了出去，灵媒跟着送他出来。两位女士坐着，面面相觑。

「这真是惊人……惊人，」斯台普顿太太叹道，「真是个不得了的发现！」

再没别的说话。劳拉夫人坐在那里，自己也如同入了通灵状态一般。

接着文森特先生回来了。

「你们万不可跟丢了这年轻人，」他开门见山地说，「这是个非同寻常的病例。」

「我所有的笔记都在这里，」斯台普顿太太说。

「不错；你且好好收着。眼下还不能给他看见。」

## V / V

### I / I

===Original===
As the weeks went by Maggie’s faint uneasiness disappeared. She was one of those fortunate persons who, possessing what are known as nerves, are aware of the possession, and discount their effects accordingly.
