There, in the cabinet, somewhere overhead behind the curtain, a faint but perfectly distinct radiance was visible. It was no more than a diffused glimmer, but it was unmistakable, and it shone out faintly and clearly upon the medium’s face. By its light Laurie could make out every line and every feature, the drooping clipped moustache, the strong jutting nose, the lines from nostril to mouth, and the closed eyes. As he watched the light deepened in intensity, seeming to concentrate itself in the hidden corner at the top. Then, with a smooth, steady motion it emerged into full sight, in appearance like a softly luminous globe of a pale bluish color, undefined at the edges, floating steadily forward with a motion like that of an air balloon, out into the room. Once outside the cabinet it seemed to hesitate, hanging at about the height of a man’s head—then, after an instant, it retired once more, reentered the cabinet, disappeared in the direction from which it had come, and once more died out.

Well, there it had been; there was no doubt about it.… And Laurie was unacquainted with any mechanism that could produce it.

The clergyman too had seemed affected. He had watched, with turned-back head, the phenomenon from beginning to end, and at the close, with a long indrawing of breath, had looked once at Laurie, licked his dry lips with a motion that was audible in that profound silence, and once more dropped his eyes. The ladies had been silent, and all but motionless throughout.

Well, the rest had happened comparatively quickly.

===Chinese===
只见柜子里、帘子后面、头顶上方的某处，有一团微弱却十分分明的亮光。那不过是一团弥散的微光，却错不了，淡淡而清晰地照在灵媒的脸上。借着它的光，劳里能辨出每一条纹路、每一处五官：那剪得齐齐的、垂下来的髭，那高耸而突出的鼻梁，从鼻翼到嘴角的那几道纹路，还有那双闭着的眼睛。他注视着，那光愈来愈亮，仿佛在顶部那个隐没的角落里凝聚起来。随后，它以一种平稳而沉静的动作整个地浮现在人眼前，形如一只柔和发光的淡蓝色圆球，边缘朦胧，像一只气球那样平稳地向前飘进屋里来。一出柜子，它似乎犹豫了一下，悬在大约与人齐头的高度——然后，过了一瞬，它又退了回去，重入柜中，朝着它出来的方向隐去，再度熄灭了。

好啦，它是确确实实在那里的；这一点无可怀疑。……而劳里想不出哪一种机关能弄出它来。

那牧师似乎也受了影响。他仰着头，从头到尾看完了这一幕，到末了，长长吸了一口气，看了劳里一眼，在那深沉的寂静里，用听得见的动作舔了舔发干的嘴唇，又把眼睛垂了下去。两位太太始终不作声，也几乎一动不动。

接下来的一切，发生得就相当快了。

---

===Original===
Once more, after the lapse of a few minutes, the radiance had begun to reform; but this time it had emerged almost immediately, diffused and misty like a nebula; had hung again before the cabinet, and then, with a strange, gently whirling motion, had seemed to arrange itself in lines and curves.

Gradually, as he stared at it, it had begun to take the shape and semblance of a head, swathed in drapery, with that same drapery, hanging, as it appeared in folds, dripping downwards to the ground, where it lost itself in vagueness. Then, as he still stared, conscious of nothing but the amazing fact, features appeared to be forming—first blots and lines as of shadow, finally eyes, nose, mouth, and chin as of a young girl.…

A moment later there was no longer a doubt. It was the face of Amy Nugent that was looking at him, grave and steady—as when he had seen it in the moonlight above the sluice—and behind, seen half through the strange drapery, and half apart from it, a couple of feet behind, the face of the sleeping medium.

At that sight he had not moved nor spoken. It was enough that the fact was there. Every power he possessed was concentrated in the one effort of observation.…

He heard from somewhere a gasping sigh, and there rose up between him and the face the figure of the clergyman, with his head turned back staring at the apparition, and one hand only on the table, yet with that hand so heavy upon it that the whole table shuddered with his shudder.

There was a movement on the left, and he heard a fierce feminine whisper—

“Sit down, sir; sit down this instant.…

When the clergyman had again sunk down into his seat with that same strong shudder, the luminous face was already incoherent; the features had relapsed again into blots and shadows, the drapery was absorbing itself upwards into the center from which it came. Once more the nebula trembled, moved backwards, and disappeared. The next instant the radiance went out, as if turned off by a switch. The medium groaned gently and awoke.

Well, that had ended it. Laurie scarcely remembered the talking that followed, the explanations, the apologies, the hardly concealed terror of the young clergyman. The medium had come out presently, dazed and confused. They had talked… and so forth. Then Laurie had come home, still trying to assimilate the amazing fact, of which he said that it could make no difference—that he had seen with his own eyes the face of Amy Nugent four months after her death.

Now here he was in bed on the following morning, trying to assimilate it once more.

===Chinese===
过了几分钟，那亮光又开始重新凝聚；这一回却几乎立刻便现了出来，弥散而朦胧，像一团星云；又在柜前悬住，随即以一种古怪的、轻柔的盘旋动作，似乎自行排成了一道道线与弧。

他盯着它看，渐渐地，它开始现出一个头的形状与模样来，裹在帷帐之中，那同一片帷帐又仿佛叠成一道道褶子向下垂坠，滴落般地一直拖到地上，在那里没入朦胧。然后，他还在盯着，除了这一桩惊人的事实之外什么也不觉得，五官似乎正在成形——起初是些阴影似的斑点和线条，末了是眼睛、鼻子、嘴巴和下巴，是一个少女的五官。……

过了一下，再无疑义了。望着他的，正是艾米·纽金特（Amy Nugent）的脸，端庄而沉静——一如他在月光下的水闸上方所见过的那样——而在它后面，半透过那奇异的帷帐、半在帷帐之外，相隔两英尺开外，是那熟睡的灵媒的脸。

见到这一幕，他一动也没动，一言也没发。事实既在那里，便够了。他所拥有的一切力量都凝聚到观看这一桩努力上去了。……

他从某处听见一声喘息般的叹息，随即在他与那张脸之间，升起了牧师的身影——他仰着头瞪着那幻影，只有一只手撑在桌上，然而那只手压得那样重，整张桌子都随他的颤抖而颤抖起来。

左边有了动静，他听见一声凌厉的女人低语——

「坐下，先生；马上坐下。……

待那牧师带着同样强烈的颤抖重新坐回位子上，那张发光的脸已经散乱了；五官又塌回成斑点和阴影，帷帐正向着它所由来的中央自下而上地收回。星云再一次颤动、后退、消失了。下一瞬，亮光熄灭，仿佛被开关关掉了一样。灵媒轻轻哼了一声，醒了过来。

好啦，这一来便了结了。其后那一番谈话——种种解释、种种道歉、那年轻牧师几乎掩饰不住的恐惧——劳里几乎都记不得了。灵媒随即走了出来，神色茫然而困惑。他们谈了一阵……如此种种。其后劳里便回了家，仍在试着消化那桩惊人的事实，嘴上却说它不会带来什么不同——他亲眼看见了艾米·纽金特死后四个月的脸。

如今，次晨，他便躺在这里，再次试着消化它。

---

===Original===
It seemed to him as if sleep had done its work—that the subconscious intelligence had been able to take the fact in—and that henceforth it was an established thing in his experience. He was not excited now, but he was intensely and overwhelmingly interested. There the thing was. Now what difference did it make?

First, he understood that it made an enormous difference to the value of the most ordinary things. It really was true—as true as tables and chairs—that there was a life after this, and that personality survived. Never again could he doubt that for one instant, even in the gloomiest mood. So long as a man walks by faith, by the acceptance of authority, human or divine, there is always psychologically possible the assertion of self, the instinct that what one has not personally experienced may just conceivably be untrue. But when one has seen—so long as memory does not disappear—this agnostic instinct is an impossibility. Every single act therefore has a new significance. There is no venture about it any more; there is, indeed, very little opportunity for heroism. Once it is certain, by the evidence of the senses, that death is just an interlude, this life becomes merely part of a long process.…

Now as to the conduct of that life—what of religion? And here, for a moment or two, Laurie was genuinely dismayed. For, as he looked at the Catholic religion, he perceived that the whole thing had changed. It no longer seemed august and dominant. As he contemplated himself as he had been at Mass on the previous morning, he seemed to have been rather absurd. Why all this trouble, all this energy, all these innumerable acts and efforts of faith? It was not that his religion seemed necessarily untrue; it was certainly possible for a man to hold simultaneously Catholic and Spiritualistic beliefs; there had not been a hint last night against Christianity, and yet, in the face of this evidence of the senses, Catholicism seemed a very shadowy thing. It might well be true, as any philosophy may be true, but—did it matter very much? To be enthusiastic about it was the frenzy of an artist, who loves the portrait more than the original—and possibly a very misleading and inadequate portrait. Laurie had seen for himself the original last night; he had seen a disembodied soul in a garb assumed for the purpose of identification.… Did he need, then, a “religion?” Was not his experience all-sufficing?…

===Chinese===
他只觉得，仿佛睡眠已经做完了它的工作——那潜意识的智慧已经能把这事实吞纳下去——从此它便成了他经验中立定了的一桩事。他如今并不激动，却极其、压倒一切地被它攫住了。事情就在那里。那么，它带来了什么不同呢？

首先，他明白了，它叫那些最寻常不过的事物的价值，都天翻地覆地变了样。原来竟是真的——真得如同桌子和椅子一样——这一世之外还有一世，人格是延续不灭的。从此他再也不能怀疑这个了，一瞬也不能，即便在最阴郁的心境下也不能。一个人只要还凭信心行事，只要还接受着人或神的权威，那么从心理上讲，自我的主张、那种「自己未曾亲历的事也许终究可以算作不真」的本能，便始终是可能的。可一旦亲眼见了——只要记忆不曾消失——这种不可知的本能便无从谈起。因此，每一桩单个的行为都有了新的意义。这其中再无冒险可言；的确，连英雄气概的用武之地都已所剩无几。一旦凭感官的证据断定了死亡不过是一段插曲，此生便无非是一段漫长过程的区区一节罢了。……

那么，关于这一世的度过——宗教又如何呢？这一处，劳里一时半刻是当真惊惶了的。因为，当他打量天主教这一信仰时，他发觉整个事情都变了。它不再显得庄严而权威。他回想昨日早晨自己在弥撒（Mass）中的模样，竟觉得自己未免可笑。何必这般周折、这般尽心、这般数不清的信仰的行为和努力呢？倒不是说他的宗教似乎必定不真；一个人同时持守天主教与通灵术的信仰，这原是可能的；昨夜也并无半句针对基督教的微词；然而，在这感官的证据面前，天主教竟似乎成了一桩极缥缈的东西。它尽可以是真的，正如任何哲学尽可以是真的一样，可是——它要紧得很么？为它狂热，无异于一个艺术家的癫狂，爱画像胜过爱本人——而且那画像说不定还是一幅极易引人误入歧途、又极不到家的画像。劳里昨夜亲眼见到了本人；他见到了一具脱离了肉身的灵魂，披着为了让人辨认而临时披上的衣裳。……那么，他还需要一种「宗教」么？他这一番经历，难道不是已然足够？……

---

===Original===
Then suddenly all speculation fled away in the presence of the personal element.

Three days ago he had contemplated the thought of Amy with comparative indifference. She had been to him lately little more than a “test case” of the spiritual world, clothed about with the memory of sentiment. Now once more she sprang into vivid vital life as a person. She was not lost; his relations with her were not just incidents of the past; they were as much bound up with the present as courtship has a continuity with married life. She existed—her very self—and communication was possible between them.…

Laurie rolled over on to his back. The thought was violently overwhelming; there was a furious, absorbing fascination in it. The gulf had been bridged; it could be bridged again. Even if tales were true, it could be bridged far more securely yet. It was possible that the phantom he had seen could be brought yet more forward into the world of sense, that he could touch again with his very hand a tabernacle enclosing her soul. So far Spiritualism had not failed him; why should he suspect it of failure in the future? It had been done before; it could, and should, be done again. Besides, there was the pencil incident.…

He threw off the clothes and sprang out of bed. It was time to get up; time to begin again this fascinating, absorbingly interesting earthly life, which now had such enormous possibilities.

===Chinese===
随后，骤然之间，一切玄想都在那「人」的元素面前飞散了。

三天之前，他想起艾米时，心境还算得上淡然。近来她于他，已不过是灵界的一桩「试验例」，外面裹着一层情感的回忆罢了。如今她又作为一个「人」，跃入了鲜活的、有血有肉的生命。她并未失落；他与她的种种牵连，并不只是过去的几桩事端；它们与现在的关系，正像求亲与婚后生活原是一脉相承。她存在着——就是她自己——而他们之间的交通，是可能的。……

劳里翻过身来仰卧着。这一个念头来势猛烈，压倒一切；其中有一种狂暴的、叫人全身心被吸住的蛊惑。那道鸿沟已经架起了桥梁；它还能再架。便算那些传闻是真的，这桥梁还能架得牢上加固。他所见到的那个幻影，说不定还能被更往前带到感官的世界里来，说不定他还能用自己的手，再度触到一个安放着她的灵魂的圣龛。通灵术至今未曾辜负过他；他又何必怀疑它将来会辜负？这等事从前有人做过；它能再被做成，也理当再被做成。况且，还有那回铅笔的事呢。……

他一把掀开被窝，跳下了床。该起来了；该重新开始这迷人而叫人全神贯注的尘世生活了，如今它已有了这般无穷的可能。

### II / II

===Original===
The rooms of Mr. James Morton were conveniently situated up four flights of stairs in one of those blocks of buildings, so mysterious to the layman, that lie not a very long way from Charing Cross. There is a silence always here as of college life, and the place is frequented by the same curious selections from the human race as haunt university courts. Here are to be seen cooks, aged and dignified men, errand boys, and rather shabby old women.

The interior of the rooms, too, is not unlike that of an ordinary rather second-rate college; and Mr. James Morton’s taste did not redeem the chambers in which he sat. From roof to floor the particular apartment in which he sat was lined with bookshelves filled with unprepossessing volumes and large black tin boxes. A large table stood in the middle of the room, littered with papers, with bulwarks of the same kind of tin boxes rising at either end.

Mr. Morton himself was a square-built man of some forty years, clean-shaven, and rather pale and stout, with strongly marked features, a good loud voice, and the pleasant, brusque manners that befit a university and public school man who has taken seriously to business.

Laurie and he got on excellently together. The younger man had an admiration for the older, whose reputation as a rather distinguished barrister certainly deserved it, and was sufficiently in awe of him to pay attention to his directions in all matters connected with law. But they did not meet much on other planes. Laurie had asked the other down to Stantons once, and had dined with him three or four times in return. And there their acquaintance found its limitations.

===Chinese===
詹姆斯·莫顿先生（Mr. James Morton）的寓所，方便地坐落在查令十字街（Charing Cross）不远处的某一幢大楼里，沿楼梯爬上四层便是——那一种大楼对外行人而言颇为神秘。这里总有一种如学院生活般的寂静，出没于此的，也是常常流连于大学庭院的那同一类古怪的人群。这里看得见厨子、上了年纪而气派十足的男子、跑腿的小厮，还有颇有些寒酸的老妇。

屋子里的格局，也同一所寻常而略为二流的学院差不多；而詹姆斯·莫顿先生的趣味，也并未给他所坐的这间屋子添几分光彩。他所坐的这间屋子，从顶到地都嵌满了书架，架上塞满了其貌不扬的卷帙和一只只大号的黑色铁皮箱。屋子正中摆着一张大桌，堆满了文件，两头各筑起一道由同样的铁皮箱垒成的屏障。

莫顿先生本人是个四旬上下、身量敦实的男子，脸刮得干净，面色略白、略胖，五官轮廓深刻，嗓门洪亮，举止爽利而讨人喜欢，正合一个认真做起生意来的、出身大学与公学的人的做派。

劳里和他相处得极好。年轻的一位钦佩年长的一位——后者作为一位颇有名望的律师，这份钦佩也当之无愧——又对之敬畏有加，凡关涉法律的事宜都肯留心听从他的指点。可他们在别的层面上并无多少来往。劳里请他到斯坦顿家（the Stantons）去过一回，作为回报也回请过他三四次饭。他们的交情便到此为止了。

---

===Original===
This morning, however, the boy’s interested air, with its hints of suppressed excitement and his marked inattention to the books and papers which were his business, at last caused the older man to make a remark. It was in his best manner.

“What’s the matter, eh?” he suddenly shot at him, without prelude of any kind.

Laurie’s attention came back with a jump, and he flushed a little.

“Oh!—er—nothing particular,” he murmured. And he set himself down to his books again in silence, conscious of the watchful roving eye on the other side of the table.

About half-past twelve Mr. Morton shut his own book with a slap, leaned back, and began to fill his pipe.

“Nothing seems very important,” he said.

As the last uttered word had been spoken an hour previously, Laurie was bewildered, and looked it.

“It won’t do, Baxter,” went on the other. “You haven’t turned a page an hour this morning.”

Laurie smiled doubtfully, and leaned back too. Then he had a spasm of confidence.

“Yes. I’m rather upset this morning,” he said. “The fact is, last night…”

Mr. Morton waited.

“Well?” he said. “Oh! don’t tell if me you don’t want to.”

Laurie looked at him.

“I wonder what you’d say,” he said at last.

The other got up with an abrupt movement, pushed his books together, selected a hat, and put it on.

“I’m going to lunch,” he said. “Got to be in the Courts at two; and.…”

“Oh! wait a minute,” said Laurie. “I think I want to tell you.”

“Well, make haste.” He stood, in attitude to go.

“What do you think of Spiritualism?”

“Blasted rot,” said Mr. Morton. “Anything more I can do for you?”

“Do you know anything about it?”

“No. Don’t want to. Is that all?”

“Well, look here,” said Laurie.… “Oh! sit down for two minutes.”

===Chinese===
然而今天早晨，少年那副兴致冲冲、隐约透着压抑不住的激动的神气，以及他对那些本该是他正经的书籍文件那一望而知的漫不经心，到底叫年长的那位开了口。那是他最拿手的做派。

「怎么了，嗯？」他毫无铺垫地朝他突然射过来一句。

劳里的注意力猛地收了回来，脸微微一红。

「哦！——呃——没什么要紧的。」他含含糊糊地说。便又埋头对着书本默不作声，却感觉得到桌子那头那双警觉而游移的眼。

约莫十二点半，莫顿先生「啪」地合上自己的书，往后一靠，开始装他的烟斗。

「什么都好像不大要紧似的。」他说。

这一句最后一个词，他一个钟头之前刚说过，劳里被弄得摸不着头脑，脸上也现出这般神情。

「这可不行啊，巴克斯特，」那一位接着说，「今儿一上午你一页书都没翻过。」

劳里讪讪地笑了笑，也往后一靠。接着他起了一阵倾吐的冲动。

「是啊。我这会儿心绪不大好，」他说，「实不相瞒，昨夜……」

莫顿先生等着。

「怎么？」他说，「哦！不想说就别说。」

劳里看着他。

「我不知道你会怎么说。」他末了说。

那一位猛地一起身，把书往一处一推，挑出一顶帽子戴上。

「我去吃午饭了，」他说，「两点得到法院；而且。……

「哦！等一下，」劳里说，「我想我还是得告诉你。」

「那就快点。」他站着，一副要走的样子。

「你对通灵术怎么看？」

「一堆狗屁不通的鬼话，」莫顿先生说，「还有别的要我效劳么？」

「你懂它么？」

「不懂。也不想懂。完了么？」

「那么，你听我说，」劳里说。……「哦！坐下两分钟。」

---

===Original===
Then he began. He described carefully his experiences of the night before, explaining so much as was necessary of antecedent events. The other during the course of it tilted his hat back, and half leaned, half sat against a side-table, watching the boy at first with a genial contempt, and finally with the same curious interest that one gives to a man with a new disease.

“Now, what d’you make of that?” ended Laurie, flushed and superb.

“D’you want to know?” came after a short silence.

Laurie nodded.

“What I said at the beginning, then.”

“What?”

“Blasted rot,” said Mr. Morton again.

Laurie frowned sharply, and affected to put his books together.

“Of course, if you take it like that,” he said. “But I don’t know what respect you can possibly have for any evidence, if.…”

“My dear chap, that isn’t evidence. No evidence in the world could make me believe that the earth was upside down. These things don’t happen.”

“Then how do you explain…?”

“I don’t explain,” said Mr. Morton. “The thing’s simply not worth looking into. If you really saw that, you’re either mad or else there was a trick.… Now come along to lunch.”

“But I’m not the only one,” cried Laurie hotly.

“No, indeed you’re not.… Look here, Baxter, that sort of thing plays the devil with nerves. Just drop it once and for all. I knew a chap once who went in for all that. Well, the end was what everybody knew would happen.…

“Yes?” said Laurie.

“Went off his chump,” said the other briefly. “Nasty mess all over the floor. Now come to lunch.”

“Wait a second. You can’t argue from particulars to universals. Was he the only one you ever knew?”

The other paused a moment.

“No,” he said. “As it happens, he wasn’t. I knew another chap—he’s a solicitor.… Oh! by the way, he’s one of your people—a Catholic, I mean.”

“Well, what about him?”

“Oh! he’s all right,” admitted Mr. Morton, with a grudging air. “But he gave it up and took to religion instead.”

“Yes? What’s his name?”

“Cathcart.”

He glanced up at the clock.

“Good Lord,” he said, “ten to one.”

Then he was gone.

===Chinese===
于是他开了口。他细细地讲了昨夜的经过，又把前因后果必要的那一些解释了一番。对方听着，把帽子往后一推，半倚半坐在一张小桌边，起初是带着一种和善的轻蔑看这少年，到末了则带着人们看一个得了新病的人那种好奇的兴趣。

「那么，你怎么看？」劳里讲完了，脸涨红着，一副理直气壮的样子。

「你想知道么？」沉默了一阵之后，对方问道。

劳里点了点头。

「那就是我开头那句话了。」

「哪句？」

「一堆狗屁不通的鬼话，」莫顿先生又说了一遍。

劳里眉头一皱，装出整理书本的样子。

「当然啦，你要是这么个看法，」他说，「可我不知道你还能对什么证据存几分敬意，如果……」

「我亲爱的伙计，那不是证据。世上没有任何证据能叫我相信地球是倒着转的。这种事根本不会发生。」

「那你又怎么解释……？」

「我不解释，」莫顿先生说，「这玩意儿压根不值得探究。你要真是看见了那东西，你要么是疯了，要么就是有人做了手脚。……好了，吃饭去。」

「可看见的不止我一个，」劳里急了。

「是啊，确实不止你一个。……听我说，巴克斯特，那种玩意儿最伤神经。趁早一刀两断吧。我从前认识一个家伙，也好上这一口。唉，结局嘛，是人人料得到的那一桩。……

「哪一桩？」劳里说。

「疯了，」对方简短地说，「脑浆涂了一地，恶心得很。好了，吃饭去。」

「等一下。你不能以偏概全。那是你认识的唯一一个么？」

对方顿了一下。

「不，」他说，「巧得很，不是。我还认识另一个——他是个律师。……哦！说起来，他还是你们那边的人——我是说，是个天主教徒。」

「哦？他怎么样？」

「哦！他没事，」莫顿先生勉强承认道，带着一股吝惜的神气，「可他丢开了那一套，改而信起了教。」

「是么？他叫什么名字？」

「卡特卡特（Cathcart）。」

他抬眼瞧了瞧钟。

「老天，」他说，「差十分一点了。」

说完他就走了。

---

===Original===
Laurie was far too exalted to be much depressed by this counsel’s opinion; and had, indeed, several minutes of delightful meditation on the crass complacency of a clever man when taken off his ground. It was deplorable, he said to himself, that men should be so content with their limitations. But it was always the way, he reflected. To be a specialist in one point involved the pruning of all growth on every other. Here was Morton, almost in the front rank of his particular subject, and, besides, very far from being a bookworm; yet, when taken an inch out of his rut, he could do nothing but flounder. He wondered what Morton would make of these things if he saw them himself.

In the course of the afternoon Morton himself turned up again. The case had ended unexpectedly soon. Laurie waited till the closing of the shutters offered an opportunity for a break in the work, and once more returned to the charge.

“Morton,” he said, “I wish you’d come with me one day.”

The other looked up.

“Eh?”

“To see for yourself what I told you.”

Mr. Morton snorted abruptly.

“Lord!” he said, “I thought we’d done with that. No, thank you: Egyptian Hall’s all I need.”

Laurie sighed elaborately.

“Oh! of course, if you won’t face facts, one can’t expect.…

“Look here, Baxter,” said the other almost kindly, “I advise you to give this up. It plays the very devil with nerves, as I told you. Why, you’re as jumpy as a cat yourself. And it isn’t worth it. If there was anything in it, why it would be another thing, but.…

“I… I wouldn’t give it up for all the world,” stammered Laurie in his zeal. “You simply don’t know what you’re talking about. Why… why, I’m not a fool… I know that. And do you think I’m ass enough to be taken in by a trick? And as if a trick could be played like that in a drawing room! I tell you I examined every inch.…

“Look here,” said Morton, looking curiously at the boy—for there was something rather impressive about Laurie’s manner—“look here; you’d better see old Cathcart. Know him?… Well, I’ll introduce you any time. He’ll tell you another tale. Of course, I don’t believe all the rot he talks; but, at any rate, he’s sensible enough to have given it all up. Says he wouldn’t touch it with a pole. And he was rather a big bug at it in his time, I believe.”

===Chinese===
劳里此刻志得意满得很，这位律师的高见并不怎样叫他泄气；他倒反而津津有味地冥想了几分钟，想到一个聪明人一旦被挪出自己那一亩三分地，竟会这般自满得可笑。他说服自己：人竟这般安于自家的局限，实在可叹。可历来都是这样的，他想。要在一点上做个专家，便须把别处的一切枝蔓都修剪净尽。眼前这位莫顿，在自己那一门学问里几乎要算首屈一指了，况且也远非书呆子一个；然而，一旦把他挪出老路哪怕一寸，他便只能手忙脚乱地乱扑腾。他不禁好奇，倘若莫顿亲眼看见这些东西，又当如何。

到了下午，莫顿自己又出现了。那桩案子出乎意料地早早了结。劳里等到关百叶窗的间隙——这给了他一个停一停的空当——便又卷土重来。

「莫顿，」他说，「我盼着你哪天能跟我去一趟。」

那一位抬起头来。

「嗯？」

「好亲眼瞧瞧我对你说过的那些。」

莫顿先生陡然嗤了一声。

「主啊！」他说，「我还以为这事儿已经完了呢。不，谢了：埃及厅（Egyptian Hall）那点儿就够我受的了。」

劳里夸张地叹了一口气。

「哦！当然了，你要是不肯面对事实，那也不能指望……」

「听我说，巴克斯特，」那一位几乎带着几分和气地说，「我劝你把这玩意儿丢开吧。我跟你说过，它最伤神经。瞧瞧你自己，神经绷得像只猫似的。再说，它不值当。要是这里头真有什么名堂，那又是一回事，可……」

「我……我拿全世界来换也不肯丢开，」劳里一腔热切，结结巴巴地说，「你根本不知道自己在说什么。为什么……为什么，我不是个傻瓜……这个我知道。你以为我会蠢到被一手把戏蒙了去么？再说了，在起居室里哪能那样玩把戏！我告诉你，我每一寸地方都察看过。……

「听我说，」莫顿说，好奇地看着这少年——因为劳里的神情里有点颇能打动人的东西——「听我说；你还是去见见老卡特卡特吧。认识他么？……好吧，我什么时候给你引见一下。他会给你讲另一番话。当然，他讲的那些鬼话我也并不全信；可不管怎么说，他总算明白事理，把这一切都丢开了。他说他连拿长竿子去碰都不肯。当年在那行里，他倒也算得上一个有头有脸的人物，我相信。」

---

===Original===
Laurie sneered audibly.

“Got frightened, I suppose,” he said. “Of course, I know well enough that it’s rather startling—”

“My dear man, he was in the thick of it for ten years. I’ll acknowledge his stories are hair-raising, if one believed them; but then, you see—”

“What’s his address?”

Morton jerked his head towards the directories in the bookshelf.

“Find him there,” he said. “I’ll give you an introduction if you need it. Though, mind you, I think he talks as much rot as anyone—”

“What does he say?”

“Lord!—I don’t know. Some theory or other. But, at any rate, he’s given it up.”

Laurie pursed his lips.

“I daresay I’ll ask you some time,” he said. “Meanwhile—”

“Meanwhile, for the Lord’s sake, get on with that business you’ve got there.”

===Chinese===
劳里出声地冷笑了一下。

「想必是被吓坏了吧，」他说，「当然，我十分清楚那的确颇为骇人——」

「我亲爱的伙计，他在那潭浑水里泡了整整十年。我承认，他讲的那些故事要是信的话，叫人毛骨悚然；可是你瞧——」

「他住哪儿？」

莫顿朝书架上那些姓名录摆了摆头。

「在那上头找得到，」他说，「你要是想要，我给你写封引见信。不过，跟你说清楚，我觉得他讲的那些鬼话也不比别人少——」

「他都怎么说？」

「主啊！——我不知道。什么理论之类的吧。可不管怎么说，他到底把它丢开了。」

劳里抿了抿嘴。

「改日我也许会请你引见，」他说，「眼下么——」

「眼下，看在老天的分上，把你手头那点活儿干了吧。」

---

===Original===
Mr. Morton was indeed, as Laurie had reflected, extraordinarily uninterested in things outside his beat; and his beat was not a very extended one. He was a quite admirable barrister, competent, alert, merciless and kindly at the proper times, and, while at his business, thought of hardly anything else at all. And when he was not at his business, he threw himself with equal zest into two or three other occupations—golf, dining out, and the collection of a particular kind of chairs. Beyond these things there was for him really nothing of value.

But, owing to circumstances, his beat had been further extended to include Laurie Baxter, whom he was beginning to like extremely. There was an air of romance about Laurie, a pleasant enthusiasm, excellent manners, and a rather delightful faculty of hero-worship. Mr. Morton himself, too, while possessing nothing even resembling a religion, was, like many other people, not altogether unattracted towards those who had, though he thought religiousness to be a sign of a slightly incompetent character; and he rather liked Laurie’s Catholicism, such as it was. It must be rather pleasant, he considered (when he considered it at all), to believe “all that,” as he would have said.

So this new phase of Laurie’s interested him far more than he would have allowed, so soon as he became aware that it was not merely superficial; and, indeed, Laurie’s constant return to the subject, as well as his air of enthusiastic conviction, soon convinced him that this was so.

Further, after a week or two, he became aware that the young man’s work was suffering; and he heard from his lips the expression of certain views that seemed to the elder man extremely unhealthy.

For example, on a Friday evening, not much afterwards, as Laurie was putting his books together, Mr. Morton asked him where he was going to spend the weekend.

“Stopping in town,” said the boy briefly.

“Oh! I’m going to my brother’s cottage. Care to come? Afraid there’s no Catholic church near.”

Laurie smiled.

“That wouldn’t deter me,” he said. “I’ve made up my mind—”

“Yes?”

“Oh, it doesn’t matter,” said Laurie. “No—thanks awfully, but I’ve got to stop in town.”

“Lady Laura’s again?”

“Yes.”

“Same old game?”

===Chinese===
莫顿先生诚如劳里所想，对自家那一亩三分地以外的事物，兴趣寡淡得不同寻常；而他那块地盘也并不怎么大。他是一位颇可钦佩的律师，胜任、机警、该无情时无情、该宽厚时宽厚，做起正经来，几乎别的一概不想。而一旦放下了正经，他便以同等的兴头扑到另外两三桩营生上去——打高尔夫、赴宴，以及收藏某一种特别的椅子。除了这些，世间于他便再无什么真正要紧的东西了。

然而因着种种缘由，他的地盘又被扩大，把劳里·巴克斯特也圈了进来，他正开始极其喜欢这少年。劳里身上有一股浪漫气，有一种讨人喜欢的热忱，举止出众，又有一种颇可爱的、肯崇拜英雄的本领。莫顿先生自己呢，虽说连一样略似宗教的东西都不曾有过，却和许多人一样，并非全然不被那些有宗教的人所吸引——尽管他把有宗教倾向视作性格略欠干练的一种表征；他倒颇喜欢劳里那种天主教——就其所有而言。能信「那一整套」（他会这么说的），他想来必定是颇惬意的事（在他偶尔想到的时候）。

是以劳里这一番新的状态，等他发觉并非只是浮在表面之后，便远比他肯承认的更引起了他的兴趣；而劳里三番五次回到这个话题上，加上那副热切地信以为真的神情，不久便叫他确信了这一点。

再者，一两周之后，他发觉这年轻人的功课受了影响；又从他嘴里听见了几句看法，在这位长者听来极不康健。

比如，不多日之后的一个星期五傍晚，劳里正收拾书本，莫顿先生问他周末打算上哪儿过。

「留在城里，」少年简短地说。

「哦！我要去我兄弟的乡舍。愿不愿同去？怕的是附近没有天主教堂。」

劳里微微一笑。

「那挡不住我，」他说，「我已打定主意——」

「什么？」

「哦，没什么，」劳里说，「不——多谢了，可我得留在城里。」

「又是劳拉夫人那儿？」

「是的。」

「又是那一套老把戏？」

---

===Original===
Laurie sat down.

“Look here,” he said, “I know you don’t mean anything; but I wish you’d understand.”

“Well?”

The boy’s face flushed with sudden nervous enthusiasm.

“Do you understand,” he said, “that this is just everything to me? Do you know it’s beginning to seem to me just the only thing that matters? I’m quite aware that you think it all the most utter bunkum; but, you see, I *know* it’s true. And the whole thing is just like heaven opening.… Look here…I didn’t tell you half the other day. The fact is, that I was just as much in love with this girl as—as a man could be. She died; and now—”

“Look here, what were you up to last Sunday?”

Laurie quieted a little.

“You wouldn’t understand,” he said.

“Have you done any more of that business?”

“What business?”

“Well—thinking you saw her—All right, seeing her, if you like.”

The boy shook his head.

“No. Vincent’s away in Ireland. We’ve been going on other lines.”

“Tell me; I swear I won’t laugh.”

“All right; I don’t care if you do.… Well, automatic handwriting.”

“What’s that?”

Laurie hesitated.

“Well, I go into trance, you see, and—”

“Good Lord, what next?”

“And then this girl writes through my hand,” said Laurie deliberately, “when I’m unconscious. See?”

“I see you’re a damned young fool,” said Morton seriously.

“But if it’s all rot, as you think?”

“Of course it’s all rot! Do you think I believe for one instant—” He broke off. “And so’s a nervous breakdown all rot, isn’t it, and D.T.? They aren’t real snakes, you know.”

Laurie smiled in a superior manner.

“And you’re getting yourself absorbed in all this—”

Laurie looked at him with a sudden flash of fanaticism.

“I tell you,” he said, “that it’s all the world to me. And so would it be to you, if—”

“Oh, Lord! don’t become Salvation Army.… Seen Cathcart yet?”

“No. I haven’t the least wish to see Cathcart.”

===Chinese===
劳里坐了下来。

「听我说，」他说，「我知道你没恶意；可我盼着你能明白。」

「什么？」

少年的脸骤然因一阵神经质的热忱而涨红。

「你明不明白，」他说，「这对我而言便是一切？你知不知道，它在我眼里已开始显得是唯一要紧的事了？我十分清楚你把这一切当成最最不堪的胡说八道；可是你要知道，我*知道*它是真的。而这整件事，简直就像天门大开。……听我说……那天我没跟你讲上一半。实情是，我曾深爱这姑娘，深得——深得一个人所能爱的那样。她死了；而如今——」

「听我说，上星期天你干什么来着？」

劳里稍稍收敛了一点。

「你不会明白的。」他说。

「那一套你又来过没有？」

「哪一套？」

「呃——自以为看见了她——好啦，看见了她，随你怎么说。」

少年摇了摇头。

「没有。文森特先生去了爱尔兰（Ireland）。我们改走别的路子了。」

「说给我听；我发誓不笑。」

「好吧；你就是笑我也不在乎。……那么，是自动书写（automatic handwriting）。」

「那是什么？」

劳里迟疑了一下。

「呃，我进入了出神状态（trance），你瞧，然后——」

「老天爷，还有什么花样？」

「然后这姑娘就借我的手写起字来，」劳里一字一顿地说，「就在我神志不清的时候。明白么？」

「我明白你是个该死的傻小子，」莫顿正经地说。

「可要是像你想的那样，全是胡说八道呢？」

「当然是胡说八道！你以为我会有一瞬相信——」他停住了。「那么精神崩溃也是胡说八道了，是么？还有震颤性谵妄（D.T.）呢？你要知道，那些蛇并不是真的。」

劳里露出一副高高在上的微笑。

「而你竟把自己整个浸到这一切里去——」

劳里看着他，眼里忽地一闪，闪出一种狂热。

「我告诉你，」他说，「这于我就是整个世界。换了是你，也会一样，如果——」

「哦，主啊！可别变成救世军（Salvation Army）那一套。……见过卡特卡特了么？」

「没有。我一丝一毫也不愿意见卡特卡特。」

---

===Original===
Morton rose, put his pens in the drawer, locked it; slid half a dozen papers into a black tin box, locked that too, and went towards his coat and hat, all in silence.

As he went out he turned on the threshold.

“When’s that man coming back from Ireland?” he said.

“Who? Vincent? Oh! another month yet. We’re going to have another try when he comes.”

“Try? What at?”

“Materialization,” said Laurie. “That’s to say—”

“I don’t want to know what the foul thing means.”

He still paused, looking hard at the boy. Then he sniffed.

“A young fool,” he said. “I repeat it.… Lock up when you come.… Good night.”

===Chinese===
莫顿站起身，把钢笔放进抽屉，锁上；把六七份文件塞进一只黑色铁皮箱，也锁上，走向他的外衣和帽子，这一切都在沉默中做完。

出门时他在门槛上回过头来。

「那个人什么时候从爱尔兰回来？」他说。

「谁？文森特？哦！还有一个月呢。他一回来，我们打算再试一回。」

「试？试什么？」

「显形（materialization），」劳里说，「这就是说——」

「我不想晓得这肮脏玩意儿是什么意思。」

他仍站住，直直地看着这少年。接着他吸了吸鼻子。

「一个傻小子，」他说，「我再说一遍。……你走时把门锁上。……晚安。」

## X / X

### I / I

===Original===
Mrs. Baxter possessed one of the two secrets of serenity. The other need not be specified; but hers arose from the most pleasant and most human form of narrow-mindedness. As has been said before, when things did not fit with her own scheme, either they were not things, but only fancies of somebody inconsiderable, or else she resolutely disregarded them. She had an opportunity of testing her serenity on one day early in February.

She rose as usual at a fixed hour—eight o’clock—and when she was ready knelt down at her prie-dieu. This was quite an elaborate structure, far more elaborate than the devotions offered there. It was a very beautiful inlaid Florentine affair, and had a little shelf above it filled with a number of the little leather-bound books in which her soul delighted. She did not use these books very much; but she liked to see them there. It would not be decent to enter the sanctuary of Mrs. Baxter’s prayers; it is enough to say that they were not very long. Then she rose from her knees, left her large comfortable bedroom, redolent with soap and hot water, and came downstairs, a beautiful slender little figure in black lace veil and rich dress, through the sunlight of the staircase, into the dining room.

There she took up her letters and packets. They were not exciting. There was an unimportant note from a friend, a couple of bills, and a Bon Marché catalogue; and she scrutinized these through her spectacles, sitting by the fire. When she had done she noticed a letter lying by Maggie’s place, directed in a masculine hand. An instant later Maggie came in herself, in her hat and furs, a charming picture, fresh from the winter sunlight and air, and kissed her.

===Chinese===
宁静这门秘诀，世间共有两条，巴克斯特太太掌握的便是其中之一。另一条这里不必细说；而她这一条，却出自那一种最叫人舒坦、最富人情味的狭隘。如前所述，凡与她自家的那套安排合不拢的事，要么根本算不得一件事，不过是哪个无关紧要之人的胡思乱想，要么她便硬是视而不见。二月头上的一天，她便得了个机缘，好生检验一番她这份宁静。

她照旧在固定的时辰——八点——起身，梳洗停当，便在经台前跪下。这经台是件极考究的物事，比在那上头献出的祈祷还要考究得多。那是一具极美的佛罗伦萨镶嵌小台，上头有一只小搁架，摆满了那些令她灵魂欢喜的皮面小书。这些书她并不大翻；可她爱看它们摆在那儿。要闯进巴克斯特太太祷告的禁地去窥看，未免有失体统；这里只消说，那些祷告都不甚长，也就够了。于是她从膝上起身，离开那间散发着肥皂与热水气息的宽敞惬意的卧房，下了楼——黑蕾丝面纱，华贵的衣裙，一个秀丽纤弱的小小身形，穿过楼梯上的阳光，走进了饭厅。

在那里她拿起自己的信件和包裹。都无甚趣味：一位朋友写来一封无关紧要的短信，两三张账单，还有一本「平价市场」（Bon Marché）的货样册；她便坐在炉边，隔着眼镜一一端详。看完之后，她留意到玛吉座位旁搁着一封信，字迹是男子的手笔。过了一瞬玛吉本人走了进来，戴着帽子，裹着皮草，刚从冬日的阳光与寒风里进来，模样可爱极了，俯身吻了她。

===Original===
While Mrs. Baxter poured out tea she addressed a remark or two to the girl, but only got back those vague inattentive murmurs that are the sign of a distracted mind; and, looking up presently with a sense of injury, noticed that Maggie was reading her letter with extraordinary diligence.

“My dear, I am speaking to you,” said Mrs. Baxter, with an air of slightly humorous dignity.

“Er—I am sorry,” murmured Maggie, and continued reading.

Mrs. Baxter put out her hand for the Bon Marché catalogue in order to drive home her sense of injury, and met Maggie’s eyes, suddenly raised to meet her own, with a curious strained look in them.

“Darling, what is the matter?”

Maggie still stared at her a moment, as if questioning both herself and the other, and finally handed the letter across with an abrupt movement.

“Read it,” she said.

It was rather a business to read it. It involved spectacles, a pushing aside of a plate, and a slight turning to catch the light. Mrs. Baxter read it, and handed it back, making three or four times the sound written as “Tut.”

“The tiresome boy!” she said querulously, but without alarm.

“What are we to do? You see, Mr. Morton thinks we ought to do something. He mentions a Mr. Cathcart.”

===Chinese===
巴克斯特太太一边倒茶，一边对那姑娘说了一两句话，得到的回音却只是那一种含含糊糊、心不在焉的嗯嗯声——那正是心思另有所属的标记；过了一会儿，她带着几分被冒犯的意思抬起头来，只见玛吉正以非同寻常的专注读着那封信。

「亲爱的，我在跟你说话呢。」巴克斯特太太说，带着一丝诙谐的庄重神气。

「呃——对不住，」玛吉含糊地道，继续读着信。

巴克斯特太太伸手去够那本「平价市场」货样册，好坐实她那点被冒犯之意，却正撞见玛吉忽然抬起眼来迎向她——眼里有一种古怪而紧张的神情。

「好孩子，怎么啦？」

玛吉仍盯着她看了一会儿，仿佛既在拷问自己、也在拷问对方，末了猛地一动，把那封信递了过来。

「你看吧。」她说。

读这封信倒颇费一番手脚。得戴眼镜，得把盘子推到一边，还得稍微转个身去迎光。巴克斯特太太读罢，把它递还回去，嘴里「啧、啧、啧」地连响了三四下。

「这讨厌的孩子！」她发着牢骚道，却并无惊慌。

「我们该怎么办？你瞧，莫顿先生觉得我们总该做点什么。他提到一位卡特卡特先生（Mr. Cathcart）。」

===Original===
Mrs. Baxter reached out for the toast-rack.

“My dear, there’s nothing to be done. You know what Laurie is. It’ll only make him worse.”

Maggie looked at her uneasily.

“I wish we could do something,” she said.

“My dear, he’d have written to me—Mr. Morton, I mean—if Laurie had been really unwell. You see he only says he doesn’t attend to his work as he ought.”

Maggie took up the letter, put it carefully back into the envelope, and went on with breakfast. There was nothing more to be said just then.

But she was uneasy, and after breakfast went out into the garden, spud in hand, to think it all over, with the letter in her pocket.

Certainly the letter was not alarming per se, but per accidens—that is to say, taking into account who it was that had written, she was not so sure. She had met Mr. Morton but once, and had formed of him the kind of impression that a girl would form of such a man in the hours of a weekend—a brusque, ordinary kind of barrister without much imagination and a good deal of shrewd force. It was surely rather an extreme step for a man like this to write to a girl in such a condition of things, asking her to use her influence to dissuade Laurie from his present course of life. Plainly the man meant what he said; he had not written to Mrs. Baxter, as he explained in the letter, for fear of alarming her unduly, and, as he expressly said, there was nothing to be alarmed about. Yet he had written.

===Chinese===
巴克斯特太太伸手去够吐司架。

「亲爱的，没什么可做的。你知道劳里是什么脾气。那只会叫他更糟。」

玛吉不安地看着她。

「但愿我们能做点什么。」她说。

「亲爱的，他要真病了——我是说莫顿先生——早写信给我了。你瞧，他不过说劳里没把心思放在该放的正事上罢了。」

玛吉拿起信，仔细装回信封里，继续吃她的早餐。眼下再没什么可说的了。

可她心里不安，早饭一吃完便拿着小锄出了园子，揣着那封信，把这事从头到尾再想一遍。

诚然，这封信本身并不叫人惊慌，可它的来由却叫人惊慌——也就是说，把写信的是何人这一点算进来，她就不那么有把握了。她只见过莫顿先生（Mr. Morton）一面，对他的印象，也无非是一个姑娘在一个周末的几个钟头里会对这样一个男子形成的印象——一个生硬的、普普通通的出庭律师，没多少想象力，却有一股颇为精明的劲头。这样一个人，竟会在这样的情势下写信给一位姑娘，求她运用自己的影响去劝劳里改掉眼下的生活路数，这实在是个相当出格的举动。这男子显然说的是心里话；正如他在信里所解释，他没写信给巴克斯特太太，是怕她过分惊慌，而且他明明白白地说，实在没有什么可惊慌的。可他终究还是写了。

===Original===
Maggie stopped at the lower end of the orchard path, took out the letter, and read the last three or four sentences again:

Please forgive me if you think it was unnecessary to write. Of course I have no doubt whatever that the whole thing is nothing but nonsense; but even nonsense can have a bad effect, and Mr. Baxter seems to me to be far too much wrapped up in it. I enclose the address of a friend of mine in case you would care to write to him on the subject. He was once a Spiritualist, and is now a devout Catholic. He takes a view of it that I do not take; but at any rate his advice could do no harm. You can trust him to be absolutely discreet.

Believe me,

							Yours sincerely,


							**James Morton**

It really was very odd and unconventional; and Mr. Morton had not seemed at all an odd or unconventional person. He mentioned, too, a particular date, February 25, as the date by which the medium would have returned, and some sort of further effort was going to be made; but he did not attempt to explain this, nor did Maggie understand it. It only seemed to her rather sinister and unpleasant.

She turned over the page, and there was the address he had mentioned—a Mr. Cathcart. Surely he did not expect her to write to this stranger.…

===Chinese===
玛吉在果园小径的尽头停下脚步，掏出信来，把末尾那三四句又读了一遍：

若你以为我多此一举，还请见谅。我自然毫无疑义：这整桩事无非是胡闹罢了；可胡闹也会生出坏处来，而巴克斯特先生，在我看来，未免陷得太深了些。兹附上我一位朋友的地址，倘若你愿就此写信给他，不妨一用。他曾是个通灵术（Spiritualism）信奉者，如今则是一名虔信的天主教徒。他的看法与我的不同；但无论如何，他的忠告总不会有害。他为人绝对谨慎，你尽可放心。

相信我是，

你真诚的


**詹姆斯·莫顿（James Morton）**

这实在是十分古怪、十分不照常规的；而莫顿先生看上去全然不像个古怪或不照常规的人。他还提到一个具体的日期——二月 25 日——说那位灵媒（medium）届时将已回来，某种新的尝试也将展开；可他并未打算解释这一点，玛吉也不明白。她只觉得这事颇有些阴险、颇叫人不快。

她翻过那一页，那里写着他所提到的地址——一位卡特卡特先生。他总不见得指望她给这么个陌生人写信吧。……

===Original===
She walked up and down with her spud for another half-hour before she could come to any conclusion. Certainly she agreed with Mr. James Morton that the whole thing was nonsense; yet, further, that this nonsense was capable of doing a good deal of harm to an excitable person. Besides, Laurie obviously had a bad conscience about it, or he would have mentioned it.

She caught sight of Mrs. Baxter presently through the thick hedge, walking with her dainty, dignified step along the paths of the kitchen garden; and a certain impatience seized her at the sight. This boy’s mother was so annoyingly serene. Surely it was her business, rather than Maggie’s own, to look after Laurie; yet the girl knew perfectly well that if Laurie was left to his mother nothing at all would be done. Mrs. Baxter would deplore it all, of course, gently and tranquilly, in Laurie’s absence, and would, perhaps, if she were hard pressed, utter a feeble protest even in his presence; and that was absolutely all.…

“Maggie! Maggie!” came the gentle old voice, calling presently; and then to some unseen person, “Have you seen Miss Deronnais anywhere?”

Maggie put the letter in her pocket and hurried through from the orchard.

“Yes?” she said, with a half hope.

“Come in, my dear, and tell me what you think of those new teacups in the Bon Marché catalogue,” said the old lady. “There seem some beautiful new designs, and we want another set.”

Maggie bowed to the inevitable. But as they passed up the garden her resolution was precipitated.

“Can you let me go by twelve,” she said. “I rather want to see Father Mahon about something.”

“My dear, I shall not keep you three minutes,” protested the old lady.

And they went in to talk for an hour and three-quarters.

===Chinese===
她又拿着小锄来回走了半个钟头，才算得出个所以然来。她自然同意詹姆斯·莫顿先生的判断，整桩事无非是胡闹；可更进一步，这种胡闹对一个容易兴奋的人是颇能酿出祸害来的。况且，劳里对此分明心里有愧，否则他早该提起了。

过了一会儿，她透过厚厚的树篱望见巴克斯特太太，正迈着那精致而端庄的步子，在菜园的小径上走；一见这情形，她心里便涌起一股不耐。这少年的母亲，宁静得实在叫人生气。照管劳里，本该是做母亲的本分，而不是她玛吉的本分；可这姑娘心里雪亮：劳里若交给母亲，便什么也不会做。巴克斯特太太当然会哀叹这一切——在劳里背后，轻轻地、安安静静地哀叹；倘若被逼得紧了，也许当着劳里的面，也会发出一声有气无力的抗议；如此而已，绝对仅此而已。……

「玛吉！玛吉！」过了一会儿，传来老太太那柔和苍老的嗓音，唤着她；接着又对某个看不见的人说：「你可在哪儿见过德罗奈小姐？」

玛吉把信揣进口袋，匆匆穿过果园走出来。

「什么事？」她说，半带期冀。

「进来吧，亲爱的，跟我说说你对『平价市场』货样册里那些新茶杯的看法，」老太太说，「好像有几款漂亮的新花样，我们正好再添一打。」

玛吉只得向那无可奈何之事低头。可两人顺着园子往回走时，她的决心反倒落定了。

「你十二点能放我走么，」她说，「我有点事想见马洪神父（Father Mahon）。」

「亲爱的，我留你不打三分钟。」老太太分辩道。

于是她们进屋，一谈就是一个钟头又三刻。

### II / II

===Original===
Father Mahon was a conscientious priest. He said his Mass at eight o’clock; he breakfasted at nine; he performed certain devotions till half-past ten; read the paper till eleven, and theology till twelve. Then he considered himself at liberty to do what he liked till his dinner at one. (The rest of his day does not concern us just now.)

He, too, was looking round his garden this morning—a fine, solid figure of a man, in rather baggy trousers, short coat, and expansive waistcoat, with every button doing its duty. He too, like Mr. James Morton, had his beat, an even narrower one than the barrister’s, and even better trodden, for he never strayed off it at all, except for four short weeks in the summer, when he hurried across to Ireland and got up late, and went on picnics with other ecclesiastics in straw hats, and joined in cheerful songs in the evening. He was a priest, with perfectly defined duties, and of admirable punctuality and conscientiousness in doing them. He disliked the English quite extraordinarily; but his sense of duty was such that they never suspected it; and his flock of Saxons adored him as people only can adore a brisk, businesslike man with a large heart and peremptory ways, who is their guide and father, and is perfectly aware of it. His sermons consisted of cold-cut blocks of dogma taken perseveringly from sermon outlines and served up Sunday by Sunday with a sauce of a slight and delightful brogue. He could never have kindled the Thames, nor indeed any river at all, but he could bridge them with solid stones; and this is, perhaps, even more desirable.

===Chinese===
马洪神父是个尽职的本堂神父。他八点钟献弥撒（Mass）；九点钟吃早饭；做几样规定的灵修功课做到十点半；读报纸读到十一点；读神学读到十二点。然后他便算自己得了空闲，爱做什么便做什么，直到一点钟吃正餐。（他这一日余下的光景，此刻与我们无关。）

这天早晨，他也正在自家园子里四下转悠——身量魁伟结实，一条颇宽松的长裤，一件短外褂，一件宽大的马甲，每一颗扣子都克尽其职。他与詹姆斯·莫顿先生一样，也有自己的一条既定路线，比那律师的还要窄些，却踩得更实些，因为他从不越出这条路线一步——除了夏天短短四个星期，那时他匆匆赶去爱尔兰（Ireland），睡到日上三竿，戴着草帽与别的神职人员去野餐，晚上还参加欢快的合唱。他是个神父，职责界限一清二楚，做起事来又勤谨又按时，无可挑剔。他厌恨英国人厌恨到了极点；可他的责任心之大，竟使他们从不曾察觉这一点；于是他那群撒克逊羔羊反倒爱戴他，如同人们唯有爱戴一个爽利、干练、心宽而又行事不容置辩的人才可能爱戴得起来那样——他是他们的向导和父亲，而且对这一点一清二楚。他的讲道，是由从讲道提纲里一板一眼抠下来的、冷冰冰的教义方块所组成，每个礼拜日端将上来，浇上一层薄薄的、悦耳的爱尔兰土腔作调料。他绝无把泰晤士河点着的能力，其实点着任何一条河都做不到；可他能用结实的石头在河上搭起桥来；而这，也许倒更可取些。

===Original===
Maggie had begun by disliking him. She had thought him rather coarse and stupid; but she had changed her mind. He was not what may be called subtle; he had no patience at all with such things as scruples, nuances, and shades of tone and meaning; but if you put a plain question to him plainly, he gave you a plain answer, if he knew it; if not, he looked it up then and there; and that is always a relief in this intricate world. Maggie therefore did not bother him much; she went to him only on plain issues; and he respected and liked her accordingly.

“Good morning, my child,” he said in his loud, breezy voice, as he came in to find her in his hideous little sitting room. “I hope you don’t mind the smell of tobacco-smoke.”

The room indeed reeked; he had started a cigar, according to rule, as the clock struck twelve, and had left it just now upon a stump outside when his housekeeper had come to announce a visitor.

“Not in the least, thanks, father.… May I sit down? It’s rather a long business, I’m afraid.”

The priest pulled out an armchair covered with horsehair and an antimacassar.

“Sit down, my child.”

Then he sat down himself, opposite her, in his trousers at once tight and baggy, with his rather large boots cocked one over the other, and his genial red face smiling at her.

“Now then,” he said.

“It’s not about myself, father,” she began rather hurriedly. “It’s about Laurie Baxter. May I begin at the beginning?”

He nodded. He was not sorry to hear something about this boy, whom he didn’t like at all, but for whom he knew himself at least partly responsible. The English were bad enough, but English converts were indescribably trying; and Laurie had been on his mind lately, he scarcely knew why.

===Chinese===
玛吉起初是讨厌他的。她曾觉得他有些粗鲁、有些蠢；可她改了主意。他算不上所谓精微一派；他对良心上的过细、对种种细微差别、对语气与意义的层层色调，全无半点耐性；可你若把一个直截了当的问题直截了当地问他，他若知道，便给你一个直截了当的答复；若不知道，便当场现查；而这在一个繁复的世上，永远叫人如释重负。因此玛吉并不大去烦他；她去找他，只在直截了当的事上；而他也因此敬重她、喜欢她。

「早上好，我的孩子，」他嗓门洪亮、语气爽朗地说着，走进屋来，在他那间难看的小起居室里见到了她，「但愿你不嫌这烟味儿。」

屋里的确烟味扑鼻；按规矩，十二点钟一敲，他便点上一支雪茄，方才女管家来报有客时，他正把它撂在外头一个树桩上。

「一点也不，谢谢您，神父。……我可以坐下么？只怕这事说来话长。」

神父拉出一把蒙着马毛布、铺着椅背巾的圈手椅。

「坐下吧，我的孩子。」

于是他自己也坐了下来，与她对面，穿着那条既紧又松的长裤，两只颇大的靴子一只搭在另一只上，那张和气的红脸含笑望着她。

「好啦，」他说。

「不是为我自己来的，神父，」她颇有些急促地开口道，「是为劳里·巴克斯特。我可以从头说起么？」

他点点头。听到这少年的事他并不为难；这少年他一点都不喜欢，可他知道自家至少要担上一份干系。英国人本就够糟了，而皈依的英国人更叫人难以形容地费神；近来越发有个劳里在他心头压着，他自己也几乎说不上来为什么。

===Original===
Then Maggie began at the beginning, and told the whole thing, from Amy’s death down to Mr. Morton’s letter. He put a question or two to her during her story, looking at her with pressed lips, and finally put out his hand for the letter itself.

“Mrs. Baxter doesn’t know what I’ve come about,” said the girl. “You won’t give her a hint, will you, father?”

He nodded reassuringly to her, absorbed in the letter, and presently handed it back, with a large smile.

“He seems a sensible fellow,” he said.

“Ah! that’s what I wanted to ask you, father. I don’t know anything at all about Spiritualism. Is it—is it really all nonsense? Is there nothing in it at all?”

He laughed aloud.

“I don’t think you need be afraid,” he said. “Of course we know that souls don’t come back like that. They’re somewhere else.”

“Then it’s all fraud?”

“It’s practically all fraud,” he said, “but it’s very superstitious, and is forbidden by the Church.”

This was straight enough. It was at least a clear issue to begin to attack Laurie upon.

“Then—then that’s the evil of it?” she said. “There’s no real power underneath? That’s what Mr. Rymer said to Mrs. Baxter; and it’s what I’ve always thought myself.”

===Chinese===
于是玛吉从头说起，把整桩事讲了一遍——从艾米去世一直讲到莫顿先生的信。她讲述之间，他问了一两个问题，抿着嘴唇望着她，末了伸手把信本身要了过去。

「我为何而来，巴克斯特太太并不知道，」姑娘说，「神父，您不会向她露半句口风吧？」

他安慰地朝她点点头，全神贯注看着信，过了一会儿把它递还，呵呵地笑了起来。

「瞧着是个明白人。」他说。

「啊！这正是我想问您的，神父。通灵术这回事，我一无所知。这——这果真全是胡闹么？里头当真一点东西都没有么？」

他放声大笑。

「我看你不必害怕，」他说，「灵魂自然不会那样回来的。它们在别处。」

「那便全是骗术了？」

「几乎全是骗术，」他说，「可这也是十分迷信的事，为圣教会所禁。」

这话再直白不过。至少这是个清清楚楚的题目，好用来攻一攻劳里。

「那么——那么这就是它的害处了？」她说，「底下并无真实的权能？雷默先生（Mr. Rymer）对巴克斯特太太也是这么说的；我自己也一向这么想。」

===Original===
The priest’s face became theological.

“Let’s see what Sabetti says,” he said. “I fancy—”

He turned in his chair and fetched out a volume behind him.

“Here we are.…”

He ran his finger down the heavy paragraphs, turned a page or two, and began a running comment and translation: “‘Necromantia ex’… ‘Necromancy arising from invocation of the dead’.… Let’s see… yes, ‘Spiritism, or the consulting of spirits in order to know hidden things, especially that pertain to the future life, certainly is divination properly so called, and is… is full of even more impiety than is magnetism, or the use of turning tables. The reason is, as the Baltimore fathers testify, that such knowledge must necessarily be ascribed to Satanic intervention, since in no other manner can it be explained.’”

“Then—” began Maggie.

“One moment, my child.… Yes… just so. ‘Express divination.’… No, no. Ah! here we are, ‘Tacit divination… even if it is openly protested that no commerce with the Demon is intended, is per se grave sin; but it can sometimes be excused from mortal sin, on account of simplicity or ignorance or a lack of certain faith.’ You see, my child—” he set the book back in its place “—so far as it’s not fraud it’s diabolical. And that’s an end of it.”

“But do you think it’s not all fraud, then?” asked the girl, paling a little.

He laughed again, with a resonance that warmed her heart.

“I should pay just no attention to it all. Tell him, if you like, what I’ve said, and that it’s grave sin for him to play with it; but don’t get thinking that the devil’s in everything.”

===Chinese===
神父的脸变得神学化起来。

「我们瞧瞧萨贝蒂怎么说的，」他说，「我料想——」

他在椅子里转过身，从身后抽出一卷书。

「在这里头。……」

他用手指顺着那些沉甸甸的段落一行行往下划，翻过一两页，便边读边讲解边翻译起来：「『Necromantia ex』……『由招唤亡者而生之招魂术』。……让我瞧瞧……对，『通灵术（Spiritism），即为探知隐秘之事——尤其是关乎来世之事——而 consult 灵魂，诚属名副其实的占卜，且……且比磁力（magnetism），或转桌之术，更充满不敬。其缘由，如巴尔的摩教父们所证，盖因此类知识必当归诸撒殚的干预，舍此无从解释。』」

「那么——」玛吉开口。

「等一等，我的孩子。……是的……是这样。『明言的占卜。』……不对，不对。啊！在这里，『默许的占卜……即便明明声明无意与魔鬼交通，其本身亦为重罪；但有时可因单纯、无知或缺乏确定之信德，而免于死罪。』你瞧，我的孩子——」他把书放回原处——「凡非骗术之处，便是魔鬼之事。事情就是这样，再无别话可说。」

「那么您竟以为，这不全是骗术了？」姑娘问，脸稍稍白了一白。

他又笑了起来，那笑声的浑厚回响暖了她的心。

「我劝你全不必把这事故在心上。你若愿意，把我方才的话告诉他，告诉他玩弄这等事于他是重罪；可千万别去想什么魔鬼无处不在。」

===Original===
Maggie was puzzled.

“Then it’s not the devil?” she asked—“at least not in this case, you think?”

He smiled again reassuringly.

“I should suspect it was a clever trick,” he said. “I don’t think Master Laurie’s likely to get mixed up with the devil in that way. There’s plenty of easier ways than that.”

“Do you think I should write to Mr. Cathcart?”

“Just as you like. He’s a convert, isn’t he? I believe I’ve heard his name.”

“I think so.”

“Well, it wouldn’t do any harm; though I should suspect not much good.”

Maggie was silent.

“Just tell Master Laurie not to play tricks,” said the priest. “He’s got a good, sensible friend in Mr. Morton. I can see that. And don’t trouble your head too much about it, my child.”

---

===Chinese===
玛吉困惑了。

「那么并非魔鬼了？」她问——「至少这一桩事上，您这么想？」

他又安慰地笑了笑。

「我倒疑心这是机灵的把戏，」他说，「我不觉得劳里少爷这般模样会与魔鬼搅到一起。要比这容易的路子，多的是。」

「您看我可该给卡特卡特先生写信？」

「随你便。他是个皈依的人，是么？我好像听过这名字。」

「我也这么想。」

「嗯，写信也无害处；不过我看也不大会有什么用处。」

玛吉不作声。

「只消叫劳里少爷别玩把戏便罢，」神父说，「他在莫顿先生那里有个明白又通情达理的朋友，这一点我看得出来。至于你，孩子，别在这事上太费神。」

---

===Original===
When Maggie was gone, he went out to finish his cigar, and found to his pleasure that it was still alight, and after a puff or two it went very well.

He thought about his interview for a few minutes as he walked up and down, taking the bright winter air. It explained a good deal. He had begun to be a little anxious about this boy. It was not that Laurie had actually neglected his religion while at Stantons; he was always in his place at Mass on Sundays, and even, very occasionally, on weekdays as well. And he had had a Mass said for Amy Nugent. But even as far back as the beginning of the previous year, there had been an air about him not altogether reassuring.

Well, this at any rate was a small commentary on the present situation.…

(The priest stopped to look at some bulbs that were coming up in the bed beside him, and stooped, breathing heavily, to smooth the earth round one of them with a large finger.)

… And as for this Spiritualistic nonsense—of course the whole thing was a trick. Things did not happen like that. Of course the devil could do extraordinary things: or at any rate had been able to do them in the past; but as for Master Laurie Baxter—whose home was down there in the hamlet, and who had been at Oxford and was now reading law—as for the thought that this rather superior Saxon young man was in direct communication with Satan at the present time—well, that needed no comment but loud laughter.

Yet it was very unwholesome and unhealthy. That was the worst of these converts; they could not be content with the sober workaday facts of the Catholic creed. They must be always running after some novelty or other.… And it was mortal sin anyhow, if the sinner had the faintest idea—

A large dinner-bell pealed from the back door; and the priest went in to roast beef with Yorkshire pudding, apple dumplings, and a single glass of port-wine to end up with.

===Chinese===
玛吉走后，他出去抽完那支雪茄，叫人高兴的是它居然还点着，吸过一两口之后，便也十分顺畅。

他来回踱步，吸着冬日明亮清冽的空气，把方才那场谈话想了想。这倒是解开了不少疑团。他起先就已开始为这少年有点挂心。倒不是说劳里在斯坦顿家（the Stantons）时当真疏忽了他的教规；礼拜日的弥撒他总在自家位置上，甚至平日里偶尔也来。他还为艾米·纽金特献过一台弥撒。可甚至早到前年年初，他身上便有一种叫人不大放心的气数。

唉，无论如何，这倒也算眼下局面的一条小小注脚。……

（神父停下来瞧了瞧身旁花床里几棵正冒头的球根，弯下腰，喘着粗气，用一根粗大的手指把其中一棵四周的泥土抹平。）

……至于这通灵术的胡言乱语——整桩事当然是个把戏。事情断不会那样发生。魔鬼自然能做出非凡的事来：至少从前是做得到的；可要说劳里·巴克斯特少爷——他的家就在下面那个小村里，他上过牛津，如今正在读法律——要说这个颇自命不凡的撒克逊青年眼下正与撒殚直接交通着——这个嘛，除了放声大笑之外，无须任何别的评论。

可这到底是十分不康健、十分不干不净的。这些皈依的人，毛病就在这里：他们总不肯安于天主教信条里那些素朴的、日常的、干巴巴的事实。他们总得追逐这样那样的新奇。……无论如何，这总是死罪了，只要犯罪的人心里有半分明白——

后门那里响起了一只大饭铃的叮当声；神父便进去吃烤牛肉配约克郡布丁、苹果布丁，末了再喝上一小杯葡萄酒作结。

### III / III

===Original===
It was strange how Maggie felt steadied and encouraged in the presence of something at least resembling danger. So long as Laurie was merely tiresome and foolish, she distrusted herself, she made little rules and resolutions, and deliberately kept herself interiorly detached from him. But now that there was something definite to look to, her sensitiveness vanished.

As to what that something was, she did not trust herself to decide. Father Mahon had given her a point to work at—the fact that the thing, as a serious pursuit, was forbidden; as to what the reality behind was, whether indeed there were any reality at all, she did not allow herself to consider. Laurie was in a state of nerves sufficiently troublesome to bring a letter from his friend and guide; and he was in that state through playing tricks on forbidden ground; that was enough.

Her interview with Father Mahon precipitated her half-formed resolution; and after tea she went upstairs to write to Mr. Cathcart.

It was an unconventional thing to do, but she was sufficiently perturbed to disregard that drawback, and she wrote a very sensible letter, explaining first who she was; then, without any names being mentioned, she described her adopted brother’s position, and indicated his experiences: she occupied the last page in asking two or three questions, and begging for general advice.

---

===Chinese===
说来也怪，玛吉一旦置身于某种至少像是危险的东西面前，反倒镇定下来、振作起来。只要劳里不过是叫人难堪、叫人发蠢，她便信不过自己，便定下一条条小小的规矩与决心，刻意在内心里与他保持距离。可如今有了一桩明确的事可看住了，她的敏感便不见了。

至于那桩明确的事究竟是什么，她并不敢自作主张地下判断。马洪神父给了她一个可以下手之处——即：这东西若当作一项严肃的追求，是为教会所禁的；至于背后的实情是什么，是否当真有任何实情，她不许自己去考虑。劳里正处于一种够叫人头疼的神经状态，竟惹得他的朋友兼导师写来了信；而他之所以落到这步田地，是因为在禁地里玩把戏；这便够了。

她与马洪神父的会面，把她那半成的决心落了实；喝过茶，她便上楼去给卡特卡特先生写信。

这事不照常规，可她心里够乱的了，顾不得这一层不便；她写了一封很通情达理的信，先说明自己是谁；接着不点任何姓名，描述了她这位义兄的处境，并点出他的经历：她在末一页上问了两三个问题，恳求对方给予一些笼统的指教。

---

===Original===
Mrs. Baxter displayed some symptoms after dinner which the girl recognized well enough. They comprised a resolute avoidance of Laurie’s name, a funny stiff little air of dignity, and a touch of patronage. And the interpretation of these things was that the old lady did not wish the subject to be mentioned again, and that, interiorly, she was doing her best ignore and forget it. Maggie felt, again, vaguely comforted; it left her a freer hand.

---

===Chinese===
饭后巴克斯特太太表现出几样征候，这姑娘再清楚不过地读懂了。这些征候包括：刻意回避提起劳里的名字；一种滑稽的、僵硬的、带几分尊严的神气；还有一丝居高临下的意味。这些征候的含义是：老太太不愿这话题再被提起；而且在她内心，正竭力不去理它、忘掉它。玛吉又一次隐隐觉得安慰；这样她的手脚反倒更自由了。

---

===Original===
She lay awake a long time that night.

Her room was a little square one on the top of the stairs, above the smoking room where she had that odd scene with Laurie a month or so before, and looking out upon the yew walk that led to the orchard. It was a cheerful little place enough, papered in brown, hung all over with water colors, with her bed in one corner; and it looked a reassuring familiar kind of place in the firelight, as she lay open-eyed and thinking.

It was not that she was at all frightened; it was no more than a little natural anxiety; and half a dozen times in the hour or two that she lay thinking, she turned resolutely over in bed, dismissed the little pictures that her mind formed in spite of herself, and began to think of pleasant, sane subjects.

But the images recurred. They were no more than little vignettes—Laurie talking to a severe-looking tall man with a sardonic smile; Laurie having tea with Mrs. Stapleton; Laurie in an empty room, looking at a closed door.…

It was this last picture that recurred three or four times at the very instant that the girl was drowsing off into sleep; and it had therefore that particular vividness that characterizes the thoughts when the conscious attention is dormant. It had too a strangely perturbing effect upon her; and she could not imagine why.

===Chinese===
那天夜里，她睁着眼躺了很久。

她的房间是楼梯顶上一个方形的小间，就在约莫一个月前她与劳里发生那场古怪一幕的吸烟室的楼上，窗子正对着通往果园的那条紫杉步道。这是一间相当明快的小屋，糊着棕色的壁纸，墙上挂满了水彩画，她的床在屋角；她睁着眼躺在那里想着心事时，炉光照着它，那模样倒叫人安心，是熟识的那种地方。

倒不是她有什么害怕；那不过是一点自然的不安；在她躺着想心事的这一两个钟头里，足有五六回，她断然在床上翻个身，把头脑不由自主地浮现出的小小画面驱散，转而去想些愉快、清明的事。

可这些影像一再重现。它们不过是些小小的速写——劳里正与一个神情严肃、面带讥诮之笑的高个子男子说话；劳里正与斯台普顿太太（Mrs. Stapleton）一道喝茶；劳里在一间空屋子里，望着一扇关着的门。……

正是最后这一幅画面，在姑娘每次将睡未睡的当口反复浮现了三四回；因此它便带有清醒注意力沉睡之际、思绪所特有的那种鲜明。它对她也有一种古怪地搅扰人心的作用；她想不出是为什么。

===Original===
After the third return of it her sense of humor came to the rescue: it was too ridiculous, she said, to be alarmed at an empty room and Laurie’s back. Once more she turned on her side, away from the firelight, and resolved, if it recurred again, to examine the details closely.

Again the moments passed: thought followed thought, in those quiet waves that lull the mind towards sleep; finally once more the picture was there, clear and distinct.

Yes; she would look at it this time.

It was a bare room, wainscoted round the walls a few inches up, papered beyond in some common palish pattern. Laurie stood in the center of the uncarpeted boards, with his back turned to her, looking, it seemed, with an intense expectation at the very dull door in the wall opposite him. He was in his evening dress, she saw, knee-breeches and buckles all complete; and his hands were clenched, as they hung held out a little from his sides, as he himself, crouching a little, stared at the door.

She, too, looked at the door, at its conventional panels and its brass handle; and it appeared to her as if both he and she were expectant of some visitor. The door would open presently, she perceived; and the reason why Laurie was so intent upon the entrance, was that he, no more than she, had any idea as to the character of the person who was to come in. She became quite interested as she watched—it was a method she followed sometimes when wooing sleep—and she began, in her fancy, to go past Laurie as if to open the door. But as she passed him she was aware that he put out a hand to check her, as if to hold her back from some danger; and she stopped, hesitating, still looking, not at Laurie, but at the door.

===Chinese===
那画面第三次回来之后，她的幽默感赶来救场了：这也太可笑了，她对自己说，竟为一间空屋子和劳里的后背大惊小怪。她又向里侧过身去，避开炉光，并下决心：它若再来，便要把那细节看个仔细。

时刻又一刻地过去：一个念头接着一个念头，如那些把心神哄向睡眠的静静波澜；末了，那画面又来了，清晰而分明。

是的；这一回她要看看它。

那是一间光秃秃的屋子，沿墙脚几寸高处装着护墙板，板以上糊着某种俗气的、浅色的花纹纸。劳里站在没有铺地毯的木地板正中，背对着她，似乎正以一种极其专注的期待，望着对面墙上那扇乏味之极的门。她瞧见他穿着全套礼服，及膝马裤和带扣鞋一应俱全；他的双手攥着，从身侧稍稍向前伸出；他自己也微微弓着身子，凝视着那扇门。

她也望着那扇门，望着它那规规矩矩的门板和铜把手；在她看来，仿佛他跟她都在等着某位来客似的。她觉出那扇门马上就要打开了；而劳里之所以如此死死盯着入口，原因在于：要进来的是个什么样的人，他跟她一样，毫无所知。她瞧着瞧着，竟生出几分兴致——这是她有时哄自己入睡的法子——于是她在想象里越过劳里，仿佛要去开那扇门。可她经过他身边时，觉察到他伸出一只手来拦她，仿佛要把她从某种危险前挡回去；她便停下，踌躇着，依旧不望劳里，只望那扇门。

===Original===
She began then, with the irresponsibility of deepening sleep, to imagine instead what lay beyond the door—to perceive by intuitive vision the character of the house. She got so far as understanding that it was all as unfurnished as this room, that the house stood solitary among trees, and that even these, and the tangled garden that she determined must surround the house, were as listening and as expectant as herself and the waiting figure of the boy. Once more, as if to verify her semi-passive imaginative excursion, she moved to the door.…

Ah! what nonsense it was. Here she was, wide awake again, in her own familiar room, with the firelight on the walls.

… Well, well; sleep was a curious thing; and so was imagination.…

… At any rate she had written to Mr. Cathcart.

===Chinese===
于是，带着睡意渐深时那种不负责任的劲头，她转而想象起门那一头是什么光景——凭着一种直觉的视觉去揣度那宅子的脾性。她甚至弄明白了：那里头全和这间屋子一样空无家具；那宅子孤零零地立在树木之间；便连那些树木，连同她断定必在宅子四周的那座荒蔓的园子，也都像她自己、像那少年等候的身影一样，在倾听、在期待。她再一次——仿佛要印证自己这半被动的想象之旅——朝那扇门挪了过去。……

啊！真是胡说八道。瞧，她又完全醒了，躺在自家那间熟稔的屋子里，炉光照在墙上。

……好啦，好啦；睡眠这东西真古怪；想象也一样古怪。……

……无论如何，她到底是给卡特卡特先生写过了信。

## XI / XI

### I / I

===Original===
The Cock Inn is situated in Fleet Street, not twenty yards from Mitre Court and scarcely fifty from the passage that leads down to the court where Mr. James Morton still has his chambers.

It was a convenient place, therefore, for Laurie to lunch in, and he generally made his appearance there a few minutes before one o’clock to partake of a small rump steak and a pewter mug of beer. Sometimes he came alone, sometimes in company; and by a carefully thought out system of tips he usually managed to have reserved for him at least until one o’clock a particular seat in a particular partition in that row of stable-like shelters that run the length of the room opposite the door on the first floor.

On the twenty-third of February, however﻿—it was a Friday, by the way, and boiled plaice would have to be eaten instead of rump steak﻿—he was a little annoyed to find his seat already occupied by a small, brisk-looking man with a grey beard and spectacles, who, with a newspaper propped in front of him, was also engaged in the consumption of boiled plaice.

The little man looked up at him sharply, like a bird disturbed in a meal, and then down again upon the paper. Laurie noticed that his hat and stick were laid upon the adjoining chair as if to retain it. He hesitated an instant; then he slid in on the other side, opposite the stranger, tapped his glass with his knife, and sat down.

===Chinese===
公鸡客栈（the Cock Inn）坐落于舰队街（Fleet Street），离米特院（Mitre Court）不到二十码，离那条通往詹姆斯·莫顿（James Morton）先生至今仍设有事务所的院子的过道，也几乎不到五十码。

因此，这里正是劳里用午餐的一个便当去处；他通常在一点钟前几分钟到场，享用一小份牛腰肉排和一锡杯啤酒。他有时独自来，有时与人同来；凭着一套精心琢磨出来的赏钱规矩，他总能在二楼那排马厩似的隔间里——那排隔间沿进门对面的墙一字排开——给自己留住某个特定隔间里的某个特定座位，至少能留到一点钟。

然而到了二月二十三日这一天——顺带说一句，那天是星期五，只好吃煮鲽鱼，吃不上牛腰肉排——他发现自己的座位已被一个身材矮小、神情干练、留着灰胡子、戴着眼镜的男人占了，心里不免有点恼火；那人面前支着一张报纸，也正埋头吃着煮鲽鱼。

那小个子男人尖利地抬眼看他，像一只进餐时被打扰的鸟，接着又低头看回报纸。劳里留意到，他的帽子和手杖放在相邻的椅子上，仿佛是要占住那个位子。他迟疑了一瞬；随即从另一边挤了进去，与那陌生人面对面坐下，用刀敲了敲杯子。

===Original===
When the waiter came, a familiarly deferential man with whiskers, Laurie, with a slight look of peevishness, gave his order, and glanced reproachfully at the occupied seat. The waiter gave the ghost of a shrug with his shoulders, significant of apologetic helplessness, and went away.

A minute later Mr. Morton entered, glanced this way and that, nodding imperceptibly to Laurie, and was just moving off to a less occupied table when the stranger looked up.

“Mr. Morton,” he cried, “Mr. Morton!” in an odd voice that seemed on the point of cracking into falsetto. (Certainly he was very like a portly bird, thought Laurie.)

The other turned round, nodded with short geniality, and slid into the chair from which the old man moved his hat and stick with zealous haste.

“And what are you doing here?” said Mr. Morton.

“Just taking a bite like yourself,” said the other. “Friday﻿—worse luck.”

Laurie was conscious of a touch of interest. This man was a Catholic, then, he supposed.

===Chinese===
侍者过来时——一个长着络腮胡子、脸上带着熟络的恭敬的男人——劳里略带愠色地报了自己要的菜，又用责备的眼神瞟了一眼那个被占的座位。侍者做了一个几乎看不见的耸肩，意思是抱歉地爱莫能助，便走开了。

过了一分钟，莫顿先生走了进来，朝这边那边都扫了一眼，朝劳里几乎看不出来地点了点头，正要挪到一张没那么挤的桌上去，那陌生人却抬起了眼。

「莫顿先生，」他喊道，「莫顿先生！」声音古怪，仿佛随时会劈叉成假声。（他确实很像一只圆滚滚的鸟，劳里想。）

那一位转过身来，简短而和气地点了点头，便挤进了那张椅子——老人则热切而匆忙地把他的帽子和手杖挪开。

「你在这儿做什么？」莫顿先生说。

「跟你一样，随便吃一口，」那一位说。「星期五——真倒霉。」

劳里感到自己生出了一点兴趣。那么，这个人是个天主教徒了，他猜想。

===Original===
“Oh, by the way,” said Mr. Morton, “have you﻿—er﻿—” and he indicated Laurie. “No?﻿ ﻿… Baxter, let me introduce Mr. Cathcart.”

For a moment the name meant nothing to Laurie; then he remembered; but his rising suspicions were quelled instantly by his friend’s next remark.

“By the way, Cathcart, we were talking of you a week or two ago.”

“Indeed! I am flattered,” said the old man perkily. (Yes, “perky” was the word, thought Laurie.)

“Mr. Baxter here is interested in Spiritualism﻿—(rump steak, waiter, and pint of bitter)﻿—and I told him you were the man for him.”

Laurie interiorly drew in his horns.

===Chinese===
「哦，对了，」莫顿先生说，「你可——呃——」他朝劳里示意了一下。「不认识？……巴克斯特，我来介绍一下，这位是卡特卡特先生（Mr. Cathcart）。」

有一瞬间，这名字对劳里毫无意义；接着他想起来了；但他心头刚升起的疑虑，立刻被朋友接下来的一句话压了下去。

「对了，卡特卡特，一两个礼拜前我们还在谈你来着。」

「是么！我可真受宠若惊了，」老头儿精神抖擞地说。（没错，「perky」——神气活现——正是那个形容他的词儿，劳里想。）

「这位巴克斯特先生对通灵术（Spiritualism）挺感兴趣——（侍者，牛腰肉排，一品脱苦啤酒）——我便告诉他，您正是他该找的人。」

劳里暗自收敛了几分。

===Original===
“A﻿—er﻿—an experimenter?” asked the old man, with courteous interest, his eyes giving a quick gleam beneath his glasses.

“A little.”

“Yes. Most dangerous﻿—most dangerous.﻿ ﻿… And any success, Mr. Baxter?”

Laurie felt his annoyance deepen.

“Very considerable success,” he said shortly.

“Ah, yes﻿—you must forgive me, sir; but I have had a good deal of experience, and I must say﻿—You are a Catholic, I see,” he said, interrupting himself. “Or a High Churchman.”

“I am a Catholic,” said Laurie.

“So’m I. But I gave up Spiritualism as soon as I became one. Very interesting experiences, too; but﻿—well, I value my soul too much, Mr. Baxter.”

Mr. Morton put a large piece of potato into his mouth with a detached air.

It was really rather trying, thought Laurie, to be catechized in this way; so he determined to show superiority.

“And you think it all superstition and nonsense?” he asked.

===Chinese===
「一位——呃——一位实验者？」老头儿带着客气的兴致问道，眼睛在镜片底下飞快地一闪。

「略略涉猎一点。」

「是啊。极其危险——极其危险。……可曾有过什么成效么，巴克斯特先生？」

劳里觉得自己的恼火又深了一层。

「颇有相当大的成效，」他简短地说。

「啊，是——您得原谅我，先生；不过我在这方面有过相当多的阅历，我不得不说——我看您是天主教徒吧，」他打断了话头说道。「要么，是高教会派的人。」

「我是天主教徒，」劳里说。

「我也是。可我一成为天主教徒，就把通灵术丢了。那也是些很有意思的经历；可是——唉，我太把我的灵魂当回事了，巴克斯特先生。」

莫顿先生带着一种事不关己的神气，把一大块土豆送进嘴里。

这实在是相当难堪的，劳里想，竟这样被人盘问；于是他决意拿出一点居高临下的姿态。

「那么您认为这全都是迷信和无稽之谈了？」他问。

===Original===
“Indeed, no,” said the old man shortly.

Laurie pushed his plate on one side, and drew the cheese towards him. This was a little more interesting, he thought, but he was still far from feeling communicative.

“What then?” he asked.

“Oh, very real indeed,” said the old man. “That is just the danger.”

“The danger?”

“Yes, Mr. Baxter. Of course there’s plenty of fraud and trickery; we all know that. But it’s the part that’s not fraud that’s﻿—May I ask what medium you go to?”

“I know Mr. Vincent. And I’ve been to some public séances, too.”

The old man looked at him with sudden interest, but said nothing.

“You think he’s not honest?” said Laurie, with cool offensiveness.

“Oh, yes; he’s perfectly honest,” said the other deliberately. “I’ll trouble you for the sugar, Mr. Morton.”

===Chinese===
「可不是，」老头儿简短地说。

劳里把盘子推到一边，把奶酪拉到自己面前。这倒有点意思了，他想，可他仍远远说不上有什么倾谈的兴致。

「那是什么呢？」他问。

「哦，实在得很呢，」老头儿说，「危险也正在这里。」

「危险？」

「是的，巴克斯特先生。当然，那里头的欺诈和戏法多得是；这个我们都晓得。可正是那并非欺诈的那一部分——恕我冒昧，请问您常去的是哪位灵媒（medium）？」

「我认识文森特先生。我也去听过几次公开的降神会（séance）。」

老头儿忽然带了几分兴致看着他，却什么也没说。

「您是觉得他不老实？」劳里说，带着一种冷漠的冒犯。

「哦，不；他是再老实不过的，」那一位不慌不忙地说，「麻烦您把糖递给我，莫顿先生。」

===Original===
Laurie was determined not to begin the subject again. He felt that he was being patronized and lectured, and did not like it. And once again the suspicion crossed his mind that this was an arranged meeting. It was so very neat﻿—two days before the séance﻿—the entry of Morton﻿—his own seat occupied. Yet he did not feel quite courageous enough to challenge either of them. He ate his cheese deliberately and waited, listening to the talk between the two on quite irrelevant subjects, and presently determined on a bit of bravado.

“May I look at the *Daily Mirror*, Mr. Cathcart?” he asked.

“There is no doubt of his guilt,” the old man said, as he handed the paper across (the two were deep in a law case now). “I said so to Markham a dozen times﻿—” and so on.

But there was no more word of Spiritualism. Laurie propped the paper before him as he finished his cheese, and waited for coffee, and read with unseeing eyes. He was resenting as hard as he could the abruptness of the opening and closing of the subject, and the complete disregard now shown to him. He drank his coffee, still leisurely, and lit a cigarette; and still the two talked.

He stood up at last and reached down his hat and stick. The old man looked up.

“You are going, Mr. Baxter?﻿ ﻿… Good day.﻿ ﻿… Well then; and as I was waiting in court﻿—”

Laurie passed out indignantly, and went down the stairs.

So that was Mr. Cathcart﻿ ﻿… Well, he was thankful he hadn’t written to him, after all. He was not his kind in the least.

===Chinese===
劳里打定主意不再先提这个话题。他觉得自己正被人居高临下地说教，心里很不痛快。那个念头又一次掠过他心头：这是一次安排好的会面。这一切未免太巧了——降神会前两天——莫顿的进场——他自己的座位被人占了。可他又觉着自己没有足够的胆量去当面质问这两个人里的任何一个。他从容地吃着奶酪，等着，听这两个人谈着些毫不相干的事，不久便决意来一点虚张声势。

「我可以看看那份《每日镜报》么，卡特卡特先生？」他问。

「他的罪是无可置疑的了，」老头儿把报纸递过来时说道（这两人此刻正谈一桩案子谈得起劲）。「这话我跟马卡姆说过不下十几回——」如此等等。

可通灵术再没人提起一个字。劳里吃完奶酪，把报纸支在面前，等咖啡，两只眼睛却视而不见地看着。他正拼命地恼恨这个话题开得突兀、收得也突兀，以及此刻他俩对他全然的漠视。他仍是从容地喝着咖啡，点上一支烟；那两人仍在谈着。

他终于站起身来，伸手取下帽子和手杖。老头儿抬起头来。

「您要走啦，巴克斯特先生？……再见。……那么，我刚才说到我在法庭上等着的时候——」

劳里愤愤地走了出去，下了楼梯。

原来这就是卡特卡特先生……唉，他还得多谢自己到底没有写信给他呢。这个人完全不是他那一类人。

### II / II

===Original===
The moment he passed out of the door the old man stopped his fluent talking and waited, looking after the boy. Then he turned again to his friend.

“I’m a blundering idiot,” he said.

Mr. Morton sniffed.

“I’ve put him against me now﻿—Lord knows how; but I’ve done it; and he won’t listen to me.”

“Gad!” said Mr. Morton; “what funny people you all are! And you really meant what you said?”

“Every word,” said the old man cheerfully.﻿ ﻿… “Well; our little plot’s over.”

“Why don’t you ask him to come and see you?”

“First,” said the old man, with the same unruffled cheerfulness, “he wouldn’t have come. We’ve muddled it. We’d much better have been straightforward. Secondly, he thinks me an old fool﻿—as you do, only more so. No; we must set to work some other way now.﻿ ﻿… Tell me about Miss Deronnais: I showed you her letter?”

The other nodded, helping himself to cheese.

“I told her that I was at her service, of course; and I haven’t heard again. Sensible girl?”

“Very sensible, I should say.”

“Sort of girl that wouldn’t scream or faint in a crisis?”

“Exactly the opposite, I should say. But I’ve hardly seen her, you know.”

===Chinese===
他一走出门，老头儿便停下了他那滔滔不绝的话头，望着这少年的背影等了一会儿。然后他又回过头来对着他的朋友。

「我是个笨手笨脚的傻瓜，」他说。

莫顿先生吸了吸鼻子。

「我如今把他得罪了——天晓得是怎么得罪的；可我确实是得罪了；他再也不会听我的了。」

「咄！」莫顿先生说，「你们这帮人也真好笑！你方才那些话，可是当真的？」

「字字当真，」老头儿乐呵呵地说。……「好啦；咱们这点小计谋算是完了。」

「你怎么不请他上你那儿去坐坐呢？」

「第一，」老头儿带着同样不动声色的乐呵说，「他本来就不会来。我们把事情弄糟了。我们本该开门见山的。第二，他觉得我是个老傻瓜——正如你也这么觉得，只不过他觉得更甚。不行；如今我们只好另想法子了。……跟我说说德罗奈小姐（Miss Deronnais）的事吧：我把她的信给你看过了？」

那一位点了点头，动手给自己夹奶酪。

「我当然回复她说，我听候她的差遣；之后便没再有回音。是个明事理的姑娘？」

「我看是非常明事理的。」

「是那种到了紧要关头，不会尖叫、也不会晕倒的姑娘？」

「我看恰恰相反。不过你也知道，我几乎没怎么见过她。」

===Original===
“Well, well.﻿ ﻿… And the mother?”

“No good at all,” said Mr. Morton.

“Then the girl’s the sheet anchor.﻿ ﻿… In love with him, do you know?”

“Lord! How d’you expect me to know that?”

The old man pondered in silence, seeming to assimilate the situation.

“He’s in a devil of a mess,” he said, with abrupt cheerfulness. “That man Vincent﻿—”

“Well?”

“He’s the most dangerous of the lot. Just because he’s honest.”

“Good God!” broke in the other again suddenly. “Do all Catholics believe this rubbish?”

“My dear friend, of course they don’t. Not one in a thousand. I wish they did. That’s what’s the matter. But they laugh at it﻿—laugh at it!”﻿ ﻿… His voice cracked into shrill falsetto.﻿ ﻿… “Laugh at hellfire.﻿ ﻿… Is Sunday the day, did you say?”

“He told me the twenty-fifth.”

“And at that woman’s in Queen’s Gate, I suppose?”

“Expect so. He didn’t say. Or I forget.”

“I heard they were at their games there again,” said Mr. Cathcart with meditative geniality. “I’d like to blow up the stinking hole.”

Mr. Morton chuckled audibly.

“You’re the youngest man of your years I’ve ever come across,” he said. “No wonder you believe all that stuff. When are you going to grow up, Cathcart?”

The old man paid no attention at all.

“Well﻿—that plot’s over,” he said again. “Now for Miss Deronnais. But we can’t stop this Sunday affair; that’s certain. Did he tell you anything about it? Materialization? Automatic﻿—”

“Lord, I don’t know all that jargon.﻿ ﻿…”

“My dear Morton, for a lawyer, you’re the worst witness I’ve ever﻿—Well, I’m off. No more to be done today.”

===Chinese===
「好，好。……那么那位母亲呢？」

「一点用处也没有，」莫顿先生说。

「那么这姑娘便是主锚了。……她爱着他，这你可知道？」

「老天！这你让我怎么晓得？」

老头儿默默地思忖着，仿佛在消化这个局面。

「他这回可陷进了一个要命的烂摊子，」他忽然又乐呵呵地说，「那个文森特——」

「怎么了？」

「他是这伙人里最危险的一个。正因为他老实。」

「老天爷！」那一位又突然插嘴道，「所有的天主教徒都信这套鬼话么？」

「我亲爱的朋友，他们当然不。一千个人里也没有一个。我倒巴不得他们信呢。毛病正出在这里。可他们笑话它——笑话它！」……他的嗓音陡地劈成了尖锐的假声。……「连地狱之火也笑话。……你说是礼拜天？」

「他告诉我，是二十五号。」

「就在王后门（Queen’s Gate）那位太太家里，我猜？」

「估摸是。他没说。要不就是我忘了。」

「我听说他们又在那一带玩他们那套把戏了，」卡特卡特先生带着若有所思的和气说，「我真想把那臭窟窿炸上天。」

莫顿先生笑出声来。

「论你这把年纪，你是我见过的最年轻的人了，」他说，「怪不得你信那套玩意儿。你什么时候才能长大成人哪，卡特卡特？」

老头儿根本没理会。

「好吧——那场计谋算完了，」他又说了一遍。「如今就看德罗奈小姐的了。可这个礼拜天的事，我们是拦不住了；这是肯定的。他可跟你说过什么细节？显形（materialization）？自动书写——」

「老天，我哪儿懂得那一套行话。……」

「我亲爱的莫顿，做一个律师，你可真是我见过的最糟糕的证人——好啦，我走了。今儿能做的就这些了。」

---

===Original===
The other sat on a few minutes over his pipe.

It seemed to him quite amazing that a sensible man like Cathcart could take such rubbish seriously. In every other department of life the solicitor was an eminently shrewd and sane man, with, moreover, a youthful kind of brisk humor that is perhaps the surest symptom of sanity that it is possible to have.

He had seen him in court for years past under every sort of circumstance, and if it had been required of him to select a character with which superstition and morbid humbug could have had nothing in common, he would have laid his hand upon the senior partner of Cathcart and Cathcart. Yet here was this sane man, taking this fantastic nonsense as if there were really something in it. He had first heard him speak of the subject at a small bachelor dinner party of four in the rooms of a mutual friend; and, as he had listened, he had had the same sensation as one would have upon hearing a Cabinet minister, let us say, discussing stump-cricket with enthusiasm. Cathcart had said all kinds of things when once he was started﻿—all with that air of businesslike briskness that was so characteristic of him and so disconcerting in such a connection. If he had apologized for it as an amiable weakness, if he had been in the least shamefaced or deprecatory, it would have been another matter; one would have forgiven it as one forgives any little exceptional eccentricity. But to hear him speak of materialization as of a process as normal (though unusual) as the production of radium, and of planchette as of wireless telegraphy﻿—as established, indubitable facts, though out of the range of common experience﻿—this had amazed this very practical man. Cathcart had hinted too of other things﻿—things which he would not amplify﻿—of a still more disconcertingly impossible nature﻿—matters which Morton had scarcely thought had been credible even to the darkest medievalists; and all this with that same sharp, sane humor that lent an air of reality to all that he said.

For romantic young asses like Laurie Baxter such things were not so hopelessly incongruous, though obviously they were bad for him; they were all part of the wild credulousness of a religious youth; but for Cathcart, aged sixty-two, a solicitor in good practice, with a wife and two grownup daughters, and a reputation for exceptionally sound shrewdness﻿—! But it must be remembered he was a Catholic!

So Mr. James Morton sat in the Cock and pondered. He was not sorry he had tried to take steps to choke off this young fool, and he was just a little sorry that so far they had failed. He had written to Miss Deronnais in an impulse, after an unusually feverish outburst from the boy; and she, he had learnt later, had written to Mr. Cathcart. The rest had been of the other’s devising.

Well, it had failed so far. Perhaps next week things would be better.

He paid his bill, left two pence for the waiter, and went out. He had a case that afternoon.

===Chinese===
那一位衔着烟斗又坐了几分钟。

一个像卡特卡特这样明白事理的人，竟会把这般荒唐的话当真，在他看来实在不可思议。在生活的其他一切方面，这位律师都是个极其精明、极其清醒的人，此外还带着一种年轻人式的干练幽默，而这幽默，也许正是一个人神志清明最可靠的征兆。

多年来，他曾在法庭上各种场合下见过他，倘若要他挑出一个与迷信和病态的鬼话绝无半点瓜葛的人物，他一定会把手按在「卡特卡特与卡特卡特」律师事务所的那位资深合伙人身上。然而，眼前就是这位神志清明的人，竟把这种荒诞不经的昏话当作真有那么回事似的听着。他头一回听他谈起这话题，是在一位共同朋友寓所里举行的一次四个人的小型单身汉晚宴上；他听着的时候，那种感觉，就如同听见一位内阁大臣，比方说，正眉飞色舞地大谈残柱板球（stump-cricket）。卡特卡特一旦起了头，便什么话都说得出——说的当儿，始终带着那种公事般的干练劲儿，这是他极鲜明的特点，放在这样一个话题上又格外叫人无所适从。倘若他把这当作一个无伤大雅的弱点来致歉，倘若他有一丝半点的难为情或自谦，那便另当别论了；人自然会像原谅任何一点少见的怪癖那样原谅他。可如今，听他谈显形，仿佛那是与镭的提炼同样正常（虽则罕见）的一道过程；听他谈普朗歇特（planchette），仿佛那是无线电报——是既定而无可置疑的事实，只不过超乎寻常经验之外——这一切，着实叫这位极其讲求实际的人吃惊。卡特卡特还含蓄地提起过别的事——那些他不肯深说的事——性质上还要更加骇人地不可能——那些莫顿原以为连最黑暗的中世纪人都不大会相信的事；而这一切，他仍带着那种尖利、清醒的幽默说出来，正是这种幽默，给他所说的一切都披上了一层真实的外衣。

至于像劳里·巴克斯特这种浪漫的傻小子，这类事倒不算那么格格不入，虽说对他显然是有害的；这无非是一个有宗教热忱的少年那种毫无节制的轻信里的一部分；可换成卡特卡特——六十二岁、事业兴旺的一位律师，有妻有两位成年的女儿，且一向以格外稳健精明著称——！不过，也得记住，他是个天主教徒！

詹姆斯·莫顿先生就这样坐在公鸡客栈里沉思。他对自己曾设法打消这傻小子的念头并不后悔，只是对他们至今未能奏效略感惋惜。他是在那少年一次异常狂热的发作之后，一时冲动之下写信给德罗奈小姐的；后来他得知，她又写了信给卡特卡特先生。余下的一切，便都是那一位筹谋的了。

唉，至今是失败了。也许下周事情会好些。

他付了账，给侍者留下两个便士，走了出去。那天下午他还有一桩案子。

### III / III

===Original===
Laurie left chambers as it was growing dark that afternoon, and went back to his rooms for tea. He had passed, as was usual now, an extremely distracted couple of hours, sitting over his books with spasmodic efforts only to attend to them. He was beginning, in fact, to be not quite sure whether law after all was his vocation.﻿

His kettle was singing pleasantly on the hob, and a tray glimmered in the firelight on the little table, as the woman had left it; and it was not until he had poured himself out a cup of tea that he saw on the white cloth an envelope, directed to him, inscribed “By hand,” in the usual handwriting of persons engaged in business. Even then he did not open it at once; it was probably only some note connected with his chief’s affairs.

For half an hour more he sat on, smoking after tea, pondering that which was always in his mind now, and dwelling with a vague pleasant expectancy on what Sunday night should bring forth. Mr. Vincent, he knew, was returning to town that afternoon. Perhaps, even, he might look in for a few minutes, if there were any last instructions to be given.

===Chinese===
那天下午天色渐暗时，劳里离开事务所，回自己的寓所去喝茶。他像如今惯常的那样，度过了极其心不在焉的两个钟头，坐在书前，只是断断续续地强打精神去读。事实上，他已经开始有点拿不准，法律到底是不是他的天职了。……

他的水壶在炉灶上轻快地唱着歌，一张托盘在炉光里、在那张小桌上微微发亮，是那女佣留下的样子；直到他给自己倒了一杯茶，他才看见白台布上有一只写明给他的信封，上书「面交」二字，是干办事的人惯用的那种笔迹。即便那时，他也没有立刻拆开；多半不过是与他上司的事务有关的一张便条。

他又坐了半个钟头，喝完茶吸着烟，心里转着如今始终盘踞在他脑中的那些事，怀着一种模糊而惬意的期待，想象星期天晚上会带来些什么。他知道，文森特先生那天下午要回城。说不定，倘若还有什么最后的吩咐，他甚至会顺道过来坐几分钟。

===Original===
The effect of the medium on the young man’s mind had increased enormously during these past weeks. That air of virile masterfulness, all the more impressive because of its extreme quiet assurance, had proved even more deep than had at first appeared.

It is very hard to analyze the elements of a boy’s adoration for a solid middle-aged gentleman with a “personality”; yet the thing is an enormously potent fact, and plays at least as big a part in the sub-currents that run about the world as any more normal human emotions. Psychologists of the materialistic school would probably say that it was a survival of the tribe-and-war instinct. At any rate, there it is.

Added to all this was the peculiar relation in which the medium stood to the boy; it was he who had first opened the door towards that strange other world that so persistently haunts the imaginations of certain temperaments; it was through him that Laurie had had brought before the evidence of his senses, as he thought, the actuality of the things of which he had dreamed﻿—an actuality which his religion had somehow succeeded in evading. It was not that Laurie had been insincere in his religion; there had been moments, and there still were, occasionally, when the world that the Catholic religion preached by word and symbol and sacrament, became apparent; but the whole thing was upon a different plane. Religion bade him approach in one way, Spiritualism in the other. The senses had nothing to do with one; they were the only ultimate channels of the other.
